Summations of large logarithms by parton showers Preprint: DESY 20-182
Zoltán Nagy, Davison E. Soper

Abstract
We propose a method to examine how a parton shower sums large logarithms. In this method, one works with an appropriate integral transform of the distribution for the observable of interest. Then, one reformulates the parton shower so as to obtain the transformed distribution as an exponential for which one can compute the terms in the perturbative expansion of the exponent. We apply this general program to the thrust distribution in electron-positron annihilation, using several shower algorithms. Of the approaches that we use, the most generally applicable is to compute some of the perturbative coefficients in the exponent by numerical integration and to test whether they are consistent with next-to-leading-log summation of the thrust logarithms.



I
Introduction

Parton shower event generators provide a way to approximately sum large logarithms in QCD. Consider an infrared safe observable labelled by
J
in hadron-hadron, lepton-hadron, or lepton-lepton collisions at a large energy scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}
. Suppose that one is interested in a cross section
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
for the observable to take the value
\bm{v}
. The observable is characterized by a scale
\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})
, such that the
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
is not sensitive to parton splittings at a scale smaller than
\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})
. For instance, one might be interested in the
\bm{k}_{\perp}
distribution in the Drell-Yan process in hadron-hadron collisions. Then
\bm{v}=\bm{k}_{\perp}
and
\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})\sim\bm{k}_{\perp}^{2}
. If
\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})\sim\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
, one can use straightforward QCD perturbation theory to calculate
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
. However, if
\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})\ll\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
, the perturbative expansion for
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
will contain large logarithms,
\log(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}/\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v}))
.

Often, one can analyze these logarithms by taking an appropriate integral transform of
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
. Then one calculates a cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
depending on a variable or variables
\bm{r}
. The cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
contains logarithms
L(\bm{r})
that are large when
\bm{r}
approaches a limit. For instance, one might take the Fourier transform, with transverse position
\bm{b}
, of the Drell-Yan
\bm{k}_{\perp}
distribution. In this example,
\bm{r}
stands for
\bm{b}
, the limit is
\bm{b}^{2}\to\infty
, and the logarithm is
L=\log(\bm{b}^{2}\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
. Typically the cross section then has the form

\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=c_{0}\Bigg\{1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{2n}c(n,j)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{j}(\bm{r})\Bigg\}\,.

(1)

The logarithms
L^{j}(\bm{r})
arise in QCD from the soft and collinear singularities of the theory. These same soft and collinear singularities are contained in the splitting functions of a parton shower algorithm. Thus running a parton shower event generator to calculate
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
will produce an approximation to the series in Eq. (
1
). That is, the parton shower approximately sums the large logarithms. The object of this paper is to investigate the form of the result of this summation.
1
1

1

The analysis applies not just when
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
represents an integral transform of some other distribution, but also whenever the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
that we use to measure
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
after the shower has an inverse. That is,
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
must have no eigenvalues equal to zero.

To exhibit the summation of logarithms, we rearrange the parton shower algorithm so that it is specialized to calculate just
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
and so that it expresses
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
directly in terms of an exponential

\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\right)\;,

(2)

where
\mathbb{T}
indicates ordering in
\mu^{2}
.
The integral of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
in the exponent has an expansion

\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\sum_{j=0}^{2n}e(n,j)\,L^{j}(\bm{r})\;.

(3)

The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
is determined by the parton splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
in the original shower. This gives one direct access to the coefficients
e(n,j)
. With this representation, one has the potential to prove that
e(n,j)=0
for
j>n+1
. The terms with
j=n+1
are called leading-log (LL) terms and the terms with
j=n
are called next-to-leading-log (NLL) terms. One also has the potential to prove that
e(n,j)
for
j=n+1
and for
j=n
are what is expected in full QCD if a full QCD result is known.

There is a long history of investigations of how well parton shower event generators reproduce the expectation from full QCD for the large logarithm expansion of Eq. (
3
). We can provide some examples. For instance, Marchesini and Webber argued that a parton shower event generator based on angular ordering would better sum large logarithms than alternative formulations
MarchesiniWebber1984
. Ref.
IngelmanSoper1984
found that the event generator
MarchesiniWebber1984
of Marchesini and Webber, a precursor to
Herwig

Herwig1992
;
Herwig
, agreed with the analytic QCD summation of double logarithms
ChaoCollinsSoper1983
for the energy-energy correlation function in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation better than an alternative event generator due to Gottschalk
Gottschalk1983
. In 1991, Catani, Webber, and Marchesini compared QCD theory and the structure of parton shower algorithms for several observables that involve large logarithms and concluded that a small adjustment in the parton shower algorithm could improve the summation of the large logarithms
CMW
. The connection between parton showers and large nonglobal logarithms was investigated in
Banfi2007
. One paper
DGLAPnogo
argued that a dipole parton shower might not properly sum the logarithms that are encoded in the Dokshitzer-Gribov-Lipatov-Altarelli-Parisi (DGLAP) evolution equation for the distribution of hadrons in a final state parton. However, two investigations
NSdglap
;
SkandsWeinzierl
showed that this argument was not correct. A similar investigation showed analytically that a virtuality ordered dipole shower correctly sums the double logs that appear in the Drell-Yan transverse momentum distribution
NSZpT
. Some issues in large logarithm summations in dipole parton showers were investigated in
Hoeche2018
. The logarithmic accuracy of final state parton showers was investigated at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
in
Dasgupta2018
and
angular2020
. A more powerful analysis for
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation was undertaken in
DasguptaShowerSum
. A very recent paper
HamiltonShowerSum
addresses corrections to the leading color approximation in a dipole parton shower in order to maintain proper color factors for leading logarithm terms.

Our plan for sections
II
through
X
of this paper is to develop the general theory behind the representation (
2
) along the lines of Ref.
NSAllOrder
. In this exposition, we also present the main steps of the construction of Ref.
NSAllOrder
in a form that, in our opinion, makes these steps more transparent. Then, starting in section
XI
, we apply the representation (
2
) to an important example, the thrust distribution in electron-positron annihilation. We consider just the thrust distribution and not other distributions involving large logarithms. However, we look in some detail at how the exact form of the shower algorithm affects the results.

Since the construction presented in this paper is simpler for
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation than for hadron-hadron or electron-hadron collisions and since Secs.
XI
through
XXVIII
are quite self-contained, some readers may prefer to jump to the later sections before reading the more general analysis in the earlier sections.

II
Parton shower from perturbation theory

The starting point for the general analysis is the perturbative cross section for an infrared safe observable in hadron-hadron collisions. This starting point also applies with some simplifications also to electron-hadron and electron-positron collisions. We describe this structure briefly here. A more detailed explanation can be found in Ref.
NSAllOrder
.

The parton shower is described as in our parton shower event generator
Deductor

NSI
;
NSII
;
NSspin
;
NScolor
;
Deductor
;
ShowerTime
;
NSThreshold
;
NSThresholdII
using operators on a vector space, the “statistical space,” that describes the momenta, flavors, colors, and spins for all of the partons created in a shower as the shower develops. The colors and spins are quantum variables and are described using a density matrix. With
m
final state partons plus two initial state partons with labels “a” and “b,” the partons carry labels
\mathrm{a},\mathrm{b},1,2,\dots,m
. The partons have momenta
\{p\}_{m}=\{p_{\mathrm{a}},p_{\mathrm{b}},p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\}
and flavors
\{f\}_{m}
. We take the partons to be massless:
p_{i}^{2}=0
. For color, there are ket color basis states
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
and bra color basis states
\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. We use the trace basis, as described in Ref.
NSI
. For spin, there are ket basis states
|{\{s\}_{m}}\rangle
and bra basis states
\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. Then the
m
-parton basis states for the statistical space are denoted by
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. A vector
|{\rho})
in the statistical space is a linear combination of the basis states. The statistical space is introduced in some detail in Secs. 2 and 3 of Ref.
NSI
. These sections also show how shower evolution is expressed using evolution operators that act on the statistical space. (However, in the present paper the names of the operators follow Ref.
NSAllOrder
rather than Ref.
NSI
.) The spin basis is described in Sec. 5 of Ref.
NSI
and the color basis is described in Sec. 7 of Ref.
NSI
.

In sections
II
through
X
of this paper, we maintain a general framework with full color and spin. Practical parton shower programs
Herwig
;
Pythia
;
Sherpa
typically average over spins, so that no spin quantum numbers appear in the shower equations. One can then carry out the analysis of the summation of large logarithms using the spin averaged shower, as we do starting in Sec.
XI
. For color, parton shower programs often use the leading color (LC) approximation, which provides the leading term in an expansion in powers of
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=1/9
. With this approach, the color states
\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}
do not affect the splitting probabilities, which are simply proportional to a factor
C_{\mathrm{F}}
or
C_{\mathrm{A}}
. Our program,
Deductor
, uses what is called the LC+ approximation
NScolor
. Thus we mostly use full color in this paper with the understanding that one could approximate to the LC+ or LC level if desired. We discuss this further and relate the discussion to Ref.
HamiltonShowerSum
in Sec.
XXV
.

II.1
Perturbative cross section

If the QCD matrix element is calculated up to a given order,
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K}
, the cross section is

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{F}_{0}\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})}\big)\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\Lambda^{2}_{\rm QCD}/Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})\right)\;.\end{split}

(4)

Here the renormalized perturbative QCD density operator is represented by a vector in the statistical space
|\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}))
. It is based on the exact matrix elements and contains all the possible partonic final states at order
K
. The density operator is already renormalized, typically in the modified minimal subtraction (
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
) scheme, thus it is independent of the renormalization scale,
\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}
, up to the
desired order

\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}}\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})}\big)=\mathcal{O}\big(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1}\big)\;.

(5)

The next factor in Eq. (
4
) is the operator of the bare parton distribution functions (PDFs),

\mathcal{F}_{0}=\big[\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\circ\mathcal{K}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\circ\mathcal{Z}_{F}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\big]\;.

(6)

Here the circles,
a\circ b
, represent convolutions in the momentum fraction variables. The renormalized PDF operator for the hadron-hadron initial state is
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})
. The corresponding
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
subtraction of initial state singularities is done by the
\mathcal{Z}_{F}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})
operator, which contains factors
1/\epsilon^{n}
in dimensional regularization. As described in Ref.
NSAllOrder
, one should typically use something other than the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
scheme to define the parton distribution functions used internally in the shower. The factor
\mathcal{K}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})
transforms to the shower scheme for the parton distribution functions
\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})
. The bare PDF is scale independent,

\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}\frac{d}{d\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}}\big[\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\circ\mathcal{K}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\circ\mathcal{Z}_{F}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1}\big)\;.

(7)

This equation leads to the proper evolution equation of the renormalized PDFs.

The next factor in Eq. (
4
) is the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
representing an infrared (IR) safe measurement, characterized by a set of parameters
\bm{r}
.

After applying these operators, we have a sum and integral over basis states
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. Finally, we multiply by the statistical bra vector
({1}|
and obtain a cross section after performing the integrations using

\big({1}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{s\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

(8)

(The spin states
|{\{s\}_{m}}\rangle
are orthogonal and normalized, but the color states
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
in the trace basis that we use are not orthogonal and some of them are not normalized exactly to 1
NSI
. The statistical bra vector
({1}|
is defined in Sec. 3.5 of Ref.
NSI
.)

If the calculation includes perturbative contributions up to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K}
, then there is an error term
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1})
in Eq. (
4
). The formula is based on standard QCD factorization for infrared safe observables. This has power suppressed corrections of order
[\Lambda_{\mathrm{QCD}}^{2}/Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})]^{p}
where
Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})
is the lowest scale that the measurement operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
can resolve and
p>0
. In the rest of this paper, we mostly omit explicit mention of these error terms.

The expression in Eq. (
4
) simplifies substantially in electron-positron annihilation. In this case, we can replace the operator
\mathcal{F}_{0}
by 1.

We point out that Eq. (
4
) is valid only in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. It is not directly useful for practical calculations.

II.2
IR singular operator

To define a good subtraction scheme for a fixed order calculation one can use the IR singular operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})

NSAllOrder
. This operator has a perturbative expansion

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})={}&1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(9)

The operators
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
are key to defining a parton shower algorithm in a general framework. For a first order shower, one uses only
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
, but in a general framework we consider
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
for any
n
.
This operator describes the IR singularity structure of partonic states
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})})
. When
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
acts on a state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
it produces new states
\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)
with
m\leq\hat{m}\leq m+n
such that the IR singularities of

\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|{}\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

match the singularities of
n
th order QCD Feynman diagrams that connect these two states. Here the singularities include the factors
1/\epsilon
from virtual loop diagrams and they include the singular behavior of the diagrams when any two or more momenta
\hat{p}
become collinear or some of the
\hat{p}
become soft. A toy model with operators
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
beyond
n=1
is presented in Appendix A of Ref.
NSAllOrder
.

The operator
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
depends on two scales, the standard renormalization scale
\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}
and the shower scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
. The shower scale acts as an ultraviolet (UV) cutoff that separates the IR and UV regions associated with
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
. All IR singularities are included, but only regions near these singularities with a scale, specified by a parameter
k^{2}
, satisfying
k^{2}<\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
are included. There is, of course, some freedom in choosing how the UV cutoff is defined. Different prescriptions lead to differences in the shower ordering prescription in the parton shower algorithm produced by
\mathcal{D}^{(n)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
.

The singular operator is based on the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
renormalized matrix elements and is independent of the renormalization scale. Thus we have

\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})=\mathcal{O}\big(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1}\big)\;.

(10)

This allows us to choose the renormalization scale conveniently.

In order to avoid large logarithms of
\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}/\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
, it is useful to relate the renormalization scale to the shower scale. We define

\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}=\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}\;.

(11)

Then we can avoid large
\log(\kappa_{\textsc{r}})
factors by choosing
\kappa_{\textsc{r}}
of order 1.

The singular operator is perturbative and we can always define its perturbative inverse operator,

\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})=1\;,

(12)

by working order by order in the perturbative expansion of Eq. (
12
).

II.3
Fixed order cross section

We can make Eq. (
4
) more useful by inserting 1 in the form
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{D}^{-1}
,

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|{}&\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{F}_{0}\\
&\times\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\,\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(13)

We notice that the expression
\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})})
is well defined in
d=4
dimensions since the inverse of the singular operator removes all the IR singularities of
|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})})
. Accordingly, we define the subtracted hard matrix element by

\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})}\big)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{D}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\big|{\rho(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})}\big)\;.

(14)

This gives us

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|{}&\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\,\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(15)

We will use Eq. (
15
) to explore parton showers. First, however, suppose that we are interested only in the fixed order cross section. Then we can choose the scale
\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
small enough that the measurement operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
does not resolve parton momentum scales of order
\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2}
. Then
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
commutes with
\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
, giving us

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|{}&\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(16)

One can calculate
({1}|\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions. The operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
creates singularities, but the initial state singularities are removed by the operator
\mathcal{Z}_{F}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})
in
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and the final state singularities cancel after we multiply by
({1}|
and integrate over the parton variables. Thus we obtain a finite result in the
\epsilon\to 0
limit.

II.4
Operators
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}

The operators
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
are defined only in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions and are singular as
\epsilon\to 0
and as parton momenta become soft or collinear. However, we have noted that
({1}|\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
is finite in
d=4
dimensions. It will prove useful to introduce an operator,
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
, that is finite in four dimensions, does not change the number of partons, leaves the parton momenta and flavors
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged, and satisfies

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\big({1}\big|{}&\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(17)

The operator
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
leaves
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged, but it can act non-trivially on the color and spin space. Eq. (
17
) does not fully define the color and spin content of
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
. We discuss the definition further in Sec.
IV
, but for now, we need only Eq. (
17
).

Using
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
we define a singular operator
\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
as

\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})=\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\ \mathcal{V}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\;,

(18)

so that

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})=\big({1}\big|\;.

(19)

The “1” subscript distinguishes the operator
\mathcal{X}_{1}
from the operator
\mathcal{X}
used in Ref.
NSAllOrder
and suggests the normalization condition (
19
).

With
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}
, the cross section in Eq. (
15
) can be written as

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|{}&\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\\
&\times\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(20)

This form will be useful to help us define a parton shower.

Before we continue with the discussion of the parton shower cross section we introduce a more compact notation for operators with renormalization scale dependence. According to Eq. (
11
) the renormalization scale
is always related to the shower scale; thus we can define

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\mu^{2})\equiv{}&\mathcal{D}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2},\mu^{2})\;,\\
\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\equiv{}&\mathcal{X}_{1}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2},\mu^{2})\;,\\
\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})\equiv{}&\mathcal{V}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2},\mu^{2})\;,\\
\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu^{2})}\big)\equiv{}&\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2},\mu^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(21)

The PDF operator depends only on the renormalization scale and in this case the convention is a little different,

\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{F}_{\textsc{r}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})\;.

(22)

The functions specified above then depend on
\kappa_{\textsc{r}}
, but we do not display this dependence. With this more compact notation, Eq. (
20
) is written as

\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu^{2})}\big)\;.

(23)

II.5
Operator
\mathcal{U}
and parton shower

The formula for the cross section
\sigma_{J}
given in Eq. (
23
) is of limited usefulness if the scale
Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})
, representing the lowest scale that the measurement operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
can resolve, is much smaller than the scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
of the hardest momentum transfer in
\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu^{2})}\big)
. When that happens,
\sigma_{J}
will contain logarithms
\log(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}/Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r}))
that need to be summed by looking for the most important terms at all orders of perturbation theory. To that end, one can use a parton shower algorithm.

To provide a parton shower, first set the scale
\mu^{2}
in Eq. (
23
) to
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
. Then define a scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
that is certainly smaller than
Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})
. Typically, one chooses
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
on the order of
1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{2}
. Finally, insert
1=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}\mu_{\rm f}^{2})
into Eq. (
23
), giving

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\\
&\times\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(24)

Since
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}<Q_{J}^{2}(\bm{r})
, the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
does not resolve partons at the scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
. Thus
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
commutes with
\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})
, giving us

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\\
&\times\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(25)

With the use of Eq. (
19
), this is

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\\
&\times\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(26)

The operator
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
is of special importance. We give it the name

\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\;.

(27)

This is the shower operator. It generates a parton shower starting at the scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
and ending at the scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
. Because of Eq. (
19
), the shower operator is probability preserving

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})=\big({1}\big|\;.

(28)

Using the notation
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, the cross section is

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(29)

We have perturbatively calculated matrix elements with their IR divergences subtracted in
\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)
. Then the operator
\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
supplies parton distribution functions. The factor
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
serves to sum threshold logarithms
NSAllOrder
;
NSThresholdII
. An approximation to this factor is contained in
Deductor
although it is lacking in other current parton shower event generators. Next, the operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
generates the parton shower and the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
measures the desired observable in the multiparton state created by the shower. Finally, we multiply by
({1}|
and integrate to get the desired cross section. We discuss
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
and
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
in more detail in Secs.
V
and
VI
.

III
Observable dependent shower evolution

The operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
in Eq. (
29
) could represent any infrared safe observable. In this paper, we have a particular sort of operator in mind. Consider, for example, the transverse momentum distribution of a Z boson produced in the Drell-Yan process. The operator that measures the transverse momentum
\bm{k}_{\perp}
of the Z boson is defined as

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{Z}}&(\bm{k}_{\perp})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&(2\pi)^{2}\delta^{(2)}\big(\bm{k}_{\perp}-\bm{k}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\{p\}_{m})\big)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(30)

where
\bm{k}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\{p\}_{m})
is the transverse momentum of the observed Z boson. The standard method for summing logarithms of
\bm{k}_{\perp}^{2}/M_{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}
is to start with the Fourier transform of the
\bm{k}_{\perp}
distribution. To measure this with a parton shower event generator, we can use the measurement operator

\begin{split}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{b}){}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\int\frac{d\bm{k}_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\bm{b}\cdot\bm{k}_{\perp}}\,(2\pi)^{2}\delta^{(2)}\big(\bm{k}_{\perp}-\bm{k}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\{p\}_{m})\big)\\
&\qquad\times\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&e^{{\mathrm{i}}\bm{b}\cdot\bm{k}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\{p\}_{m})}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(31)

We let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{b})
serve as an example of the observable
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
that we consider in this paper. There are many other similar examples. We will need one property of the observable
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
beyond infrared safety: we assume that the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
has an inverse
\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})
.

To analyze the cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
, we start with the representation (
23
) with
\mu^{2}=\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
,

\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.

(32)

Define an operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
that is finite in
d=4
dimensions, leaves the number of partons and their momenta and flavors unchanged, and is related to
\mathcal{X}_{1}
by

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.

(33)

Then define a new version of
\mathcal{X}_{1}
that depends on the measurement parameters
\bm{r}
by

\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\mathcal{Y}^{-1}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(34)

This gives us

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2},\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|

(35)

and

\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2},\bm{r})\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\;.

(36)

Then our cross section is

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2},\bm{r})\\
&\times\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(37)

With the use of Eq. (
35
), and commuting
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
past
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
and
\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, which do not change the partonic state, this becomes

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(38)

Here we measure
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
at the hard state
\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)
, obtaining typically a very simple result. Then we measure
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
inside the operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})
. This operator has the potential to sum large logarithms.

We can also relate
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
to the shower operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
with a small final scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
. From Eq. (
33
), we have

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\;.

(39)

Insert
1=\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})
and use
\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
from Eq. (
27
):

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\;.

(40)

Since
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}<Q_{J}^{2}(\bm{r})
, the operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
does not resolve partons at the scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
. Thus
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
commutes with
\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})
, giving us

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\;.

(41)

Recall from Eq. (
19
) that
({1}|\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})=({1}|
. This gives us

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\;.

(42)

That is, we compare two calculations. In the first calculation, we generate a parton shower down to a very small scale starting with any statistical state at a scale
\mu^{2}
. Then we measure
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
inclusively using
({1}|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
. In the second calculation, we first operate with
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
on the state at scale
\mu^{2}
then measure
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
inclusively using
({1}|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
. These two calculations give the same result.

IV
The operator mapping
\mathbb{P}

In Sec.
II.4
we defined an operator
\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})
which is to obey
Eq. (
17
),
\big({1}\big|\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mu^{2})
. In Sec.
III
, we defined an operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
in the same way. In each case, we start with a singular operator
\mathcal{A}
and we want to define a second, nonsingular, operator
\mathcal{B}
with the property

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{B}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}\;.

(43)

When the operator
\mathcal{B}
acts on an
m
-parton basis state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
, it is to leave the number of partons, their momenta, and their flavors unchanged. It may, however, act non-trivially on the colors and spins.

These requirements do not fully specify
\mathcal{B}
. We can be somewhat more definite by requiring that there be a linear mapping
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, which we write in the form

\mathcal{B}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(44)

This mapping must satisfy

\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}

(45)

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
must leave
m
and
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged,

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}A(\{p,f\}_{m})_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}^{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\\
&\times\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(46)

The requirement (
43
) is then a restriction on the spin and color matrix
A
,

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\sum_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big\langle{\{\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\hat{c}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\big\langle{\{\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{\hat{s}\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\times A(\{p,f\}_{m})_{\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime},\hat{s},\hat{s}^{\prime}\}_{m}}^{\{c,c^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}\}_{m}}\;.\end{split}

(47)

We can place another requirement on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
: if
\mathcal{A}
has the property that it leaves
m
and
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged, then

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}\;.

(48)

One consequence of this is that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
.

These requirements do not fully specify the mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
. For now we do not need to be more specific. However in Sec.
XVI.3
we provide an example (without spin) that is useful for the analysis of a first order shower.

We will find that the combination
\mathcal{A}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
appears frequently in formulas. It useful to define an operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(49)

V
Generator of shower

We now turn to a more detailed study of the operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
that creates a parton shower between a hard scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
and a small, cutoff scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
. The generator of this shower evolution is the operator

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\left[\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu^{2})\right]_{\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}=\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2}}.

(50)

Here, we differentiate with respect to the shower scale. Because of Eq. (
10
) (with the use of Eqs. (
17
) and (
18
)), this is the same as

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{X}_{1}^{-1}(\mu^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu^{2})\;.

(51)

Because of Eq. (
19
),

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=0\;.

(52)

Eq. (
51
) gives us a differential equation for
\mathcal{U}

\mu^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2})=\mathcal{U}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2})\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\;.

(53)

We use the notation

\mathcal{U}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2}_{1})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu^{2}_{2}}^{\mu^{2}_{1}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\right)

(54)

to represent the solution of this equation. Here
\mathbb{T}
indicates the instruction to order the operators
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
with the smallest
\mu^{2}
to the left.

VI
The threshold factor

In Sec.
II.4
we have defined an operator
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
. With our notation in Eq. (
21
) for the scale dependence of
\mathcal{V}
, the crucial property given in Eq. (
17
) can be written

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{V}(\mu)=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{D}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mu)\;.

(55)

In Eq. (
29
) or Eq. (
38
), the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
contains large logarithms
NSThreshold
;
NSAllOrder
;
NSThresholdII
. These are the much studied threshold logarithms
Sterman1987
. We sum the threshold logarithms by writing
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
as an exponential. Define

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2}_{1})=\mathcal{V}^{-1}(\mu^{2}_{2})\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2}_{1})\;.

(56)

Then
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
can be written as

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})=\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\,\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(57)

Define a generator operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})
by

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{V}^{-1}(\mu^{2})\,\frac{d\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})}{d\mu^{2}}\;.

(58)

Then
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2}_{1})
is the solution of the differential equation

\mu^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2})=\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2}_{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})\;.

(59)

We write the solution of this equation as

\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})\right)\;.

(60)

As long as we expand the running coupling
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in Eq. (
60
) to some finite order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, the integral in Eq. (
60
) is convergent in the limit
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}\to 0

NSAllOrder
. Thus
\mathcal{V}(\mu^{2})
at small scales is almost the unit operator,

\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\approx 1\;.

(61)

That is

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\approx\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\;.\end{split}

(62)

VII
Perturbative expansions

The operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
can be expanded in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2})=\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})
:

\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\;.

(63)

In the general theory from Ref.
NSAllOrder
,
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
is constructed from the singular operator
\mathcal{D}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
. If we use only the first order part
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{s}}^{2})
of
\mathcal{D}
because that is all we know, then all we get is
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})
. However, in a practical parton shower program (such as the
\Lambda
-ordered
Deductor
), one often takes a guess at approximate higher order contributions
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}
. The approximate form is obtained by changing the argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in the splitting functions to
\kappa_{\textsc{r}}k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
and, additionally, making a special choice for
\kappa_{\textsc{r}}
. Expanding
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2})
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})
then produces contributions
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})
for
n>1
.

In
Deductor
, the first order contribution has three parts
NSAllOrder
;
NSThreshold
:

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}+{\mathrm{i}}\pi\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}_{{\mathrm{i}}\pi}(\mu^{2})\;.

(64)

The operator
\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})
describes parton splitting, changing an
m
parton state to an
m+1
parton state. The operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
leaves
m
and
\{p,f\}_{m}
in an
m
parton state unchanged, although it can modify the color state.
2
2

2

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
was denoted by
[\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})\circ\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})]\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mu^{2})
in Ref.
NSAllOrder
.
In a leading color parton shower, the color is unchanged and the eigenvalue of this operator then gives the order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
contribution to the integrand in the exponent of the Sudakov factor that represents the probability not to split between two scales. The final operator,
\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}_{{\mathrm{i}}\pi}(\mu^{2})
, leaves
m
and
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged. It gives the imaginary part of virtual graphs
NSAllOrder
;
NSThreshold
and obeys
({1}|\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}_{{\mathrm{i}}\pi}(\mu^{2})=0
.

The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})
has a perturbative expansion

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mu^{2})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\;.

(65)

The first order operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})
has the form
NSAllOrder
;
NSThreshold
;
NSThresholdII

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}+\mathrm{Re}\ \mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}_{\mathrm{pert}}(\mu^{2})\\
&-[\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})\circ\mathcal{P}^{(1)}]\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mu^{2})\;.\end{split}

(66)

Here
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{(1,0)}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
is proportional to the integral of the first order splitting function over the splitting variables and appears also in Eq. (
64
). In the third term,
[\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})\circ\mathcal{P}^{(1)}]
denotes the convolution of
\mathcal{F}(\mu^{2})
with the first order PDF evolution kernel
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
. In the second term,

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}^{(0,1)}_{\mathrm{pert}}(\mu^{2})=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\mathcal{D}^{(0,1)}(\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2},\mu^{2})\right]_{\mu_{\textsc{r}}^{2}=\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2}}

(67)

is the derivative with respect to the shower scale of the singular operator for a one loop virtual graph. It is sometimes assumed that the effect of virtual graphs and PDF evolution cancels the integral over the splitting variables of parton splitting
ISSudakov
. However, this cancellation is not complete, so that the effect of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{V}}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})
is quite important
ISSudakov
;
NSThresholdII
.

VIII
Generator of
\mathcal{Y}

We now turn to a more detailed study of the operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
. This operator sums logarithms, so we want to write it as an exponential. Define

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\mathcal{Y}^{-1}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\frac{d}{d\mu^{2}}\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(68)

This gives us a differential equation for
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})

\mu^{2}\frac{d}{d\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(69)

We solve this equation with a boundary condition at the shower cutoff scale
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
:

\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2};\bm{r})+\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(70)

Recall the defining condition Eq. (
33
) for
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
. At
\mu^{2}=\mu_{\rm f}^{2}
this condition is

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2};\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.

(71)

The measurement operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
is an infrared safe operator that is not sensitive to parton scales below a scale
Q_{J}^{2}(\bm{r})
. We suppose that
\mu_{\rm f}^{2}<Q_{J}^{2}(\bm{r})
. Then
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
commutes with
\mathcal{X}_{1}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2})
and we can use Eq. (
19
), which gives us
({1}|\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2};\bm{r})=({1}|
. Thus we can define

\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2};\bm{r})=1\;.

(72)

This allows us to write the solution of Eq. (
69
) as

\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})-1=\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\mathcal{Y}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(73)

We can also write
\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})
as a hardness-ordered exponential,

\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\right)\;.

(74)

To find the generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
we start with Eq. (
42
), which we write as

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.

(75)

This applies for
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
and
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
evaluated at any order
K
of perturbation theory, with corrections of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{K+1}
. We can also use Eq. (
75
) if
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
is an approximate shower evolution operator as defined in a particular parton shower algorithm. In this case, the shower splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
may be based on lowest order perturbation theory. If
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
is approximate, then Eq. (
75
) defines the corresponding approximate operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
and Eq. (
68
) defines the corresponding approximate generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
.

We can differentiate Eq. (
75
) with respect to
\mu^{2}
and use Eq. (
69
) for the derivative of
\mathcal{Y}
and Eq. (
53
) for the derivative of
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})
,

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,&\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(76)

We insert
1=\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
to give

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,&\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\\
&\times\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(77)

Using Eq. (
75
) then gives us

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};&\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(78)

The operators
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are nonsingular operators that leave the number of partons and their momenta and flavors unchanged. Thus we can use the mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
defined in Sec.
IV
to write this as

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};&\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(79)

The expansion of
\mathcal{Y}
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
starts at
\mathcal{Y}=1+\cdots
, so a useful way to write this is

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&-\{\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})-1\}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(80)

Now we can use Eqs. (
80
) and (
73
) recursively to generate
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\mathcal{Y}
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. We write

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;,\\
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;,\end{split}

(81)

with

\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=1\;.

(82)

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, Eq. (
80
) gives

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}&(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{(n-j)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(j)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{Y}^{(n-j)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(j)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(83)

This gives us
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}
if we know
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(j)}
for
j<n
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}
for
k<n
.

For
\mathcal{Y}
we use Eq. (
73
), in which an integration over an intermediate scale
\bar{\mu}^{2}
appears. We can expand
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\bar{\mu}^{2})
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})
in the form

\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\bar{\mu}^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{k}=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\gamma(k,n;\bar{\mu}^{2}/\mu^{2})\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\;,

(84)

with coefficients
\gamma
derived from the QCD
\beta
-function. Using this expansion in Eq. (
73
), we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-j}\gamma(k+j,n;\bar{\mu}^{2}/\mu^{2})\\
&\times\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\bm{r})\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(j)}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(85)

This gives us
\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}
if we know
\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}
for
k<n
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(j)}
for
j\leq n
.

These recursion relations successively generate
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}
,
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
, …. The first order terms are

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\end{split}

(86)

and

\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})=\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(87)

IX
Using
\mathcal{Y}

We now outline how the operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
can be used. This operator is the key to calculating an observable cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
according to a parton shower algorithm. The operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
that defines this cross section must be infrared safe. That is, there is a scale
Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})
such that
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
does not resolve parton splittings at scales
\mu^{2}
smaller than
Q^{2}_{J}(\bm{r})
. In order to define
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
, the inverse operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})
must exist. The anticipated use case is that there is a distribution of direct interest that involves large logarithms and the logarithms can be summed analytically by taking an integral transform of the distribution that depends on parameters
\bm{r}
. Then
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
represents the value of this integral transform. Starting in Sec.
XI
, we examine an important example, the thrust distribution in electron-positron annihilation. Then one uses the Laplace transform of the thrust distribution and
\bm{r}
is the Laplace parameter
\nu
.

In the applications that we have in mind, the perturbative expansion of
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
contains powers of a large logarithm
L(\bm{r})
when the parameter or parameters
\bm{r}
approach some limit. Typically, we have

\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=c_{0}\Bigg\{1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{2n}c(n,j)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{j}(\bm{r})\Bigg\}\,.

(88)

In favorable cases, there is an analytical formula that sums these logarithms in the form

\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})=c_{0}\exp\!\Bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{n+1}d(n,j)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{j}(\bm{r})\Bigg)\,.

(89)

It is crucial here that the maximum power of
L
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
is
j=n+1
, not
2n
. We can say that a
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
with this property exponentiates. One never knows all of the coefficients
d(n,j)
, but when the coefficients for
j=n+1
are known, we can say that the formula sums the logarithms at the leading-log (LL) level. When the coefficients for
j=n
are also known, we can say that the formula sums the logarithms at the next-to-leaadng-log (NLL) level.

In some important cases, the color space for the partons involved in the hard scattering process is trivial. For instance, for shape observables in electron-positron annihilation, there is only one color basis vector for the
q\bar{q}
state in
e^{+}e^{-}\to q\bar{q}
. Then the coefficients
d(n,j)
are numbers. The initial partonic state in hadron-hadron scattering has a nontrivial color structure. Then the coefficients
d(n,j)
may be integrals of matrices in the parton color space, with some specification for the ordering of noncommuting matrices in the exponent.

What does a parton shower algorithm say about
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
? Different parton showers can give different answers, so we should have a particular parton shower algorithm in mind.

We have seen that there are two ways to express
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
as given by a parton shower. First, we can use Eq. (
29
),

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;.\end{split}

(90)

Typically the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\rm f}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
is based on lowest order perturbation theory, as discussed at the beginning of Sec.
VII
. Additionally,
\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
is present in
Deductor
, but for many parton shower algorithms
\mathcal{V}=1
. Equation (
90
) says to run the parton shower to its cutoff scale and then measure the observable by applying
({1}|\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})
. The perturbative expansion of this result has the form (
88
), but not directly the form (
89
). One can run the corresponding parton shower event generator to obtain a numerical result with statistical errors and other numerical errors. Even with errors, it is possible
IngelmanSoper1984
;
DasguptaShowerSum
to use numerical results from Eq. (
90
) to check these results against a known QCD analytic result, as we will see later in this paper.

The second way to express
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
as given by a parton shower is contained in Eq. (
38
),

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})\mathcal{V}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{F}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})}\big)\;,\end{split}

(91)

with
\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})
given by Eq. (
74
) as an exponential of a generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2};\bm{r})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\right)\;.

(92)

The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is obtained from the shower generator
\mathcal{S}
using Eqs. (
83
) and (
85
). This second expression for
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
gives exactly the same
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
as given by Eq. (
90
). However, now the logarithms
L
appear in the exponent in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Thus we have a representation that is very close to the representation in Eq. (
89
).

The exponent in
\mathcal{Y}
is
3
3

3

This includes, possibly, ordering of operators or matrices in the exponential. For simplicity, we ignore questions of ordering here.

\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})=\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(93)

If we use the perturbative expansion of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, this is

\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.

(94)

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}
we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&+\Delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(95)

Here
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}
is the first term in Eq. (
83
) and is the only term for
n=1
. For
n>1
,
\Delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
is everything else in Eq. (
83
).

We can now expand
\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
. The perturbative coefficients will contain powers of the large logarithm
L(\bm{r})
. Let us divide
\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
into two pieces

\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})=\mathcal{I}_{0}(\bm{r})+\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})\;,

(96)

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{0}(\bm{r})={}&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\\
&\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}_{J}(\bm{r})\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}_{J}^{-1}(\bm{r})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;,\\
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})={}&\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\!\Delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})\;.\end{split}

(97)

If we use just
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\bm{r})
, we put just one shower splitting
\mathcal{S}
into the exponent. This is the candidate for the summation of logarithms
L(\bm{r})
as given by the shower. Its lowest order contribution, proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, will normally contain two powers of
L
after integrating over
\mu^{2}
. One power comes from integrating over a momentum fraction
z
inside
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
and the second power comes from integrating over
\mu^{2}
. Thus we have a LL contribution
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}L^{2}
. We also generate terms with higher powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, both from expanding the factor
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\kappa_{\textsc{r}}\mu^{2})
inside the integral and from using
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}(\mu^{2})
for
n>1
. With appropriate choices for the algorithm that constitutes
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
, one may be able to generate a whole series of terms
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{n+1}
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{n}
that match a known QCD result at the LL and NLL levels.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\bm{r})
gives the expected QCD result for the summation of logarithms at the NLL level. What, then, does the complete shower algorithm give? For this, we must examine
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
. We need to ask whether
\Delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{(n)}(\mu^{2};\bm{r})
is sufficiently small that it does not ruin the result from
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\bm{r})
. If
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
contains no nonzero contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{j}(\bm{r})
with
j>N+1
, then the logarithms
L
exponentiate. If there are no nonzero contributions with
j\geq N+1
, then the shower sums the logarithms at the LL level. If there are no nonzero contributions with
j\geq N
, then the shower sums the logarithms at the NLL level.

Eqs. (
96
) and (
97
) provide a way to check how accurately the parton shower algorithm sums the large logarithms
L(\bm{r})
. Suppose that we wish to check whether the shower sums the logarithms at NLL accuracy. The best method is to prove analytically that
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
meets the requirement for log summation at NLL accuracy. A second approach is to calculate the perturbative terms in
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
as numerical integrals and check how many powers of
L(\bm{r})
they contain. Although one can never check every term in
\Delta\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
, this method has the advantage that if the check for NLL summation fails for any one contribution, then we know that NLL summation fails.

X
Remarks about the general analysis

It is, we think, of some importance to understand how accurately a parton shower algorithm sums large logarithms in an observable
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
.

In analytical approaches to summing such logarithms, one typically defines an integral transform of the original distribution so that one considers a cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
that depends on parameters
\bm{r}
. Then the perturbative expansion of
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
contains large logarithms
L(\bm{r})
.

Sometimes, one can compare the results of the shower for
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
to the results in full QCD by writing the same differential equations as for full QCD but applying the differential operators to the shower approximation rather than full QCD
NSdglap
;
NSZpT
. This method has the disadvantage that one needs a separate and quite elaborate analysis for each observable to be studied.

An alternative is to calculate the observable
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
numerically with the parton shower event generator of interest and to compare the result with a known QCD result
IngelmanSoper1984
;
DasguptaShowerSum
. This method can work, at least for electron positron annihilation, but presents significant numerical challenges.

We have presented a reformulation of the calculation of
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
according to a parton shower so that the large logarithms appear directly as an exponential. The exponent can be expanded perturbatively. This gives us a path to an analytical understanding the summation of these logarithms in the parton shower. It also provides a simple way to test this summation numerically.

In the sections that follow, we find interesting results for the thrust distribution in electron-positron annihilation. The analysis for electron-positron annihilation, we represent
\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
in a form that is somewhat less general than the form presented above but is better adapted to practical applications. Then we analyze
\mathcal{I}(\bm{r})
analytically and numerically for the trust distribution in electron-positron annihilation.

XI
Analysis for electron-positron annihilation

As outlined in the previous sections, a parton shower event generator can provide a QCD based approximation for a cross section
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
for an observable
J
to take a value
\bm{v}
in hadron-hadron, lepton-hadron, or electron-positron collisions. In the following sections, we concentrate on electron-positron annihilation, which is simpler because parton distribution functions do not appear. We begin in this section by framing the issues in a little more detail than we presented in Sec.
I
.

We suppose that the observable
J
is infrared safe with a scale
\hat{Q}^{2}_{J}(\bm{v})
substantially greater than
1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{2}
. Then we can, at least in principle, omit a model for hadronization in the event generator. The QCD perturbative expansion for
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
will contain logarithms,
L=\log(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}/\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v}))
, where
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
is the scale of the hardest interaction in the event. Typically one finds perturbative contributions to
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})L^{2n}
. If
1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{2}\ll\hat{Q}_{J}^{2}(\bm{v})\ll\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
, and
L^{2}\gtrsim 1/\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
, one must try to sum the contributions at each order of perturbation theory that have the most powers of
L
.

For some observables
J
one can derive an analytical approximation,
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{analytical})
, to
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
that sums the large logarithms in an appropriate sense. It is then of interest to see whether the parton shower generator sums the large logarithms at a specified level of approximation.

Normally, the approximation
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
obtained with a parton shower is limited to a numerical result obtained by averaging over many generated events. In the limit of very large hard scattering scales
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
,
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
should match
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{analytical})
. However, for
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
in the kinematic range of experiments,
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
contains effects that are numerically important but are not included in
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{analytical})
. Thus it is difficult to tell whether
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
agrees with
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{analytical})
.

One approach to comparing
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
to
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{analytical})
is to directly calculate
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v};\mathrm{shower})
for a sequence of very large hard scattering scales
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
that are far from the range of experiments. This approach can work
DasguptaShowerSum
, and in fact we use it to a limited extent in this paper. However, it is difficult to maintain the required numerical accuracy at very large values of
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}
in a practical parton shower event generator.

In an analytical approach, one typically starts by taking an appropriate integral transform of
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\bm{v})
. Then one calculates a cross section
\sigma_{J}(\bm{r})
depending on a variable or variables
\bm{r}
. For instance, one may be interested in the distribution of the thrust parameter,
T
, so that one examines
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\tau)
where
\tau=1-T
. Then one takes the Laplace transform of
\hat{\sigma}_{J}(\tau)
with Laplace parameter
\nu
. Then we need to sum logarithms
L=\log(\nu)
, which is large when
\nu\to\infty
.

The aim of the following sections is to follow the general method outlined in sections
II
through
X
so as to redesign the calculation of the parton shower cross section so that it produces the same result for the integral transform of the cross section as before but so that it produces a calculation of this quantity and not a cross section for other observables. The redesigned calculation gives the integral transform of interest as an exponential of a quantity that can be expanded in powers of the shower splitting operator. The leading order term in the exponent is simple and is the candidate for the summation of large logarithms produced by the shower. If the higher order contributions to the exponent are suitably small, then they to not interfere with the summation represented by the leading order terms. In some favorable cases, we can analyze all higher order contributions to the exponent analytically. In other cases, we calculate low order contributions to the exponent numerically.

Our example is the thrust distribution. There is much to be learned from this example. In particular, we learn that the shower result depends on some details of the parton shower algorithm that one might have thought are not important.

XII
The parton shower framework

We begin with a brief review of the parton shower framework that we will use, expanding on the material at the start of Sec.
II
, but simplifying this material with respect to initial state partons, which do not appear in electron-positron annihilation, and with respect to spin.

A parton shower can be described using operators on a vector space, the “statistical space,” that describes the momenta, flavors, colors, and spins for all of the partons created in a shower as the shower develops. We use this description in the parton shower event generator
Deductor

NSI
;
NSII
;
NSspin
;
NScolor
;
Deductor
;
ShowerTime
;
NSThreshold
;
NSAllOrder
;
NSNewColor
;
NSColoriPi
. The general theory includes parton spins but
Deductor
simply averages over spins, so our explanation in the following sections will leave out parton spins. With
m
final state partons in electron-positron annihilation, the partons carry labels
1,2,\dots,m
. The partons have momenta
\{p\}_{m}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\}
and flavors
\{f\}_{m}
. We take the partons to be massless:
p_{i}^{2}=0
. For color, there are ket color basis states
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
and bra color basis states
\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. We use the trace basis (or color-string basis), as described in Sec. 7 of Ref.
NSI
. Color appears in the statistical space as the density matrix, with basis elements
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. Then the
m
-parton basis states for the statistical space are denoted by
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. The statistical space is described in Secs. 2 and 3 of Ref.
NSI
. These sections also show how shower evolution is expressed using evolution operators that act on the statistical space. (However, in this paper the names of the operators follow Ref.
NSAllOrder
rather than Ref.
NSI
.)

Parton shower programs often use the leading color (LC) approximation, which provides the leading term in an expansion in powers of
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}=1/9

Herwig
;
Pythia
;
Sherpa
. With this approach, the color states
\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}
obey
\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}=\{c\}_{m}
. The splitting probabilities are simply proportional to a factor
C_{\mathrm{F}}
or
C_{\mathrm{A}}
, with
C_{\mathrm{F}}
being equivalent to
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
within the LC approximation.

Our program,
Deductor

Deductor
, uses what is called the LC+ approximation
NScolor
. The LC+ approximation consists of simply dropping some color operator contributions in the splitting functions. The LC+ approximation is more powerful than the LC approximation because it has corrections only for soft, finite angle emissions but is exact in the limit of collinear emissions
NScolor
. For this reason, the LC+ approximation is more accurate than the LC approximation for summing large logarithms correctly in a parton shower. Ref.
HamiltonShowerSum
analyzes the effect of various forms of the LC approximation on the summation of large logarithms.

In this paper, we focus on the effect on the summation of large logarithms from characteristics of the parton shower formulation such as the ordering variable, the momentum mapping, and the splitting functions. We do not focus on the treatment of color. Thus we mostly use full QCD color without approximation. Where we simply run
Deductor
to produce the thrust distribution, we use
Deductor
’s default color approximation, LC+. There, for the
Deductor
default ordering variable and momentum mapping, we verify numerically that the LC+ approximation is essentially exact for the thrust distribution. (See Fig.
4
.) At one point, in Fig.
5
, we make contact with Ref.
HamiltonShowerSum
by investigating what happens when one uses a less exact color approximation that is one version of the LC approximation.

Deductor
uses specific choices with respect to shower kinematics, the shower ordering variable, and the parton splitting functions. In the remainder of this section, we outline some of these choices that play a role in the analysis of the following sections.

In
Deductor
, the default is to order splittings according to decreasing values of a hardness parameter
\Lambda^{2}

ShowerTime
. This hardness parameter is based on virtuality. For massless final state partons in electron-positron collisions, the definition is
4
4

4

In hadron-hadron collisions,
Q
in Eq. (
98
) is replaced by the momentum
Q_{0}
of the final state partons at the start of the shower.

\begin{split}\Lambda^{2}={}&\frac{(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}\,Q^{2}\;.\end{split}

(98)

Here the mother parton in a final state splitting has momentum
p_{l}
and the daughters have momenta
\hat{p}_{l}
and
\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}
. Here
Q
is the total momentum
Q
of all of the final state partons, which remains the same throughout the shower. It proves convenient to use a dimensionless virtuality variable
y=\Lambda^{2}/Q^{2}
:

y=\frac{(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}\;.

(99)

Thus
y
decreases from one shower splitting to the next.

One could use a hardness parameter other than
\Lambda
to order the shower. We will consider also a shower ordered by the transverse momentum
NSThreshold
in a splitting,

k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}=z(1-z)(\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{2}=z(1-z)y\,Q^{2}\!/a_{l}\;,

(100)

where
z
is the momentum fraction in the splitting and

a_{l}=\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}\;.

(101)

We denote the hardness scale of a splitting by
\mu^{2}
. When we use the default ordering variable
\Lambda
for the shower, then
\mu^{2}=\Lambda^{2}
. If we use
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, then
\mu^{2}=k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
.

To measure an infrared-safe observable
\mathcal{O}_{J}
in electron-positron annihilation, we can use the notation

\begin{split}\sigma_{J}={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}_{J}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},Q^{2})\,\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(102)

Here
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
is the starting parton state for the hard scattering process. If we were to evaluate
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
beyond leading order, then it would contain appropriate subtractions to remove infrared singularities. In this paper, we evaluate
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
at lowest order so that it is simply a
q\bar{q}
state. We associate a scale
\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2}=Q^{2}
with the hard scattering, where
Q
is the
q\bar{q}
momentum. The operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},Q^{2})
expresses the evolution of the system from the scale
Q^{2}
to a scale
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
of order
1\ \mathrm{GeV}^{2}
, at which the shower is turned off. After this evolution, we have a statistical state that can be expanded in the basis states
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. This expansion is realized as an integral, which takes the form of a Monte Carlo integration that is obtained by generating many Monte Carlo events. We then apply an operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}
that embodies the desired measurement. We still have a sum and integral of basis states. We take the product with the statistical bra state
({1}|
, which is defined by

\big({1}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\;.

(103)

This leaves us with the numerical result for
\sigma_{J}
. The use of the statistical bra vector
({1}|
is discussed in Sec. 3.5 of Ref.
NSI
.

The shower operator
\mathcal{U}
takes the form

\mathcal{U}(\mu_{2}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{2}^{2}}^{\mu_{1}^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\right)\;.

(104)

There is an instruction
\mathbb{T}
that indicates that if we expand the exponential, the operators
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
with the smallest values of
\mu^{2}
belong on the left. This is simply a compact way of saying that
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{2}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})
obeys the differential equation

\mu_{1}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu_{1}^{2}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{2}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})=\mathcal{U}(\mu_{2}^{2},\mu_{1}^{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{1}^{2})\;.

(105)

In general, the generator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
is a sum of terms with approximations to
n_{\textsc{r}}
real emissions and
n_{\textsc{v}}
virtual exchanges,
