\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}}=0\\
n_{\textsc{r}}+n_{\textsc{v}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\mathcal{S}^{[n_{\textsc{r}},n_{\textsc{v}}]}(\mu^{2})\;.

(106)

In existing parton shower event generators like
Deductor
, only the terms with
n_{\textsc{r}}+n_{\textsc{v}}=1
are implemented. This is also the case for other parton shower algorithms that we consider here. Thus we assume

\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})\;.

(107)

The operator
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
creates a splitting, changing an
m
parton state to an
m+1
parton state. The operator
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})
leaves the number of partons and their momenta and flavors unchanged, although in a full color treatment it modifies the parton color state. The operator
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})
is related to the inclusive sum over splitting variables in
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
by
({1}|\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})=-({1}|\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
, so that

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=0\;.

(108)

If we had contributions to the shower generator with
n_{\textsc{r}}+n_{\textsc{v}}>1
, we would still have
({1}|\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=0

NSAllOrder
.

The operator
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(yQ^{2})
in
Deductor

NScolor
;
NSThreshold
is not simple. However, in the cases for which we need an explicit expression in our analytical formulas here, we need only its form when
y\ll 1
and
(1-z)\ll 1
. This is the limit in which
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(yQ^{2})
expresses the soft
\times
collinear double singularity of QCD. (However, our numerical results use the full
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(yQ^{2})
.) In this limit, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}&(yQ^{2})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
\approx{}&-\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\,[T_{l}\otimes T_{k}^{\dagger}+T_{k}\otimes T_{l}^{\dagger}]\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\int\!\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\int\!\frac{dz}{1-z}\ \frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)yQ^{2}/a_{l})}{2\pi}\\
&\times\Theta\!\left(\frac{a_{l}y}{\vartheta(l,k)}<1-z<1\right)\\
&\times\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(109)

There is a sum over parton indices
l
and
k
. We split parton
l
with dipole partner parton
k
, creating a new parton
m+1
, which we consider to be a gluon. The momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}
of the partons after the splitting are functions of the momenta
\{p\}_{m}
before the splitting and the splitting variables
y,z,\phi
, as specified in Eqs. (
189
) and (
194
).

In Eq. (
109
),
[T_{l}\otimes T_{k}^{\dagger}]
and
[T_{k}\otimes T_{l}^{\dagger}]
are operators on the parton color space. The notation
(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})
for color operators represents the following. A color basis vector
|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
in the statistical space represents the color density operator
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. Here
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
and
|{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\rangle
are basis vectors for color amplitudes. Let
C_{\textrm{ket}}
and
C_{\textrm{bra}}
be operators on color amplitudes for
m
partons that yield color amplitudes for
\hat{m}
partons with
\hat{m}\geq m
. In the case of
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(yQ^{2})
,
\hat{m}=m+1
. The statistical space vector
(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
then represents the color density operator
C_{\textrm{ket}}|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}
. In the case of
[T_{l}\otimes T_{k}^{\dagger}]
, the operator creates a new gluon with color index
a
by inserting a color generator matrix
T^{a}
on the color line for parton
l
in the ket state and inserting
T^{a}
on the color line for parton
k
in the bra state.

The argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in Eq. (
109
) contains the standard factor
CMW

\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}=\exp\left(-\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}(67-3\pi^{2})-10\,n_{\mathrm{f}}}{3\,(11C_{\mathrm{A}}-2\,n_{\mathrm{f}})}\right)\;.

(110)

The rest of the argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
is
k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
, Eq. (
100
), except that we drop the factor
z
because we are interested only in small
1-z
. Although the operators
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
contain one power of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
, this
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
is evaluated at a scale that is not
\mu^{2}
. Thus if we expand
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})
, all powers will appear.

The parameter
\vartheta(l,k)
is

\vartheta(l,k)=\frac{1}{2}[1-\cos(\theta(l,k))]\;,

(111)

where
\theta(l,k)
is the angle between partons
l
and
k
in
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. With this definition,
\vartheta\approx\theta^{2}/4
for small
\theta
. The angle
\hat{\theta}(l,{m\!+\!1})
between partons
l
and
m+1
after a splitting is given by

\begin{split}1-\cos(\hat{\theta}(l,{m\!+\!1}))={}&\frac{Q^{2}}{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot Q\ \hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\cdot Q}\,\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}\\
\sim{}&\frac{2a_{l}y}{z(1-z)}+\mathcal{O}(y^{2})\;.\end{split}

(112)

For small
y
and small
(1-z)
, this gives

\hat{\vartheta}(l,{m\!+\!1})\approx\frac{a_{l}y}{1-z}\;.

(113)

Thus the lower limit on
(1-z)
is equivalent to an upper limit on the splitting angle,
\hat{\vartheta}(l,{m\!+\!1})<\vartheta(l,k)
. The splitting angle should be smaller than the angle between the two partons
l
and
k
. The restriction
(1-z)<1
gives a lower limit on the splitting angle. The net range for the new splitting angle is

a_{l}y<\hat{\vartheta}(l,{m\!+\!1})<\vartheta(l,k)\;.

(114)

XIII
Preview

In the following sections, we propose a way, for electron-positron annihilation, to gain more direct access to the summation of large logarithms in a parton shower than by simply running the shower and examining the result numerically. The analysis adapts the general formulation of the method in Secs.
II
through
X
to the practical analysis of first order parton shower algorithms. Our example is the thrust distribution in electron-positron annihilation. Here is a brief preview.

•

We are interested in the thrust distribution
g(\tau)
with
\tau=1-T
, where
T
is the thrust.

•

As in analytical approaches, we work with the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
of
g(\tau)
.

•

\tilde{g}(\nu)
contains large logarithms,
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})^{n}\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq 2n
.

•

We suppose that we know the proper summation of the
\log(\nu)
factors in full QCD at a certain level of accuracy, but a leading order parton shower is not full QCD. We wish to know what result the parton shower gives.

•

The result of simply running the shower and examining the result numerically can be expressed as in Eq. (
102
),

\tilde{g}(\nu)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},Q^{2})\,\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.

(115)

Here
\sigma_{\textsc{h}}
is the total hard scattering cross section and
({1}|\cdots|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
indicates an ensemble average in the statistical state
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
representing the perturbative hard scattering. Then
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},Q^{2})
represents the operator on the statistical space that generates the shower. This gives us states consisting of tens of partons. We could measure any operator
\mathcal{O}_{J}
that we like in this many-parton state. We apply a simple operator
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
that measures the Laplace transformed thrust distribution on this state.

•

In this paper, we rewrite
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in the form

\tilde{g}(\nu)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathbb{T}\exp\!\big(\mathcal{I}(\nu)\big)\mathcal{O}(\nu)\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.

(116)

The notation
\mathbb{T}
indicates an ordering instruction for the exponential, as in Eq. (
104
) and later in Eq. (
145
). In the example used in this paper, the operator
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
applied to the hard state
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
simply gives an eigenvalue 1.

•

With this form, we have expressed
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in terms of the exponential of an operator
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
. This operator has an expansion
5
5

5

In Secs.
II
through
X
, we expanded operators in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})
at a running scale
\mu^{2}
appropriate to the operator. Here, we expand operators in powers of the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}
of the parton shower. This technique simplifies the analysis of a shower algorithm that is based on lowest order perturbation theory.

\mathcal{I}(\nu)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)\;,

(117)

where each term in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
contains
k
factors of the splitting operator
\mathcal{S}
.

•

We can further expand in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
evaluated at a fixed scale
Q^{2}/\nu
:

\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)\;.

(118)

•

The most important feature of Eq. (
116
) is that the operators
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
can be computed using two fairly simple recursion relations.

•

The first order contribution,
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
, is obtained rather trivially from one power of the shower splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
. This operator is then the obvious candidate for the exponentiation of
\tilde{g}(\nu)
generated by the shower. If
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
is suitably defined,
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
matches the exponentiation in full QCD.

•

If
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
generates the desired exponentiation, then
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
for
k\geq 2
should be small, so as not to destroy the desired exponentiation.

•

For next-to-leading-log summation (NLL), this implies that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
should not contain more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

•

In one case examined in this paper, we can show analytically that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
does not contain more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

•

The operator
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
is of special interest. It should not contain more than one power of
\log(\nu)
.

•

In some cases, we can show analytically that
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
does not contain more than one power of
\log(\nu)
.

•

We can write the integral for
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{n}(\nu)
and evaluate it numerically to see if it contains more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

•

For some shower algorithms examined here,
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{n}(\nu)
passes this test. For one algorithm examined, it fails.

XIV
The thrust distribution and its Laplace transform

We will examine the distribution of thrust,
T
, defined for parton momenta
\{p\}_{m}
by
thrustdef1
;
thrustdef2

T=\max_{\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}}\frac{\sum_{i}|\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}|}{\sum_{i}|\vec{p}_{i}|}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Q^{2}}}\ \max_{\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}}\sum_{i}|\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}|\;.

(119)

The axis defined by the unit vector
\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}
that maximizes the sum is the
thrust axis
. We will be interested in the behavior of the thrust distribution for small values of

\tau=1-T\;.

(120)

We can write
\tau
in a useful form by defining sets
R
and
L
of partons by
\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}>0
for
i\in R
and
\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}<0
for
i\in L
. Then,

\tau=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Q^{2}}}\left[\sum_{i\in R}(E_{i}-\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}})+\sum_{i\in L}(E_{i}+\vec{p}_{i}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}})\right].

(121)

Using the thrust axis, we define
\pm
components of vectors by

p^{\pm}=\left[p_{i}^{0}\pm\vec{p}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}\right]/\sqrt{2}\;.

(122)

Then we can write

\tau=\tau_{R}+\tau_{L}\;,

(123)

where, using
Q^{2}=2Q^{+}Q^{-}
with
Q^{+}=Q^{-}
,

\begin{split}\tau_{R}=\sum_{i\in R}\frac{p_{i}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\;,\quad\quad\tau_{L}=\sum_{i\in L}\frac{p_{i}^{+}}{Q^{+}}\;.\end{split}

(124)

In order to use a parton shower to analyze the thrust distribution, we begin with the cross section

g(\tau)=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\frac{d\sigma}{d\tau}\;,

(125)

where
\sigma_{\textsc{h}}
is the hard scattering cross section, equal to
d\sigma/d\tau
integrated over
\tau
. We wish to analyze the small
\tau
behavior of
g(\tau)
. For this purpose, it is standard to work with the Laplace transform of
g(\tau)
,

\tilde{g}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\tau\ e^{-\nu\tau}g(\tau)\;.

(126)

The coefficient of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
in the perturbative expansion of
g(\tau)
is not a normal function but is a distribution with
\log^{j-1}(\tau)/\tau
singularities at
\tau=0
. In order to work with normal functions, we define the integral of
g(\tau)
,

f(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}\!d\bar{\tau}\ g(\bar{\tau})\;.

(127)

The coefficients in the perturbative expansion of
f(\tau)
are functions with
\log^{j}(\tau)
integrable singularities. The cross section
g(\bar{\tau})
vanishes for
\bar{\tau}>1/2
, so
f(\tau)=1
for
\tau>1/2
.

Consider the Laplace transform of
f(\tau)
:

\tilde{f}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\tau\ e^{-\nu\tau}f(\tau)\;.

(128)

We have

\begin{split}\tilde{f}(\nu)={}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\tau^{\prime}\ e^{-\nu\tau^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\tau^{\prime}}\!d\tau\ g(\tau)\\
={}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\tau\ g(\tau)\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\!d\tau^{\prime}\ e^{-\nu\tau^{\prime}}\\
={}&\frac{1}{\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!d\tau\ g(\tau)\,e^{-\nu\tau}\;.\end{split}

(129)

Thus

\tilde{f}(\nu)=\frac{\tilde{g}(\nu)}{\nu}\;.

(130)

The function
f(\tau)
is given by the inverse Laplace transform of
\tilde{f}(\nu)
:

f(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\int_{C}\!d\nu\ e^{\nu\tau}\,\frac{\tilde{g}(\nu)}{\nu}\;.

(131)

The contour
C
runs from
\nu_{0}-{\mathrm{i}}\infty
to
\nu_{0}+{\mathrm{i}}\infty
parallel to the imaginary
\nu
axis, where
\nu_{0}>0
so that the contour is to the right of the singularity of
\tilde{g}(\nu)/\nu
at
\nu=0
.

We expect the coefficient of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
in the perturbative expansion of
f(\tau)
to contain terms proportional to
\log^{j}(\tau)
for
\tau\to 0
. To see how this translates to
\tilde{g}(\nu)
, we can start by noting that

f(\tau)=\tau^{A}\implies\tilde{g}(\nu)=\Gamma(1+A)\,\nu^{-A}\;.

(132)

Thus

\begin{split}f(\tau)={}&\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{A^{j}}{j!}\,\log^{j}(\tau)\implies\\
\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\Gamma(1+A)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-A)^{j}}{j!}\,\log^{j}(\nu)\;.\end{split}

(133)

Matching powers of
A
, we learn that logarithms of
\tau
for small
\tau
translate into logarithms of
\nu
for large
\nu
.

We wish to use the parton shower formalism to find an analytical formula that sums the logarithms of
\nu
in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
. We can then compare what we find to the standard QCD formula that sums these logarithms. The final step needed to obtain something that can be compared to experiment would be to perform the inverse Laplace transform (
131
). This step is the same for the parton shower method or the normal analytical methods. We discuss this step only briefly in this paper.

XV
The measurement operator

If we want to measure the thrust distribution, then we define, following Eq. (
102
),

\begin{split}g(\tau)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\delta(\tau-\tau_{\mathrm{op}})\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})\,\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(134)

where
\tau
is a real number times the unit operator on the statistical space and
\tau_{\mathrm{op}}
is the operator defined by

\tau_{\mathrm{op}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\tau(\{p\}_{m})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,

(135)

where
\tau(\{p\}_{m})
is
1-T
for partons with momenta
\{p\}_{m}
, as defined in Eqs. (
123
) and (
124
). Here
\sigma_{\textsc{h}}=({1}|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
. This is the Born cross section for
e^{+}e^{-}\to q\bar{q}
since, in this paper, we evaluate
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
at lowest order.

Rather than measuring
g(\tau)
, we wish to measure the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
. For this we have, using Eq. (
126
) in Eq. (
134
),

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},Q^{2})\,\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(136)

where

\mathcal{O}(\nu)=e^{-\nu\tau_{\mathrm{op}}}\;.

(137)

We will analyze
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in the subsequent sections. For this analysis, it is important that
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
has an inverse

\mathcal{O}(\nu)^{-1}=e^{\nu\tau_{\mathrm{op}}}\;.

(138)

XVI
Setting up the shower analysis

Eq. (
136
) allows us to calculate
\tilde{g}(\nu)
numerically using the shower evolution operator
\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu_{\textsc{h}}^{2})
. We would now like to reformulate the shower result so that it takes the form of an exponential in which the exponent can be perturbatively calculated.

XVI.1
The operators
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}

We begin with an operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, which is defined in Secs.
III
and
VIII
using the all-order formalism of Ref.
NSAllOrder
for describing parton shower algorithms. The operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
is defined to have two properties. First, it does not change the number of partons or their momenta or flavors. Second,

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\;.

(139)

These properties apply either for electron-positron, hadron-hadron, or electron-hadron collisions. Now, we consider only electron-positron annihilation. Although
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
does not change the number of partons or their momenta or flavors, it can change the parton colors. There is some freedom to define what
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
does to the parton color state. We will define the action of
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
on states in the statistical space in Eqs. (
170
) and (
171
) below.

The property Eq. (
139
) can be written as

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu^{2})=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\mathcal{O}(\nu)\;.

(140)

This result allows us to rewrite
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by Eq. (
136
) as

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(Q^{2};\nu)\mathcal{O}(\nu)\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(141)

We see that instead of generating a complete parton shower as in Eq. (
136
) and then measuring
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
for the resulting many parton state, we can measure
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
just on the hard state and then apply the operator
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
that depends on
\nu
but leaves the number of partons unchanged.

How can one evaluate
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
? We note first from the form of Eq. (
139
), that
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
has a perturbative expansion beginning with
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)=1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}})
and at
\mu^{2}=\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
it is exactly

\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2};\nu)=1\;.

(142)

We define an infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
for
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
by

\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\mathcal{Y}^{-1}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\frac{d}{d\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;.

(143)

Then
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
obeys the differential equation

\mu^{2}\frac{d}{d\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;,

(144)

with boundary condition
\mathcal{Y}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2};\nu)=1
. We can use the notation

\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\right)

(145)

to indicate the solution to Eq. (
144
). The instruction
\mathbb{T}
indicates that the operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)
with the smallest values of
\bar{\mu}^{2}
belong on the left.

We will sometimes adopt the notation

\mathcal{I}(\nu)=\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)

(146)

when the upper integration limit is
Q^{2}
and we do not need to explicitly display
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
.
6
6

6

This is a useful definition even though
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
is not the exponential of
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
because of the
\mathbb{T}
instruction in Eq. (
145
).

XVI.2
Relation of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
to the shower generator
\mathcal{S}

We can relate
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2},\nu)
to
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
. From Eq. (
105
), we have

\begin{split}\mu^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{2}}\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu^{2})={}&\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\;.\end{split}

(147)

Using Eqs. (
147
) and (
144
) to differentiate Eq. (
139
), we have

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)&\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{U}(\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2},\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\;.\end{split}

(148)

Using Eq. (
140
), this becomes

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,&\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\\
={}&\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\;.\end{split}

(149)

We can also use Eq. (
144
), together with the boundary condition (
142
), to write an equation for
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&1+\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\ \mathcal{Y}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\;.\end{split}

(150)

XVI.3
Operator mapping
\mathbb{P}

To use Eq. (
149
), we introduce some useful notation, expanding on Sec.
IV
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an operator that increases the number of partons or leaves the number of partons unchanged and changes momenta, flavors, and colors. Let
\mathcal{B}
be an operator on the statistical space that leaves the number
m
of partons and their momenta and flavors
\{p,f\}_{m}
unchanged, although it can change the parton color state.
7
7

7

In Sec.
IV
,
\mathcal{A}
is sometimes an operator that is defined in
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensions that contains poles
1/\epsilon
and singularities when the momenta of partons created by
\mathcal{A}
become soft or collinear. However,
({1}|\mathcal{A}
is well defined in
d=4
dimensions. Then
\mathcal{B}
is well defined in 4 dimensions.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be defined such that

\big({1}\big|\mathcal{B}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}\;.

(151)

We will define a linear relation
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
that realizes this relation. To represent this linear relation, we adopt the notation

\mathcal{B}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(152)

The needed construction is straightforward. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
maps states with
m
partons into states with
\hat{m}
partons, with
\hat{m}\geq m
. Let
\mathcal{A}
have the form

\mathcal{A}=(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\,\mathcal{R}\;,

(153)

where
\mathcal{R}
acts on the momentum and flavor factor of the statistical space and
(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})
acts on the color factor. Recall from Sec.
XII
the meaning of the color operators
(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})
. Letting
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle
and
|{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\rangle
be basis vectors for color amplitudes, a color basis vector
|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
in the statistical space represents the color density operator
|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|
. Then
(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
represents the color density operator
C_{\textrm{ket}}|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}
.

Let us evaluate
({1}|\mathcal{A}|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
for an arbitrary
m
-parton basis state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. The inner product of
({1}|
with a statistical basis state is given in Eq. (
103
). We insert a sum over the basis states
NSI
with
\hat{m}
partons,

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\frac{1}{\hat{m}!}\int[d\{p,f\}_{\hat{m}}]\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big({1}\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big)\\
&\times\big({\{\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{R}\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(154)

For the color, this gives us the trace of the color density operator obtained by applying
C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}
to
|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
, namely the trace of
C_{\textrm{ket}}|{\{c\}_{m}}\rangle\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}|C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}
. The result is

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}&\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle\\
&\times\frac{1}{\hat{m}!}\int[d\{p,f\}_{\hat{m}}]\ \big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{R}\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(155)

We now need to define
\mathcal{B}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
so that

\big({1}\big|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}\;.

(156)

We distinguish two cases. First, if
\hat{m}=m
we leave the color operator in
\mathcal{A}
unchanged,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\,\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-105.2751pt

(157)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{m!}\int[d\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{m}]\ \big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{m}}\big|\mathcal{R}\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)\;.

Evidently, this satisfies
({1}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}=({1}|\mathcal{A}
.

Second, if
\hat{m}>m
we define, with one exception,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\,\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-105.2751pt

(158)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes 1+1\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\right)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\hat{m}!}\int[d\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{\hat{m}}]\ \big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{\hat{m}}}\big|\mathcal{R}\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)\;.

This also satisfies
({1}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}=({1}|\mathcal{A}
.

The one exception concerns the first order splitting operator describing real emissions,
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}
. This operator contains a number of terms. There are some terms with color content that can be written in a shorthand notation as
t_{l}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{k}
. This describes the splitting of parton
l
in the ket state interfering with the splitting of parton
k
in the bra state. Parton
l
is treated as the splitting parton in the momentum dependent part of the splitting function, while parton
k
is the dipole partner parton. We can have
k=l
. When the newly created parton is a gluon, we can also have
k\neq l
. There are also terms in the splitting operator of the form
t_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{l}
in which the roles of the bra and ket color states are reversed. For
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}
, we define

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{t_{l}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{k}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}={}&1\otimes t_{k}\,t_{l}^{\dagger}\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{t_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{l}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}={}&t_{l}\,t_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes 1\;.\end{split}

(159)

In the case
k=l
, the color operator is
(t_{l}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{k}+t_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes t_{l})/2
, so one simply averages over the two cases in Eq. (
159
).
8
8

8

Additionally, for
k=l
, the operators
t_{k}\,t_{l}^{\dagger}
and
t_{l}\,t_{k}^{\dagger}
are color Casimir operators,
C_{\mathrm{F}}
,
C_{\mathrm{A}}
, or
T_{\mathrm{R}}
, so the two cases in Eq. (
159
) are really the same.
This asymmetric definition that depends on whether the dipole partner parton is in the bra state or the ket state makes the definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
match the definition of the virtual splitting operator
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}
in the LC+ approximation
NScolor
.

There is a special case of some importance. Suppose that
\hat{m}=m
and, in addition,
\mathcal{A}
leaves the momenta and flavors of all partons unchanged. That is,
|{\{p,f\}_{m}})
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{R}
:

\mathcal{R}\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)=r(\{p,f\}_{m})\big|{\{p,f\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(160)

Then
\mathcal{A}
applied to
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
takes the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)={}&(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\,r(\{p,f\}_{m})\\
&\times\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(161)

In this case, the definition (
157
) gives us

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}\;.

(162)

There is some freedom available in fixing the color part of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{(C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}})\,\mathcal{R}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
, as discussed in Sec. VI D of Ref.
NSAllOrder
. We could add any operator
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has the property that
({1}|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=0
. The form in Eqs. (
157
), (
158
), and (
159
) is recommended by its simplicity, so we will use it in this paper.

This defines the operator
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
in general. However, when
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
acts on the
q\bar{q}
initial hard scattering state in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation, the action of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
is simpler. The color space for
q\bar{q}
contains only one basis vector,
|{\{c,c\}_{2}})
, with
\{c^{\prime}\}_{2}=\{c\}_{2}
and
({1_{\mathrm{color}}}|{\{c,c\}_{2}})=\langle{\{c\}_{2}}|{\{c\}_{2}}\rangle=1
. Therefore
C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes 1
or
1\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}
acting on
|{\{c,c\}_{2}})
can only return an eigenvalue:

\begin{split}[C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\otimes 1]\big|{\{c,c\}_{2}}\big)={}&[1\otimes C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}]\big|{\{c,c\}_{2}}\big)\\
={}&\lambda_{\mathrm{color}}\big|{\{c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(163)

where

\lambda_{\mathrm{color}}=\big\langle{\{c\}_{2}}\big|C^{\dagger}_{\textrm{bra}}C_{\textrm{ket}}\big|{\{c\}_{2}}\big\rangle\;.

(164)

This tells us that
|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})
is an eigenvector of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)=\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;.

(165)

Using
({1}|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})=\langle{\{c\}_{2}}|{\{c\}_{2}}\rangle=1
, we have a very simple result for the eigenvalue,

\lambda_{\mathcal{A}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{A}\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;.

(166)

XVI.4
Recursive definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}

We can now define
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
so that it satisfies Eq. (
149
). Recall that
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)=1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}})
. Because of this, it is possible to isolate
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
on the left hand side of Eq. (
149
):

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&\big({1}\big|\Big\{\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\\
&+\left[1-\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\right]\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\Big\}\;.\end{split}

(167)

Using the operator mapping
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}
, this is

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\left[1-\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)\right]\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(168)

Note that the operators
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
in the second line of Eq. (
168
) leave the number of partons, their momenta, and their flavors unchanged. Thus Eq. (
162
) applies and the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
operation has no effect.

Equation (
168
) can be used to define
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
and
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
recursively. We can write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
,
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, and
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
as expansions in powers of the shower evolution operator
\mathcal{S}
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;,\\
\mathcal{Y}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;,\\
\mathcal{I}(\nu)={}&\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)\;,\end{split}

(169)

where each of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
,
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, and
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
contain
k
factors of
\mathcal{S}
. Then we can write Eq. (
168
) as

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[k-1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{O}(\nu)\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{Y}^{[k-j]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;.\end{split}

(170)

Similarly, we can write Eq. (
150
) as

\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\int_{\mu_{\rm f}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\,\mathcal{Y}^{[k-j]}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu).

(171)

These equations apply for
k=1,2,\dots
with
\mathcal{Y}^{[0]}(\mu^{2};\nu)=1
.

We now illustrate this for the first two orders. At order 1, Eq. (
170
) gives us

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(172)

At order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&-\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;.\end{split}

(173)

From Eq. (
150
) at first order, we have

\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)=\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\;.

(174)

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
we can use Eq. (
172
). This gives us

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{\mu^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&\quad\quad\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(175)

Here we use the abbreviation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{A}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{A}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(176)

The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
is a complicated operator in general. However, it is significant that, because of Eqs. (
165
) and (
166
), the initial
q\bar{q}
state is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)=\lambda_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;,

(177)

where

\lambda_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}}=\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;.

(178)

XVII
Evaluation of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)

Let us see what we can say about
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
as given in Eq. (
172
). In a first order shower, like
Deductor
, we divide
S(\mu^{2})
into its real emission and virtual parts as in Eq. (
107
). Then Eq. (
172
) gives us

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\\
&+\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(179)

The virtual operator
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2};0)
leaves the momentum and flavor state unchanged, so this is

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};&\nu)\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(180)

Recall from Eq. (
108
) that
({1}|\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})=-({1}|\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
. This tells us that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}=-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(181)

Using Eq. (
181
), Eq. (
180
) becomes

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};&\nu)\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)-\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(182)

This is a convenient form for calculations.

XVIII
Change in
\tau
induced by a splitting

The operator
\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)
appears in Eq. (
182
) for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
. This operator is

\begin{split}\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)={}&e^{-\nu\tau_{\textrm{op}}}\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})e^{+\nu\tau_{\textrm{op}}}\;.\end{split}

(183)

The operator
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
is a sum of operators,

\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\;,

(184)

where
l
is the label of the parton that splits. When we apply
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
to a state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
, the splitting operator creates a new state
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}})
as long as
l\leq m
. For
l>m
,
\mathcal{S}_{l}
just gives zero. The operators
\tau_{\textrm{op}}
measure the values of
\tau
before and after the splitting. Thus

\begin{split}\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\hskip-204.85974pt&\\
={}&e^{-\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}\big({\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big|\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(185)

where
\tau=\tau(\{p\}_{m})
and
\hat{\tau}=\tau(\{\hat{p}\}_{{m\!+\!1}})
. Thus we need to know how
\tau
changes in a splitting. We are looking for the leading contributions to logarithms of
\nu
, so we can use the approximations that
\tau
is small and that the splitting is nearly soft or collinear.

We start with momenta
\{p\}_{m}
and suppose that the parton that splits is in the right thrust hemisphere,
l\in R
. The splitting produces a new parton
l
and a parton
m+1
. After the splitting, we have partons with momenta
\{\hat{p}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}
.

The emission of a parton changes the thrust axis. However, in the case of a nearly soft or collinear splitting of a parton in a state with small
\tau
, the sum of the momenta of the daughter partons is very close to the momentum of the mother parton, so that the thrust axis changes by very little
thrustsum
. For this reason, we calculate
\tau(\{\hat{p}\}_{{m\!+\!1}})
for the new parton state using the thrust axis of the old parton state
\{p\}_{m}
. We also assume that after the splitting partons
l
and
m+1
are still in the right thrust hemisphere.

Now we turn to the calculation of
\hat{\tau}-\tau
. We use the definition, Eqs. (
123
) and (
124
), to write

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}-\tau={}&\frac{\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in R\\
i\neq\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{p}_{i}^{-}-p_{i}^{-}}{Q^{-}}+\sum_{i\in L}\frac{\hat{p}_{i}^{+}-p_{i}^{+}}{Q^{+}}\;.\end{split}

(186)

Now we need to evaluate
({\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{i}^{-}})/{Q^{-}}
and
(\hat{p}_{i}^{\pm}-p_{i}^{\pm})/Q^{\pm}
. Following the notation of Appendix B of Ref.
NSThreshold
, we define

\begin{split}h_{\pm}={}&(1+y\pm\lambda)/2\;,\\
\lambda={}&\sqrt{(1+y)^{2}-4a_{l}y}\;,\\
a_{l}={}&\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{l}\cdot Q}\;,\end{split}

(187)

where
y
was defined in Eq. (
99
). We suppose that
y\ll 1
. We define a lightlike vector
n_{l}
by

n_{l}=\frac{2p_{l}\cdot Q}{Q^{2}}\,Q-p_{l}\;.

(188)

Note that
n_{l}
is independent of the normalization of
Q
.

We write the momentum vectors for partons
l
and
m+1
after the splitting as

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{l}={}&h_{+}z\,p_{l}+h_{-}(1-z)n_{l}+k_{\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}={}&h_{+}(1-z)p_{l}+h_{-}z\,n_{l}-k_{\perp}\;,\end{split}

(189)

where
k_{\perp}\cdot p_{l}=k_{\perp}\cdot n_{l}=0
. The splitting is specified by
y
, the momentum fraction
z
in Eq. (
189
), and the azimuthal angle
\phi
of
k_{\perp}
. The magnitude of
k_{\perp}
is determined by the condition
\hat{p}_{l}^{2}=0
or
\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{2}=0
:

-k_{\perp}^{2}=z(1-z)y\,2p_{l}\cdot Q\;.

(190)

Define

P_{l}=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}=h_{+}p_{l}+h_{-}n_{l}\;.

(191)

This gives us
P_{l}^{2}=2p_{l}\cdot Q\,y
. Using these results we obtain

(Q-p_{l})^{2}=(Q-P_{l})^{2}\;.

(192)

We require that momentum be conserved in the splitting, so that

Q-p_{l}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}p_{i}\;,\qquad Q-P_{l}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}^{m}\hat{p}_{i}\;.

(193)

The relation (
192
) allows the
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\notin\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}
to be obtained from the
p_{i}
by a Lorentz transformation,

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}\,p_{i}^{\nu}\;,\hskip 28.45274pti\notin\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}\;.

(194)

The needed Lorentz transformation can be a small boost in the
p_{l}
-
Q
plane. Let

p_{i}=\alpha_{i}p_{l}+\beta_{i}n_{l}+p_{i,\perp}\;,

(195)

where
p_{i,\perp}\cdot p_{l}=p_{i,\perp}\cdot n_{l}=0
. Then define
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\notin\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}
by

\hat{p}_{i}=e^{\omega}\alpha_{i}p_{l}+e^{-\omega}\beta_{i}n_{l}+p_{i,\perp}\;.

(196)

The needed boost angle is small:

\omega=y+\mathcal{O}(y^{2})\;.

(197)

Using Eq. (
194
) in Eq. (
186
), we have

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}&-\tau=\frac{\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in R\\
i\neq l\end{subarray}}(\Lambda^{-}_{\nu}-\delta^{-}_{\nu})\frac{p_{i}^{\nu}}{Q^{-}}+\sum_{i\in L}(\Lambda^{+}_{\nu}-\delta^{+}_{\nu})\frac{p_{i}^{\nu}}{Q^{+}}\;.\end{split}

(198)

We will see momentarily that
(\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-})/Q^{-}
is small, of order
y
. This allows
\hat{\tau}-\tau
to be of order
y
.

In the third term, for
i\in\mathrm{R}
,
(\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}-\delta^{\mu}_{\nu})
is of order
y
. The thrust axis defines the
\pm
components of vectors in Eq. (
198
). If
p_{l}
were exactly aligned with the thrust axis, then the only nonvanishing index choice for
\Lambda^{-}_{\nu}
would be
\nu=-
. But
p_{i}^{-}/Q^{-}\ll 1
for
i\in\mathrm{R}
, since this quantity is of order
\tau
and we suppose that
\tau\ll 1
. This restriction on the index choices is not exact. However, for
i\in\mathrm{R}
, the components
p_{i}^{\nu}/Q^{-}
for
\nu\in\{1,2\}
are of order
p_{i}^{\nu}/Q^{-}\sim[{p_{i}^{+}p_{i}^{-}}]^{1/2}/Q^{-}
, which is at most of order
\sqrt{\tau}
. The component
p_{i}^{\nu}/Q^{-}
for
\nu=+
can be of order 1. However,
\Lambda^{-}_{+}=\Lambda^{--}
is at most of order
y^{2}
since
\Lambda=\exp(\omega w)
where
\omega
is given by Eq. (
197
) and the first order contribution to
\Lambda^{--}
vanishes because the generator matrix
w^{\mu\nu}
is antisymmetric. Thus the second term in Eq. (
198
) is of order
y
times a small factor, either
\tau
,
\sqrt{\tau}
, or
y
. The same reasoning applies to the third term.

We conclude that the only surviving term in Eq. (
198
) is the first:

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}-\tau\approx{}&\frac{\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\;.\end{split}

(199)

We have

\begin{split}\frac{\hat{p}_{l}^{-}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\approx{}&\frac{(1-a_{l})yp_{l}^{-}+a_{l}yn_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\;.\end{split}

(200)

With our kinematic conventions,

\begin{split}\frac{p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}={}&\frac{1-\cos\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}})}{2a_{l}}\;,\\
\frac{n_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}={}&\frac{1+\cos\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}})}{2a_{l}}\;,\end{split}

(201)

where

\cos\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}})=\frac{|\vec{p}_{l}\cdot\vec{n}_{\textsc{t}}|}{|\vec{p}_{l}|}\;.

(202)

This gives us

\frac{\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}^{-}-p_{l}^{-}}{Q^{-}}\approx\xi_{l}\,y\;,

(203)

where

\xi_{l}=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{2a_{l}}\right)[1-\cos(\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}))]\;.

(204)

That is

\hat{\tau}-\tau\approx\xi_{l}\,y\;.

(205)

The same result holds for
l\in L
if we change
1-\cos(\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}))
to
1+\cos(\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}))
.

If we are splitting the quark or the antiquark in the two parton state created initially in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation, then
a_{l}=1
and
\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}})=0
. Then
\xi_{l}=1
.

In the general case,
0<1-\cos(\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}))<1
for
l\in R
and
1/2<(2a_{l}-1)/(2a_{l})<1
, so

0<\xi_{l}<1\;.

(206)

We get
\xi_{l}\to 0
only when
\theta_{l}\to\pi/2
and parton
l
is very soft,
1/a_{l}\to 0
. Notice that there is no singularity for
\theta_{l}\to\pi/2
, so there is no singularity for
\xi_{l}\to 0
. There is a singularity for
\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}})\to 0
for all partons
l
. This corresponds to
\xi_{l}\to 1
. Thus in the general case we can treat
\xi_{l}
as being close to 1. We will argue in Appendix
A
that for the purpose of finding next-to-leading logarithms of
\nu
we can simply set
\xi_{l}
to 1.

We conclude that the effect of the operators
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
in a splitting of parton
l
can be approximated by

\begin{split}\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}_{l}(\mu^{2})&\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
\approx{}&\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}_{l}(\mu^{2})\,e^{-\nu\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}y}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(207)

where
\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}
is an operator that, acting on a state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
, has eigenvalue
\xi_{l}
as defined in Eq. (
204
) as long as
l\leq m
. For
l>m
, we can simply define
\xi_{l}
to have eigenvalue 1. We recall that
\xi_{l}
is generally of order 1 and equals 1 exactly in the case of a splitting of one of the partons in a two parton state. Using this in Eq. (
182
) gives us

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}&(\mu^{2};\nu)\approx-\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\nu\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}y})\;.\end{split}

(208)

XIX
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}
for a quark-antiquark state

For the
q\bar{q}
state created initially in electron-positron annihilation, Eq. (
208
) simplifies considerably. First, the index
l
denoting the parton that splits can take only the values
l=1
(for the quark) and
l=2
(for the antiquark). Each choice gives the same result, so we can take
l=1
and multiply by two. Also, the color factors are trivial. In
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
we encounter color operators
\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{1}
,
\bm{T}_{2}\cdot\bm{T}_{2}
, and
\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{2}
, where
\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}=\sum_{a}T_{i}^{a}T_{j}^{a}
and
T_{i}^{a}
inserts a color matrix
T^{a}
on parton line
i
. The operators
\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{1}
and
\bm{T}_{2}\cdot\bm{T}_{2}
simply give an eigenvalue
C_{\mathrm{F}}
times the unit color operator, while
\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{2}
gives
-C_{\mathrm{F}}
. This gives us a result of the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};&\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{2}}\big)\\
\approx{}&-(1-e^{-\nu y})\,\lambda(y)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{2}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(209)

The eigenvalue
\lambda(y)
is obtained in a straightforward calculation from the
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
splitting functions used in
Deductor

NScolor
. There is an integral over the splitting variables
z
and
\phi
. The
\phi
integral is trivial and gives simply a factor
2\pi
. The integration over the momentum fraction
z
remains,

\begin{split}\lambda(y)={}&2C_{\mathrm{F}}\int_{0}^{1}\!dz\ \Big\{\frac{\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,y)\\
&\quad+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ f_{\mathrm{reg}}(z,y)\Big\}\;.\end{split}

(210)

The argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
contains the standard factor
\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}
, Eq. (
110
), and, in the first term, a factor
(1-z)
, as in Eq. (
109
) with
a_{l}=1
. The functions
f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,y)
and
f_{\mathrm{reg}}(z,y)
are taken directly from
Deductor
and are quite complicated. However, they are simple in the relevant limits,
y\to 0
with fixed
z
and
y\to 0
with
1-z\propto y
. In these limits, they are

\begin{split}f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,y)\approx{}&\frac{2}{1-z+y}-2\;,\\
f_{\mathrm{reg}}(z,y)\approx{}&1-z\;.\end{split}

(211)

Note that
f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,0)+f_{\mathrm{reg}}(z)=(1+z^{2})/(1-z)
is just the DGLAP splitting kernel for
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
. However in
f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,y)
the singularity at
(1-z)\to 0
is regulated by adding
y
in the denominator.

We have written these results in the form used in
Deductor
. In
f_{\mathrm{sing}}(z,y)
, we could recognize that the second term could have been transferred to
f_{\mathrm{reg}}(z,y)
.

We would now like to compare this to the standard results for the summation of logs of
\tau
in Ref.
thrustsum
. We begin by inserting Eq. (
211
) into Eq. (
210
):

\begin{split}\lambda(y)\approx{}&2C_{\mathrm{F}}\int_{0}^{1}\!dz\ \Big\{\frac{\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ \frac{2}{1-z+y}\\
&\quad-\frac{\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ 2\\
&\quad+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ (1-z)\Big\}\;.\end{split}

(212)

We will want to evaluate this approximately for small
y
in such a way that if we expand the result in powers of
\alpha_{s}(yQ^{2})
we retain all terms proportional to
\alpha_{s}^{n}(yQ^{2})\log^{n}(y)
and
\alpha_{s}^{n}(yQ^{2})\log^{n-1}(y)
. After integrating over
\bar{\mu}^{2}=yQ^{2}
as in Eq. (
145
), this will give contributions
\alpha_{s}^{n}(Q^{2})\log^{n+1}(\nu)
and
\alpha_{s}^{n}(Q^{2})\log^{n}(\nu)
. These are the leading log (LL) and next-to-leading log (NLL) terms. In
\lambda(y)
, we neglect contributions proportional to fewer powers of
\log(y)
or to powers of
y
.

In order to carry out this approximate evaluation, we note first that we can use

\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(A\mu^{2})=\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})-\beta_{0}\log(A)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}(\mu^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{3})\;,

(213)

where
\beta_{0}=(11C_{\mathrm{A}}-2n_{\mathrm{f}})/(12\pi)
. Then we can omit the
\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)
factor in the argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in the second term in Eq. (
212
) and the
\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}
in the third term, since these terms do not have
1/(1-z+y)
singularities that could produce
\log(y)
factors after integration. In the first term, there is a
1/(1-z+y)
singularity. For this term, we need to keep the
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}
contribution in Eq. (
213
). After performing the
z
integration in the last two terms, this gives us

\begin{split}\lambda(y)\approx{}&4C_{\mathrm{F}}\int_{0}^{1}\!d(1-z)\ \frac{1}{1-z+y}\\
&\times\frac{\alpha_{s}((1-z)yQ^{2})-\beta_{0}\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}})\,\alpha_{s}^{2}((1-z)yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ \\
&\quad-3C_{\mathrm{F}}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ \;.\end{split}

(214)

Now we note that the
y
in the denominator in the first term of Eq. (
214
) places an effective lower cutoff on
(1-z)
at about
(1-z)=y
. This observation suggests that the integration over
(1-z)
can be written in a simpler form:

\displaystyle\lambda(y)\approx{}

\displaystyle 4C_{\mathrm{F}}\int_{y}^{1}\!\frac{d(1-z)}{1-z}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\alpha_{s}((1-z)yQ^{2})-\beta_{0}\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}})\,\alpha_{s}^{2}((1-z)yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\

\displaystyle\quad-3C_{\mathrm{F}}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ \;.

(215)

In fact, this correctly reproduces the
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{n}(y)
terms and the
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{n-1}(y)
terms in the expansion of the integral. To see this, one can approximately solve the renormalization group equation for
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in the form
SurguladzeSoper

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(A\mu^{2})}={}&\frac{1+\beta_{0}\log(A)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})}{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})}\\
&+\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{0}}\,\log\big(1+\beta_{0}\log(A)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})\big)\\
&+\cdots\;,\end{split}

(216)

with
\mu^{2}=yQ^{2}
and
A=1-z
. Here
\beta_{1}=(17C_{\mathrm{A}}^{2}-5C_{\mathrm{A}}n_{\mathrm{f}}-3C_{\mathrm{F}}n_{\mathrm{f}})/(24\pi^{2})
. This yields
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(A\mu^{2})
as a series

\begin{split}\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(A\mu^{2})={}&\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\mu^{2})\Bigg\{1+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(\mu^{2})\big[c_{n}\log^{n}(A)\\
&+d_{n}\log^{n-1}(A)+\cdots\big]\Bigg\}\;.\end{split}

(217)

Then one can check that the integral (
214
) agrees with the integral (
XIX
) at the NLL level.

The current code in
Deductor
does not include the
\beta_{1}
contributions in evaluating the
z
dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}((1-z)yQ^{2})
. This appears to be not particularly significant numerically, but it is significant in principle because it means that some of the NLL contributions to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
are absent.

We can now compare to Ref.
thrustsum
by changing the integration variable to
q^{2}=(1-z)yQ^{2}
:

\begin{split}\lambda(y)\approx{}&4C_{\mathrm{F}}\int_{y^{2}Q^{2}}^{yQ^{2}}\!\frac{dq^{2}}{q^{2}}\frac{\alpha_{s}(q^{2})-\beta_{0}\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}})\alpha_{s}^{2}(q^{2})}{2\pi}\ \\
&-3C_{\mathrm{F}}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(yQ^{2})}{2\pi}\ \;.\end{split}

(218)

This agrees with the result in Eq. (64) of Ref.
thrustsum
for the LL and NLL contributions to
\lambda(y)
.

We have been seeking a formula for the summation of logarithms of
\nu
in the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
of the thrust distribution. We use Eq. (
141
) for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
, choosing for
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
the state with a quark and an antiquark with opposite momenta. The operator
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
acting on this state is just 1. Then

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{Y}(Q^{2};\nu)\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(219)

We approximate
\mathcal{Y}(Q^{2};\nu)
, using Eq. (
145
), as the exponential of the integral of the first order generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}
, which we take from Eq. (
209
). This gives

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)\approx{}&\exp\!\left(-\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}/Q^{2}}^{1}\!\frac{dy}{y}\,(1-e^{-\nu y})\,\lambda(y)\right)\;.\end{split}

(220)

Here
\lambda(y)
can be either the exact function from
Deductor
, as in Eq. (
210
), or else the approximate function given in Eq. (
XIX
). The factor
(1-e^{-\nu y})
puts an effective lower cutoff on the
y
integration at
y=1/\nu
. Then a factor
\log^{n}(y)
in
\lambda(y)
produces a factor
\log^{n+1}(\nu)
in the exponent of Eq. (
220
).

We have seen that one can start with Eq. (
136
) for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by a parton shower and rearrange the operators to express
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in the form Eq. (
219
). Then approximating
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
in
\mathcal{Y}
gives us a candidate result (
220
) for the summation of logarithms of
\nu
in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
. We do note that the shower splitting functions contain ingredients related to the argument of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in the parton splitting function. These ingredients are somewhat
ad hoc
from the perspective of just representing the soft and collinear singularities of a single splitting. Their purpose was to build into the first order splitting functions some approximation to splitting functions beyond leading order so as to improve the effectiveness of a parton shower in summing large logarithms. We have seen the effect of these ingredients in giving us the standard summation of thrust logarithms at the NLL level.

Our analysis uses primarily the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
of the thrust distribution. One can take the inverse Laplace transform of
\tilde{g}(\nu)
to obtain the thrust distribution
g(\tau)
, Eq. (
125
), itself. The function
g(\tau)
is the derivative of
f(\tau)
, Eq. (
127
):

g(\tau)=\frac{df(\tau)}{d\tau}\;.

(221)

We can follow Ref.
thrustsum
to evaluate
f(\tau)
at NLL accuracy:

\begin{split}f(\tau)={}&\exp\!\left(-\frac{C_{\mathrm{F}}}{\pi\beta_{0}^{2}}\left\{\frac{f_{1}(\lambda)}{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})}+f_{2}(\lambda)\right\}\right)\\
&\times\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\gamma(\lambda))}\,\;.\end{split}

(222)

Here

\lambda=\beta_{0}\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})\log(1/\tau)\;,

(223)

the LL function
f_{1}(\lambda)
is

f_{1}(\lambda)=(1-2\lambda)\log(1-2\lambda)-2(1-\lambda)\log(1-\lambda)\;,

(224)

the NLL function
f_{2}(\lambda)
is

\begin{split}f_{2}(\lambda)={}&-\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{0}}\,2\log^{2}(1-\lambda)+\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{0}}\log^{2}(1-2\lambda)\\
&+2\beta_{0}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\log\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{1-2\lambda}\right)\\
&-\left(\frac{\beta_{1}}{\beta_{0}}+\beta_{0}\log(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}})\right)\log\!\left(\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{1-2\lambda}\right)\\
&+\frac{3\beta_{0}}{2}\log\!\left(1-\lambda\right)\;,\end{split}

(225)

where
\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}
is Euler’s constant, and the function
\gamma(\lambda)
is

\gamma(\lambda)=-\frac{2C_{\mathrm{F}}}{\pi\beta_{0}}\,\log\!\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{1-2\lambda}\right)\;.

(226)

The logarithm of
f(\tau)
contains LL contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})^{n}\log^{n+1}(1/\tau)
and NLL contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})^{n}\log^{n}(1/\tau)
, but contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})^{n}\log^{j}(1/\tau)
with
j<n
are dropped. Of course, a parton shower does not drop terms beyond NLL.

XX
Result from the parton shower

We have manipulated the operators used in a parton shower to produce a candidate formula (
220
) for the summation of logarithms for the thrust distribution. We have seen that this formula reproduces the known result
thrustsum
for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in QCD at the NLL level. We now ask what the result for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
is in a first order parton shower that uses the
Deductor
algorithm or another algorithm of interest. That is, what do we get from Eqs. (
136
) and (
104
),

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{O}(\nu)\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\!\right)\!\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big),\end{split}

(227)

when the shower generator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
represents a first order shower? This must be the same as the result of using Eq. (
145
) in Eq. (
141
),

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\frac{1}{\sigma_{\textsc{h}}}\big({1}\big|\mathbb{T}\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\right)\mathcal{O}(\nu)\big|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(228)

Here we take
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
to be the initial
q\bar{q}
state in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation (with massless quarks). Then there is some simplification because
\mathcal{O}(\nu)|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})=|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
. There is a more significant simplification because
|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
. We use Eq. (
177
), Eq. (
178
), and
({1}|{\rho_{\textsc{h}}})=\sigma_{\textsc{h}}
to give

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\exp\!\left(\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\right)\;.\end{split}

(229)

Here
|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})
is a color singlet
q\bar{q}
basis state with
p_{1}+p_{2}=Q
. The results are independent of the direction of
\vec{p}_{1}=-\vec{p}_{2}
and independent of the quark flavor
f_{1}=-f_{2}
. There is only one possible color state. The basis state is normalized to
({1}|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})=1

NSI
.

We use the operator
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
defined in Eq. (
146
),

\mathcal{I}(\nu)=\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)\;,

(230)

to write Eq. (
229
) as

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\exp\!\left[\big({1}\big|\mathcal{I}(\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\right]\;.\end{split}

(231)

In Eq. (
231
),
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
is obtained from just
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
, not from any higher order splitting functions that might be present in a higher order shower algorithm. The result for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in Eq. (
231
) could be very different from
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by Eq. (
220
) because a first order parton shower is not the same as full QCD.

Using Eq. (
169
), we expand
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
as a series of terms
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
, where
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
contains
k
powers of the shower splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
. Thus
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
contains
k
powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
evaluated at a running scale inside the integrations that give
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
. We can expand
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
evaluated at a fixed scale. A convenient choice
9
9

9

In Sec.
XIX
, we used
\mu_{\mathrm{fixed}}^{2}=Q^{2}
. Using Eq. (
216
), one can transform between expansions
\sum c(n,j)\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
and
\sum c^{\prime}(n,j)\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2})\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq n
in each case, so both choices of
\mu_{\mathrm{fixed}}^{2}
work equally well in an analytical treatment. In a numerical evaluation,
\mu_{\mathrm{fixed}}^{2}=Q^{2}/\nu
has the advantage that this scale is closer to the running scale at which
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
is evaluated inside the integrals for
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
.
is
\mu_{\mathrm{fixed}}^{2}=Q^{2}/\nu
. Thus we write

\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)\;.

(232)

In
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{k}(\nu)
there are
k
integrations over scale variables
y
and
k
integrations over momentum fractions
z
, so
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{k}(\nu)
could contain
2k
factors of
\log(\nu)
. Changing the scale in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
can produce one more factor
\log(\nu)
for each factor
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
, so that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
could contain
n+k
factors of
\log(\nu)
. However the exponent in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in Eq. (
220
) contains only contributions proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq n+1
. Thus a minimal expectation for the parton shower is that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
contains only
j
factors of
\log(\nu)
with
j\leq n+1
. If this is the case, we can say that the
\log(\nu)
factors
exponentiate
.

If we expand the QCD result for the exponent in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by Eq. (
220
) in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, the coefficients of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n+1}(\nu)
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n}(\nu)
take particular values. These values are generated by
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
using
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
with its argument suitably specified by the shower algorithm. Thus for
k\geq 2
,
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
must not contain a factor
\log^{n+1}(\nu)
if we are to maintain the logarithmic summation at LL level and additionally must not contain a factor
\log^{n}(\nu)
if we are to maintain the logarithmic summation at NLL level.

We investigate how many powers of
\log(\nu)
are contained in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
in the following two sections.

XXI
Parton shower at leading log

In this section we examine the operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
with the aim of discovering the behavior of
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by a leading order parton shower using the
\Lambda
-ordered
Deductor
algorithm with exact QCD color. The Laplace transform of
\tilde{g}(\nu)
can be represented according to Eq. (
231
) in terms of the integral
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
defined in Eq. (
146
). We write the definition in the form

\mathcal{I}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\nu}\!\frac{dx}{x}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\;.

(233)

Here we have defined a standard scale
Q^{2}/\nu
and a scale variable
x
that gives the ratio of
\mu^{2}
to this standard scale:
\mu^{2}=xQ^{2}/\nu
. If we expand the exponential (not just the exponent) in Eq. (
231
) in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, we will find terms proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq 2n
.

The simplest expectation would be that
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
also has an expansion with terms
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq 2n
. Such a representation would not be very useful, even if we knew all of the coefficients for
j=2n
. It is much more useful if there are nonzero contributions
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
only for
j\leq n+1
and we knew the coefficients for terms with
j=n+1
. We then call the
j=n+1
terms the leading log, LL, terms.

In the notation of this paper, the operator
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
is proportional to one power of the shower splitting operator and thus to one power of a running
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
rather than the fixed
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. As we have seen, this operator generates a whole LL series
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j=n+1
. We may hope that this is all that survives at the LL level. That is, we may hope that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
for
k\geq 2
generates only terms
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j\leq n
. If so, we will say that
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by the leading order parton shower exponentiates at the LL level.

In this section, we demonstrate that
\tilde{g}(\nu)
does exponentiate at the LL level in this sense. In the following section, we will turn our attention to the NLL level.

We will need a small preliminary analysis. We see from Eq. (
175
) that for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
we will need
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}
.

For
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
, we briefly repeat the derivation that gave us Eq. (
208
). We use
Eq. (
107
), then Eq. (
181
), then Eqs. (
184
) and (
207
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\hskip-85.35826pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)-\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}-\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x})\;.

(234)

For
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{\mu}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}
, we need a somewhat different argument. We use Eqs. (
176
) and (
107
). Then we note that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
according to Eq. (
162
) because
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
leaves the parton momenta and flavors unchanged. Then we use Eqs. (
184
) and (
207
). Finally, we use the definition (
176
) again. This gives

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}\hskip-108.12054pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\Big\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\Big\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big\{\sum_{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}

\displaystyle-\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}\Big\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}\;.

(235)

Now we can start with
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
at first order. Eq. (
208
) gives us the result on the right hand side of Eq. (
XXI
):

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(x&Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\\
\approx{}&-\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x})\;.\end{split}

(236)

Recall that the eigenvalue
\xi_{l}
of
\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}
, given by Eq. (
204
), is of order 1. We will also need
\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
. When we substitute Eq. (
236
) into Eq. (
174
), we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}&(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\\
={}&-\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\ \sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}\bar{x}})\;.\end{split}

(237)

Here, and in the remainder of this section, we set the infrared cutoff
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
to zero. We notice that the factor
(1-e^{-\xi_{l}\bar{x}})
is small for
\bar{x}\ll 1
and approaches zero like
\bar{x}
when
\bar{x}\to 0
. This provides an infrared cutoff for the
\bar{x}
integration.

Now look at
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
at second order. We use Eq. (
175
):

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(&xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\\
={}&\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&\quad\quad\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\,\mathcal{S}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\,\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(238)

With the results (
XXI
) and (
XXI
), we obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(&xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\\
={}&-\sum_{\bar{l},l}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{\bar{l}}^{[1,0]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{\bar{l}}^{\mathrm{op}}\bar{x}})\\
&\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(239)

We integrate this to form the contribution to
\mathcal{I}
, Eq. (
146
), with two powers of
\mathcal{S}
:

\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{\nu}\!\frac{dx}{x}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\;.

(240)

There are potentially two
\log(\nu)
factors from the
z
integrations inside the two factors of
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}
. After expanding the running couplings in
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}
, at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there could be a total of
n
factors of
\log(\nu)
. Then we integrate over
x
and
\bar{x}
. This could produce two more factors of
\log(\nu)
, giving
\log^{n+2}(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. But what happens in the
x
and
\bar{x}
integrations that we find based on Eq. (
239
)? If
\bar{x}\ll 1
, the factor
(1-e^{-\xi_{\bar{l}}\bar{x}})
is small, so that the
\bar{x}
integration is effectively limited to the range
1\lesssim\bar{x}
. If
1\ll x
, the factor
e^{-\xi_{\bar{l}}x}
is small, so that the
x
integration is effectively limited to the range
x\lesssim 1
. We also have
\bar{x}<x
. Thus the net effective integration range is
1\lesssim\bar{x}<x\lesssim 1
. This leaves only
\bar{x}\sim x\sim 1
. There are no
\log(\nu)
factors from the
\bar{x}
and
x
integrations.

A contribution to
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}
proportional to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n+1}(\nu)
can be designated leading log. The result (
239
) shows that there is no LL contribution to
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}
. Rather, the LL contributions to the integral
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
come from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}
after we account for the argument of the strong coupling
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}
, Eq. (
218
). This leaves the possibility of a NLL,
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n}(\nu)
, contribution to
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}
. We will investigate the NLL contribution in the following section by looking at the
z
integrations in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}
.

We will also need some qualitative information about the behavior of
\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}
. From Eq. (
171
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\ \Big\{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)

(241)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\Big\}\;.

Using Eqs. (
239
), (
236
), and (
237
),

\begin{split}\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}(x&Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\\
={}&\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\\
&\times\Big\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(x_{2}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{2}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{2}})\\
&\quad\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}\,e^{-\xi_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(x_{2}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{2}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{2}})\\
&\quad\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}})\Big\}\;.\end{split}

(242)

In both terms we have a factor
(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{2}}x_{2}})
so there is an effective integration range
1\lesssim x_{2}<x_{1}<x
. This implies that
\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\to 0
for
x\ll 1
. In the first term, there is a factor
e^{-\xi_{l_{1}}x_{1}}
, so that the integrand is small for
1\ll x_{1}
. However the second term contains no such factor. The operators
\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)
and
\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(x_{2}Q^{2}/\nu)
can give us logarithms of their arguments. For this reason,
\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
can grow slowly, like a power of
\log(x)
, for
1\ll x
.

If we take
x=1
in Eq. (
242
), the effective integration range for
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
is
1\lesssim x_{2}<x_{1}<1
. Thus
x_{2}\sim x_{1}\sim 1
. Then there are no factors of
\log(\nu)
produced by the integrations over
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. Each factor of
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)
contains one factor of
\log(\nu)
. Thus
\mathcal{Y}^{[2]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
contains at most 2 factors of
\log(\nu)
.

We can generalize these observations to suggest induction hypotheses for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}
for
k\geq 2
:

1.

The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
is suppressed by a factor
x
times logarithms for
x\to 0
and by an exponential
e^{-cx}
times logarithms for
x\to\infty
. Its only unsuppressed region is for
x\sim 1
.

2.

The operator
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
is suppressed by a factor
x
times logarithms for
x\to 0
and grows at most logarithmically for
x\to\infty
.

3.

The operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
each contain at most
k
factors of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k}(Q^{2}/\nu)
.

In property 3, we note that the operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
contain higher powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
that arise from expanding the running couplings in their definitions in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. This expansion can yield one more power of
\log(\nu)
per power of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. Thus there are at most
n
powers of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
.

We have found that these properties hold at order
k=2
. We now establish that they hold for any larger order by assuming that they hold at order
k
and showing that they hold at order
k+1
.

Begin with
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}
. From Eq. (
170
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-48.36958pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle\times\big\{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\mathcal{S}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\mathcal{O}(\nu)-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\big\}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=2}^{k-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(243)

We use Eq. (
172
) to simplify the first term and Eqs. (
174
) and (
172
) to simplify the last term:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-48.36958pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\mathcal{S}(xQ^{2}/\nu)\mathcal{O}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=2}^{k-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bigg[}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{O}(\nu)\mathcal{S}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu)\mathcal{O}(\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bigg]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.
