(244)

Now we can use Eq. (
XXI
) in the first term and Eq. (
XXI
) in the last term, giving us

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-48.36958pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=2}^{k-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bigg[}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}\bar{x}})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bigg]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

(245)

In the first term, property 2 for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
implies that this term is unsuppressed only for
1\lesssim x
, while the factor
\exp(-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x)
implies that this term is unsuppressed only for
x\lesssim 1
. Thus this term is unsuppressed only for
x\sim 1
. In the second term, property 1 for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
implies that this term is unsuppressed only for
x\sim 1
. In the third term, property 1 for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
implies that this term is unsuppressed only for
x\sim 1
. This gives us property 1 for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
.

Now set
x=1
in Eq. (
XXI
). There is an integration over
\bar{x}
in the third term, but, accounting for the factor
[1-\exp(-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}\bar{x})]
, the integration region is
1\lesssim\bar{x}<1
. That is,
\bar{x}\sim 1
. We can then use property 3 for the operators that appear in order to count the maximum possible number of factors of
\log(\nu)
in each term. At order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}
, this gives the maximum number of factors of
\log(\nu)
as
k+1
, thus verifying property 3 for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
.

Now we examine
\mathcal{Y}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
. We use Eq. (
171
) to write for
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}

\displaystyle(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\ \mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=2}^{k}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{d\bar{x}}{\bar{x}}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(\bar{x}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\;.

(246)

We use Eq. (
172
) and (
XXI
) to simplify the first term:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-51.21504pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{l}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\ \mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=2}^{k}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\ \mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\ \mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\;.

(247)

In each term, condition 2 for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
or
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
or condition 1 for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
implies that the integrand of the
x_{1}
integration is unsuppressed only for
1\lesssim x_{1}
. Since
x_{1}<x
,
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
is unsuppressed only for
1\lesssim x
. This establishes property 2 for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
.

Now set
x=1
in Eq. (
XXI
). There is an integration over
x_{1}
in each term, but the integration region is
1\lesssim x_{1}<1
. We can then use property 3 for the operators that appear in order to count the maximum possible number of factors of
\log(\nu)
in each term. At order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, this gives the maximum number of factors of
\log(\nu)
as
k+1
, thus verifying property 3 for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
.

We call the properties 1,2, and 3 above the
LL exponentiation property
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, as discussed at the start of this section. In the following section we analyze the NLL contributions to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
.

XXII
Parton shower at next-to-leading log

We have seen that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
has the proper perturbative structure to allow
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as given by a leading order parton shower using the
\Lambda
-ordered
Deductor
algorithm to exponentiate correctly at the leading log level.

First, the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, constructed from one power of the shower splitting operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
has the right structure to reproduce the known QCD result
thrustsum
at LL accuracy and even at NLL accuracy, provided that the argument the running coupling
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
is properly defined. For
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
, we can state this in terms of an expansion in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. We consider the integral
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
defined in Eq. (
233
). When the running
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
in
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
is expanded in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, the coefficients of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n+1}(\nu)
, that is the LL coefficients, are correct and the coefficients of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n}(\nu)
, the NLL coefficients, are also correct.

Second, each of the operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
for
k\geq 2
has the right structure so that in the integral
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
, the coefficient of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n+1}(\nu)
, which contributes to the exponent in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
at LL accuracy, vanish. That is, the coefficient
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
contains at most
n
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

This LL exponentiation property arises from two features of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
. First,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
is suppressed for
\mu^{2}\gg Q^{2}/\nu
and for
\mu^{2}\ll Q^{2}/\nu
, so that only the integration region
\mu^{2}\sim Q^{2}/\nu
contributes to
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
and no factor of
\log(\nu)
arises from integrating over
\mu^{2}
from
Q^{2}/\nu
to
Q^{2}
. Second,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
contains at most
n
factors of
\log(\nu)
.

Now, if the coefficients of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)\log^{n}(\nu)
in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
were to vanish for
k\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
would not contribute to
\tilde{g}(\nu)
at NLL level. Then the only NLL contributions to
\tilde{g}(\nu)
would come from the expansion of the running coupling in
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
. Since these contributions match the known QCD result
thrustsum
, we would conclude that the first order parton shower according to the
Deductor
algorithm generates the known QCD result at NLL accuracy.

Remarkably, this is the case: in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
for
k\geq 2
the coefficients
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
contain at most
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
for large
\nu
. The proof of this result, with exact color, is somewhat involved, so we present it in Appendix
A
.

XXIII
Numerical behavior of
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)

We have considered analytically the coefficient
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
of
[\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)/(2\pi)]^{n}
in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
, Eq. (
230
). We have seen analytically in Secs.
XXI
and
XXII
and in Appendix
A
that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
for
k\geq 2
contains no more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
for large
\nu
.

The first nontrivial example of this is that
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
, when calculated at large
\log(\nu)
, is proportional to
\log(\nu)
plus a constant but has no
\log^{2}(\nu)
contribution. Similarly,
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
has at most a
\log^{2}(\nu)
contribution at large
\nu
. We can check these results numerically.

We define the second order term in the exponent in
\tilde{g}(\nu)
, Eq. (
231
):

\big\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)\big\rangle=\int_{0}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\mu^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\,\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(\mu^{2};\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\;.

(248)

We expand
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)\rangle
in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)/(2\pi)
and calculate numerically the first two coefficients,
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
,

\begin{split}\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)\rangle={}&\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle\left(\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\\
&+\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle\left(\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\right)^{3}+\cdots\;.\end{split}

(249)

The state
|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})
in Eq. (
248
) is a color singlet, flavor singlet,
q\bar{q}
state with
p_{1}+p_{2}=Q
. The results are the same with any quark flavor choice and there is only one possible color state. The state is normalized to
({1}|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})=1
. The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
is calculated using the exact
Deductor
splitting functions according to Eq. (
175
). We use the exact definition of thrust to calculate
\tau
in
\mathcal{O}(\nu)
, Eq. (
137
). The calculation is performed with full color, not just leading color or the LC+ approximation. The integrals over scale in
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{n}(\nu)\rangle
are infrared convergent so there is no need to impose a lower cutoff on the shower scale
\mu^{2}
. Then the coefficients
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{n}(\nu)\rangle
are independent of
Q^{2}
.

We plot
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
versus
\log(\nu)
as the solid red curve in Fig.
1
. We first note that
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
is small. For instance,
\log(\nu)=8
corresponds roughly to
\tau=e^{-8}\approx 3\times 10^{-4}
in the thrust distribution. For
\log(\nu)<8
, we find
|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|\lesssim 1
. Then if we take
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\approx 0.1
, we have
[\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}/(2\pi)]^{2}|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|\lesssim 0.0003
. The function
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)
appears in the exponent of the Laplace transform of the thrust distribution, but for such a small value of
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)
, one would not have needed to exponentiate it.

Our primary concern is the behavior of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
for very large
\log(\nu)
.
10
10

10

The function
\tau(\{p\}_{m})
is a complicated function of the parton momenta. Evaluation of this function becomes numerically unstable for parton states
\{p\}_{m}
that give very small
\tau
. For this reason, in this and later figures, we limit
\log(\nu)
to
\log(\nu)<16
, although in some cases the numerical results appear to be reliable for larger values of
\log(\nu)
.
Our analytical results indicate that
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
should be a straight line for large
\log(\nu)
. The numerical result supports this conclusion. We also evaluate the integrand for
d\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle/d\log(\nu)
analytically and then integrate this expression numerically and display the result as the dashed blue curve in Fig.
1
. The analytical result implies that
d\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle/d\log(\nu)
should approach a constant for large
\log(\nu)
and the numerical result supports this conclusion.

In our analysis, we argued that
\hat{\tau}-\tau=y
should be a good approximation in the second splitting for the purpose of determining how many powers of
\log(\nu)
can appear in
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
. We tried calculating
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
with this approximation. The result is shown as the dotted red line in Fig.
1
. This curve is, as expected, a straight line for large
\log(\nu)
and has the same slope as the curve for the exact
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
. We were a bit surprised to find that
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
with the exact
\hat{\tau}-\tau
differs by a noticeable amount from the result with the approximate thrust value. The difference is in the direction of making
|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|
smaller. We do not have an analytical explanation for this behavior.

if we take

. Our analytical results indicate that for large
\nu

in
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
should be

. This implies that for large

should be
\log^{1}(\nu)
. The numerical result, graphed as the dashed blue line in Fig.

, supports this conclusion.

XXIV
Numerical behavior of the thrust distribution

We have seen that the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mu^{2};\nu)
directly generates the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
of the thrust distribution according to Eq. (
231
). The first order term
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
in this operator is obtained from the shower splitting function for a first order
\Lambda
-ordered parton shower. We have further seen that this term generates the known
thrustsum
summation of logarithms of
\tau
at the NLL level as long as the shower splitting function is suitably defined. Furthermore, the higher order terms
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
obtained from this first order shower splitting function generate only contributions beyond the NLL level.

According to Eq. (
227
), the same result for
\tilde{g}(\nu)
as in Eq. (
231
) is obtained by running the
\Lambda
-ordered shower and measuring the Laplace transform of the thrust distribution. However, we do not need to take the Laplace transform. We can simply run the
\Lambda
-ordered shower and measure the thrust distribution
g(\tau)
, as in Eq. (
134
). Will this give the same result as the NLL analytical result listed in Eqs. (
221
) and (
222
)?

In this section, we try this experiment. It is not useful to set
Q^{2}=M_{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}
, which would be relevant for LEP (Large Electron Positron) experiments because a parton shower needs an infrared cutoff. We can take the cutoffs on allowed shower splittings to be
\Lambda>1\ \mathrm{GeV}
and
k_{\mathrm{T}}>1\ \mathrm{GeV}
, but then there is not much range between
(1\ \mathrm{GeV})^{2}
and the starting scale
Q^{2}
of the shower. The result is that there is not a wide range in
\tau
in which we can examine the dependence of
g(\tau)
on
\log(1/\tau)
free of the effects of the infrared cutoffs. Instead, we retain
(1\ \mathrm{GeV})^{2}
cutoffs but set
Q^{2}=(10\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
. We then run the
\Lambda
-ordered
Deductor
shower with the LC+ approximation for color
NScolor
. We turn off the top quark, so that the shower is based on 5-flavor QCD.

We compare
\tau g(\tau)
according to
Deductor
with
\tau g(\tau)
according to the NLL formula, Eqs. (
221
) and (
222
), in Fig.
3
. We see that the
Deductor
curve is a bit higher than the NLL curve around
\tau=0.01
and a bit lower at the smallest values of
\tau
. Generally, the results agree to within about 0.01.

Do these results agree within the expected errors?

•

The
Deductor
shower produces contributions beyond the NLL level. If we look at
\tau=0.01
so that
\log(1/\tau)=4.6
, NNLL terms lack a factor 4.6 compared to NLL terms. A simple calculation shows that the NLL terms contribute approximately
-0.03
to
\tau g(\tau)
at
\tau=0.01
. Thus we might expect that the NNLL terms in
Deductor
would contribute
\pm 0.03/4.6\approx\pm 0.007
to
\tau g(\tau)
. This gives us an error estimate from terms in
Deductor
beyond NLL of
\pm 0.007
.

•

There are typically about 20 parton splittings between the 10 TeV scale at which the shower starts and the 1 GeV scale at which it ends. We cannot be confident that there are not 0.1% errors for each splitting resulting from approximations within the
Deductor
code, so we cannot rule out a 2% systematic error in
g(\tau)
resulting from these approximations. A 2% error on the value
\tau g(\tau)\approx 0.2
at
\tau=0.01
amounts to an error of
\pm 0.004
in
\tau g(\tau)
.

•

The infrared cutoffs have some effect. The most important effect comes from the limit on the transverse momentum in a splitting, which we set to
k_{\mathrm{T}}>1\ \mathrm{GeV}
. To test for sensitivity to this cutoff, we change the cut to
k_{\mathrm{T}}>3\ \mathrm{GeV}
. In the range
0.0005<\tau<0.2
, we find that this change in cutoff produces a change in
\tau g(\tau)
that is generally smaller than 0.003. Thus we estimate an error of
\pm 0.003
in
\tau g(\tau)
due to the influence of the infrared cutoff.

•

The
Deductor
splitting kernel omits the
\beta_{1}
term in Eq. (
216
) for evaluating the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}((1-z)yQ^{2})
on
(1-z)
. This changes the
Deductor
result at the NLL level. We examine this effect below.

•

The LC+ approximation used by default in
Deductor
is not the same as exact color. This can introduce spurious terms of order
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
times logarithms of
1/\tau
into the LC+
Deductor
result, where
N_{\mathrm{c}}=3
is the number of colors. We examine this effect below.

We examine the effects of missing NLL terms and of color in Fig.
4
. Here the NLL curve is copied from Fig.
3
and the
Deductor
curve from Fig.
3
is displayed as a dashed (black) line. The remaining two curves are modified versions of the curves in Fig.
3
.

We first address the fact that
Deductor
omits the
\beta_{1}
term for evaluating the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}((1-z)yQ^{2})
on
(1-z)
. This means that in the summation of logarithms of
\log(1/\tau)
,
Deductor
is missing the term
-(\beta_{1}/\beta_{0})\log((1-\lambda^{2})/(1-2\lambda))
in
f_{2}(\lambda)
in Eq. (
225
). In order to see the effect of this term, we calculate the ratio

r(\tau)=\frac{g_{\mathrm{NLL}}(\tau)}{g_{\mathrm{NLL}}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\tau)}\;,

(250)

where
g^{\mathrm{mod}}_{\mathrm{NLL}}(\tau)
is obtained by omitting the term
-(\beta_{1}/\beta_{0})\log((1-\lambda^{2})/(1-2\lambda))
in the calculation of
g(\tau)
. Then we correct the
Deductor
result for
g(\tau)
by multiplying it by
r(\tau)
. We plot the corrected
Deductor
curve in Fig.
4
. We see that the corrected
Deductor
curve is quite close to the uncorrected curve. However the difference is visible in Fig.
4
and acts in the direction of reducing the discrepancy between the analytical summation of logarithms and the numerical
Deductor
result.
11
11

11

In a future version of
Deductor
, we may add this contribution to the splitting kernel, although its practical effect is quite small.

We next address the fact that in Fig.
3
we used the default color approximation in
Deductor
, the LC+ approximation
NScolor
. This approximation is an improvement over the leading color approximation, but it is far from being exact. In the LC+ approximation, we replace the exact first order splitting function
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})
by an approximate version
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})+\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})
.
Deductor
has the option of expanding in powers of
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})
and keeping terms up to and including
[\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})]^{n}
, where
n
can be chosen by the user
NSNewColor
. In order to assess what difference a more exact treatment of color could make, we plot in Fig.
4
the result of calculating the thrust distribution at 10 TeV with
n=2
for those splittings that have
\Lambda>10\ \mathrm{GeV}
. We have corrected this result using the factor
r(\tau)
from Eq. (
250
). Of course, using
n=2
slows the calculation down, increasing the statistical errors. Within the statistical errors, we find that improving the color treatment makes no difference.

In summary, we have made a numerical comparison of the expected NLL result for the thrust distribution and a direct calculation using a
\Lambda
-ordered parton shower with a global momentum mapping, setting
Q^{2}
to
(10\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
so as to allow
\log(1/\tau)
to be adequately large to provide a real test. We have found good agreement within the estimated errors.

XXV
Color and logarithms

Our analysis in this paper mostly does not examine the effect of approximating color on the summation of logarithms in the thrust distribution. For instance, the analytical analysis in Secs.
XXI
and
XXII
and the numerical results in Figs.
1
and
2
use exact QCD color. The
Deductor
result in Fig.
3
is calculated with the LC+ approximation, which is a numerically very good color approximation for the thrust distribution, as seen in Fig.
4
. It would certainly be of interest to examine analytically or numerically as in Figs.
1
and
2
whether the use of the LC+ approximation preserves the NLL accuracy of a parton shower that has NLL accuracy with exact color. Such a study is beyond the scope of this paper. However, we provide some brief comments on color approximations in this section.

Typically, parton shower event generators use the LC approximation
Herwig
;
Pythia
;
Sherpa
. In the simplest formulation, the LC approximation is obtained by using U(3) as the color group and then dropping all terms suppressed by a factor
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
. Then at every step of the shower the color state
\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}
has
\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}=\{c\}_{m}
and there is a factor
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
for every gluon emission vertex. At some places, one can include a
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
correction by changing
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
to
C_{\mathrm{F}}=(C_{\mathrm{A}}/2)(1-1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2})
.

Deductor
uses the LC+ approximation
NScolor
. The generator
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})
of splittings with the LC+ approximation differs from the generator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
with full color.
12
12

12

The LC+ approximation, including the calculation of the overlap
\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle
at the end of each event, is computationally efficient. The code for
\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big|{\{c\}_{m}}\big\rangle
is available in the
Deductor
code at

http://www.desy.de/
\sim
znagy/deductor/

and

http://pages.uoregon.edu/soper/deductor/
.

Define

\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})=\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})-\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{LC+}}(\mu^{2})\;.

(251)

For the first splitting from the initial
q\bar{q}
state in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation, the LC+ approximation is exact in color

\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)=0\;.

(252)

Furthermore, as noted in Sec.
XI
, for later splittings the difference
\Delta\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
is singular only for fixed angle soft splittings but not for collinear splittings or soft
\times
collinear splittings
NScolor
.

We can illustrate this with an example adapted from a 1993 paper
Gustafson1993
by Gustafson that sorted out the
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
versus
C_{\mathrm{F}}
choice for
q\mathrm{g}\mathrm{g}\bar{q}
production in electron-positron annihilation. Let us start with a state containing a quark with momentum
p_{1}
, an antiquark with momentum
p_{2}
and a gluon with momentum
p_{3}
. Now we can emit the fourth, soft, gluon with momentum
\hat{p}_{4}
. The other partons have momenta
\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3}
after the splitting. We take
\hat{p}_{4}
to be very small, so that we can neglect recoil and take
\hat{p}_{i}=p_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. We denote the energy of
\hat{p}_{i}
in the rest frame of the total momentum
Q
by
E_{i}
and we denote the angles between
\hat{p}_{i}
and
\hat{p}_{j}
in the rest frame of
Q
by
\theta_{ij}
. We assume that
\theta_{12}
is not small since
\theta_{12}\approx\pi
most probably and
\theta_{12}\ll 1
is not probable. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\theta_{13}<\theta_{23}
. We consider both the possibility that
\theta_{13}
is of order 1 and the possibility that
\theta_{13}\ll 1
.

For the emission of gluon 4 use just the dipole approximation for soft gluon emissions. With this approximation, the emission probability is

\Phi_{lk}=A^{\prime}_{lk}\,\overline{w}_{lk}^{\mathrm{dipole}}\;.

(253)

Here
\overline{w}_{lk}^{\mathrm{dipole}}
is the familiar probability density for emitting a soft gluon with index 4 from parton
l
with interference from emitting the same soft gluon from a different parton
k
,

\overline{w}_{lk}^{\mathrm{dipole}}=4\pi\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\,\frac{2\hat{p}_{k}\cdot\hat{p}_{l}}{\hat{p}_{4}\cdot\hat{p}_{k}\,\hat{p}_{4}\cdot\hat{p}_{l}}\;.

(254)

This probability density is symmetric under interchange of
l
with
k
. In a partitioned dipole shower like
Deductor
, as distinct from an antenna dipole shower line
Vincia

Vincia
, we distinguish the emitting parton
l
from the dipole partner parton
k
by multiplying by a partitioning function
A^{\prime}_{lk}
with
A^{\prime}_{lk}+A^{\prime}_{kl}=1
.
Deductor
uses

A^{\prime}_{lk}(\{\hat{p}\}_{{m\!+\!1}})=\frac{\hat{p}_{4}\cdot\hat{p}_{k}\ \hat{p}_{l}\cdot Q}{\hat{p}_{4}\cdot\hat{p}_{k}\ \hat{p}_{l}\cdot Q+\hat{p}_{4}\cdot\hat{p}_{l}\ \hat{p}_{k}\cdot Q}\;,

(255)

where
Q
is the total momentum of the final state.

The emission probability is accompanied by an operator on the partonic color state. Let us call the color state after the emission

C_{lk}=\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{4}}\big|{\{c\}_{4}}\big\rangle\,g_{lk}(\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{4})\,\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{4}}\big|\;.

(256)

Here
\big|{\{c\}_{4}}\big\rangle
are color basis states for
q\mathrm{g}\mathrm{g}\bar{q}
states in the “trace” or “string” basis used in
Deductor

NSI
. In the notation of Ref.
NSI
, we will need basis states
[1,i_{3},i_{4},2]
for a state with a quark with index
1
and an antiquark with index
2
joined by a color string with gluons
i_{3},i_{4}
along the string. The probability associated with this color state is then

\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{lk}=\sum_{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{4}}\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{4}}\big|{\{c\}_{4}}\big\rangle\,g_{lk}(\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{4})\;.

(257)

The color state of the starting
q\mathrm{g}\bar{q}
state, in the notation used for
q\mathrm{g}\mathrm{g}\bar{q}
states, is

\begin{split}\big|{\{c\}_{3}}\big\rangle={}&\big|{[1,3,2]}\big\rangle\;,\\
\big\langle{\{c^{\prime}\}_{3}}\big|={}&\big\langle{[1,3,2]}\big|\;.\end{split}

(258)

After the emission of the soft gluon, the statistical state is proportional to

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{31}={}

\displaystyle\left(\,\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle-\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\right)\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|

\displaystyle+\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\left(\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|-\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\right),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{32}={}

\displaystyle\left(\,\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle-\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\right)\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|

\displaystyle+\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\left(\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|-\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\right),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{13}={}

\displaystyle\left(\,\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle-\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\right)\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|

\displaystyle+\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\left(\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|-\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\right),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{23}={}

\displaystyle\left(\,\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle-\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\right)\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|

\displaystyle\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\left(\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|-\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\right),

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{12}={}

\displaystyle\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|

\displaystyle+\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\;,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{C_{\mathrm{F}}}\,C_{21}={}

\displaystyle\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|

\displaystyle+\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\;.

(259)

The trace of these states (using the normalization conventions of Ref.
NSI
) is

\begin{split}\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{31}={}&\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{32}=\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{13}=\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{23}=T_{\parallel}-T_{\times}\;,\\
\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{12}={}&\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{21}=T_{\times}\;,\end{split}

(260)

where

\begin{split}T_{\parallel}={}&C_{\mathrm{F}}\;,\\
T_{\times}={}&-\frac{1}{2N_{\mathrm{c}}}\;.\end{split}

(261)

This is with full color. We can use the LC+ approximation, which is a very simple approximation on the color operators
NScolor
. Let us define the difference between the color states obtained with full color and the color states obtained with the LC+ approximation:

\Delta C_{lk}=C_{lk}-C_{lk}^{\mathrm{LC+}}\;.

(262)

Then the LC+ approximation
NScolor
gives

\begin{split}\Delta C_{31}={}&0\;,\\
\Delta C_{32}={}&0\;,\\
\Delta C_{13}={}&-(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\\
&-(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\;,\\
\Delta C_{23}={}&-(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\\
&-(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\;,\\
\Delta C_{12}={}&(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\\
&+(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\;,\\
\Delta C_{21}={}&(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,3,4,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,4,3,2]}\big|\\
&+(C_{\mathrm{F}}/2)\big|{[1,4,3,2]}\big\rangle\big\langle{[1,3,4,2]}\big|\;.\end{split}

(263)

The traces of these states are

\begin{split}\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{31}={}&\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{32}=0\;,\\
\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{13}={}&\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{23}=-T_{\times}\;,\\
\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{12}={}&\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{21}=T_{\times}\;.\end{split}

(264)

This shows us what the LC+ approximation leaves out in a shower that has just two soft gluon emissions:

\begin{split}\sum_{l}\sum_{k\neq l}\Phi_{lk}&\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{lk}\\
={}&(\Phi_{12}-\Phi_{13})T_{\times}+(\Phi_{21}-\Phi_{23})T_{\times}\;.\end{split}

(265)

We note first that this is color suppressed compared to the result using full color,
C_{lk}
, since a factor
1/(2N_{\mathrm{c}})
replaces a factor
C_{\mathrm{F}}
. Second, we see immediately from Eqs. (
253
), (
254
), and (
255
) that
(\Phi_{12}-\Phi_{13})
is not singular when
\hat{p}_{4}
becomes collinear with
\hat{p}_{1}
,
\hat{p}_{3}
, or
\hat{p}_{2}
. Similarly
(\Phi_{21}-\Phi_{23})
has no collinear singularities. These functions still have a soft singularity: they have a
1/E_{4}^{2}
singularity when
E_{4}\to 0
at a fixed
\theta_{14}
,
\theta_{24}
, and
\theta_{34}
.

Although
E_{4}^{2}\sum\Phi_{lk}\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{lk}
is never singular, it can be large in certain angular regions. Consider the case that
\theta_{13}\ll 1
. Then
E_{4}^{2}(\Phi_{21}-\Phi_{23})
is never large. Additionally,
E_{4}^{2}(\Phi_{12}-\Phi_{13})
is not large when
\theta_{14}\ll\theta_{13}
because
\Phi_{13}
cancels
\Phi_{12}
in this limit. However, when
\theta_{13}\ll\theta_{14}
,
E_{4}^{2}\,\Phi_{13}
is small and
E_{4}^{2}\,\Phi_{12}\propto 1/\theta_{14}^{2}
is large when
\theta_{13}\ll\theta_{14}\ll 1
. Thus we can approximate

\sum_{l}\sum_{k\neq l}\Phi_{lk}\mathrm{Tr}\,\Delta C_{lk}\approx{}\theta(\theta_{13}<\theta_{14})\Phi_{12}\,T_{\times}\;.

(266)

This is a good approximation when
\theta_{13}\ll 1
and works also when
\theta_{13}
is not small since then
\theta(\theta_{13}<\theta_{14})E_{4}^{2}\Phi_{12}
is never large.

We have seen that the LC+ approximation is sufficient to capture most of the enhanced contributions to
E_{4}^{2}\sum\Phi_{lk}\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{lk}
, leaving just the contribution in Eq. (
266
) (assuming
\theta_{13}<\theta_{23}
). Using the LC+ approximation, the probability density associated with the emission of gluon 4 is

\begin{split}\sum_{l}\sum_{k\neq l}\Phi_{lk}&\mathrm{Tr}\,C^{\mathrm{LC+}}_{lk}\\
={}&(\Phi_{31}+\Phi_{32})\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}+(\Phi_{13}+\Phi_{23})C_{\mathrm{F}}\;.\end{split}

(267)

Compare the terms proportional to
\Phi_{31}
and
\Phi_{13}
. The emission probabilities
\Phi_{31}
and
\Phi_{13}
contain the same dipole factor
\overline{w}_{31}^{\mathrm{dipole}}=\overline{w}_{13}^{\mathrm{dipole}}
. They differ in their partitioning factors
A^{\prime}_{31}
and
A^{\prime}_{13}
, which are positive and satisfy
A^{\prime}_{31}+A^{\prime}_{13}=1
. The factor
A^{\prime}_{31}
is dominant when the direction of the momentum
\hat{p}_{4}
of the new gluon is closer to the direction of the first gluon than it is to the direction of the quark. In this case, the color factor in the emission probability is
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
. The factor
A^{\prime}_{13}
is dominant when the direction of
\hat{p}_{4}
is closer to the direction of the quark than it is to the direction of the first gluon. In this case, the color factor in the emission probability is
C_{\mathrm{F}}
. The analogous conclusion applies to the dipole formed by the first gluon and the antiquark.

Adding the contribution from Eq. (
266
), we have (assuming
\theta_{13}<\theta_{23}
)

\begin{split}\sum_{l}\sum_{k\neq l}\Phi_{lk}&\mathrm{Tr}\,C_{lk}\\
\approx{}&(\Phi_{31}+\Phi_{32})\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}+(\Phi_{13}+\Phi_{23})C_{\mathrm{F}}\\
&-\theta(\theta_{13}<\theta_{14})\Phi_{12}\,\frac{1}{2N_{\mathrm{c}}}\;.\end{split}

(268)

The added term is important when
\theta_{13}\ll\theta_{14}\ll 1
. In this region,
\Phi_{12}\approx\Phi_{32}
and the added term changes the coefficient of
\Phi_{32}
from
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
to
C_{\mathrm{F}}
.

We have discussed the case of four partons in electron-positron annihilation. For cases with an arbitrary number of partons, the LC+ approximation remains accurate up to corrections that may be large in some angular regions but that lack collinear singularities. The calculation of probabilities by taking the trace of the color density matrix is simple and is built into
Deductor
.

We have emphasized the real emission operators in the preceding discussion. There are also virtual exchange operators that create the Sudakov factor in a probability preserving shower. With full color, the virtual exchange operators can change the color vectors
\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
to which they are applied. However, with the LC+ approximation, applying the color operators to a vector
\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
returns just an eigenvalue times the vector. For gluon emission, the eigenvalue is either
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
(for emission from a gluon) or
C_{\mathrm{F}}
(for emission from a quark). Thus the Sudakov operators are simple in the LC+ approximation. They are part of
Deductor
.

Unfortunately, we do not currently know of a way to enhance the LC+ approximation so as to incorporate contributions like those in Eq. (
266
) while still avoiding the possibility that the revised virtual exchange operators change the color vectors
\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
to which they are applied, making it difficult to build the Sudakov operators in a computationally manageable way. Although we do not know of a practical way to put the virtual exchange parts of
\Delta\mathcal{S}
into a Sudakov exponential, it is possible to calculate the contributions from
\Delta\mathcal{S}
perturbatively
NSNewColor
. Typically, we have found that these contributions are numerically small, as in Fig.
4
.

The result on the right hand side of Eq. (
268
) was found, with a different notation, by Gustafson in 1993
Gustafson1993
as being a good approximation to the full
q\mathrm{g}\mathrm{g}\bar{q}
cross section. The analysis used what are now called Lund diagrams. In order to account for the
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}
terms that distinguish
C_{\mathrm{F}}
from
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
, Gustafson called on the idea of color coherence for wide angle soft gluon emission from partons with nearly collinear momenta. According to color coherence, we are to add amplitudes, not probabilities. In the formalism of the present paper, color coherence does not need to be invoked separately. It is built in because we add color amplitudes in Eq. (
XXV
).

After the present paper was submitted
NSlogsum
;
NSthrustsum
, Hamilton, Medves, Salam, Scyboz and Soyez
HamiltonShowerSum
extended the analysis of Gustafson to more parton emissions in electron-positron annihilation, providing prescriptions for making the choice between color factors
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
or
C_{\mathrm{F}}
for real gluon emissions. This paper omits direct analysis of color amplitudes or the effect on color amplitudes of the virtual exchanges needed to build a Sudakov operator.
13
13

13

The paper states that for four or more partons “one should worry about amplitude-level evolution
BottsSterman
, which is beyond the accuracy and scope of this article.”
For more than four partons, a direct, term-by-term analysis like that given above is cumbersome because there are many terms and because the color state before the soft gluon emission now has
\{c^{\prime}\}_{m}\neq\{c\}_{m}
. For this reason, we do not undertake a comparison to the results of Ref.
HamiltonShowerSum
here.

We have, however, undertaken a simple calculation to check the effects on the thrust distribution of degrading the LC+ approximation to just an LC approximation.
Deductor
has the capability to turn off the LC+ approximation at some point in the shower by changing the color group from SU(3) to U(3). With this treatment, a gluon is the same, in color, as a quark-antiquark pair. Then splittings with color connections that produce
1/N_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}
factors are omitted. This gives a variety of LC approximation with a factor
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
factor at each splitting.

In Fig.
5
, we compare the thrust distribution calculated with this U(3) leading color approximation to the thrust distribution calculated with the LC+ approximation, taken from Fig.
4
. In both cases, we apply the correction factor
r(\tau)
that was used in Fig.
4
. We see that replacing the LC+ approximation with this LC approximation makes a substantial difference. The first splitting must be
q\to q+\mathrm{g}
, for which a factor
C_{\mathrm{F}}
would be more sensible than a factor
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
, even though these are equivalent within the LC approximation. We tried the same calculation with
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
replaced by
C_{\mathrm{F}}
in the first splitting. This gives the dashed curve in Fig.
5
. This results in substantially improving the agreement with the LC+ curve. The discrepancy is reduced by a factor of roughly 7.

with

results corrected with the factor

to include more exact

evolution. The red solid curve uses the LC+ approximation and is taken from Fig.

. In the green solid curve the color approximation is reduced from LC+ to color using U(3) as the color group. This puts a factor

at

vertices. The purple dashed curve also uses U(3) as the color group but inserts a factor

at the first emission.

XXVI
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering

The default ordering variable in
Deductor
is
\Lambda
, Eq. (
98
). However, there is an option to use
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering,
14
14

14

For
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering,
k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}=-k_{\perp}^{2}
where the vector
k_{\perp}
is orthogonal to the momentum
p_{l}
of the emitting parton and to
Q
, rather than being orthogonal to
p_{l}
and the momentum
p_{k}
of the dipole partner parton.
still with exact color. We can define
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}(\nu)
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering using Eqs. (
175
) and (
248
). We simply set the scale parameters to
\mu^{2}=k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
for the first splitting and
\bar{\mu}^{2}=\bar{k}_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
for the second splitting. Then
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering means that
\bar{k}_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}<k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
in Eq. (
175
).

With
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, the reasoning supporting NLL accuracy of the
\Lambda
-ordered shower from Sec.
XXI
and Appendix
A
is lost. However, it appears that we can still get cancellation of
\log(\nu)
factors in
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
at the NLL level. That is, the integral has contributions proportional to
\log^{4}(\nu)
at large
\log(\nu)
, but after these contributions are summed, only terms proportional to
\log^{1}(\nu)
and
\log^{0}(\nu)
remain. The mechanism is that the contributions from the two terms specified by the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}
operation in the last line of Eq. (
175
), representing real emissions and virtual emissions, cancel each other. A complete proof is beyond the scope of this paper, but we present an argument that makes this conclusion plausible in Appendix
B
.

Figure 7:

Plot of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
versus
\log(\nu)
, as in Fig.
2
, for the
Deductor
shower algorithm with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering. The blue dashed curve is
d\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle/d\log(\nu)
.

We can check the effect of the choice of ordering variable on the summation of
\log(\nu)
factors in the thrust distribution by calculating
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
numerically using the
Deductor
shower algorithm with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering and exact color. The result is shown as the solid red curve in Fig.
6
. We see that
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
is quite small,
|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|<2
for
\log(\nu)<8
. For NLL accuracy, this curve should be linear for large
\log(\nu)
. To quite good, but not perfect, accuracy, it is.

We have also checked the behavior of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
as a function of
\log(\nu)
. The results are shown in Fig.
7
. For large
\nu
the highest power of
\log(\nu)
in
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
should be
\log^{2}(\nu)
. This implies that for large
\nu
the highest power of
\log(\nu)
in
d\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle/d\log(\nu)
should be
\log^{1}(\nu)
. The numerical result, graphed as the dashed blue line in Fig.
7
, supports this conclusion.

We have investigated only
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
. We have found results consistent with NLL accuracy for the
Deductor
shower with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, but there could still be inconsistencies with NLL accuracy for
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)\rangle
for other values of
k
and
n
. A promising approach to investigating this issue would be to automate the calculation of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)\rangle
so that these functions could be calculated numerically for any not-too-large values of
k
and
n
. We leave this approach to future work.

). We use a cutoff on the transverse momentum in splittings:

.

We can also look directly at
(\tau/\sigma_{\textsc{h}})\,d\sigma/d\tau
with
Q^{2}=(10\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
. We use either
Deductor
with its default
\Lambda
ordering or
Deductor
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, both with LC+ color. The result with
\Lambda
ordering, from Fig.
4
, includes the correction factor
r(\tau)
from Eq. (
250
). The result with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering needs no correction factor because
k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}
in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2})
in the
Deductor
splitting function is the same as the ordering variable. We do not include hadronization. Thus we examine only perturbative effects and the effects of the shower cutoff. With
\Lambda
ordering, the shower stops at
\Lambda=1\ \mathrm{GeV}
and there is also a cut that prevents the
k_{\mathrm{T}}
in any splitting from being smaller than
1\ \mathrm{GeV}
. With
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, the shower stops at
k_{\mathrm{T}}=1\ \mathrm{GeV}
. The result is shown in Fig.
8
. We see that the shower ordering does make a difference. Although
(\tau/\sigma_{\textsc{h}})\,d\sigma/d\tau
calculated with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering is similar to the NLL expectation
\tau g(\tau)
from Eqs. (
221
) and (
222
), the difference between these two results is greater than the expected uncertainties discussed for
\Lambda
ordering in Sec.
XXIV
.

As an alternative, we can follow the method of Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
and calculate
(\tau/\sigma_{\textsc{h}})\,d\sigma/d\tau
for various values of
Q^{2}
, and thus for various values of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})
. We choose
Q^{2}=(1\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
,
(10\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
, and
(100\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
, corresponding to
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})=0.087
,
0.069
, and
0.058
.
15
15

15

Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
considers
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})
as small as 0.005, corresponding to
Q^{2}\approx(10^{70}\ \mathrm{GeV})^{2}
but
Deductor
is not capable of working with values of
Q^{2}
as large as this.
For each value of
Q^{2}
, we calculate the expected NLL function
\tau g(\tau)
, Eqs. (
221
) and (
222
). Then we plot the ratio

R(\tau,Q^{2})=\frac{(\tau/\sigma_{\textsc{h}})\,d\sigma/d\tau}{\tau g(\tau)}\;.

(269)

The results are displayed in Fig.
9
. In the case
Q^{2}=(100\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
, there are typically around 100 partons produced in each event. This causes
Deductor
to operate very slowly, which leads to substantial statistical fluctuations that are visible in the plot.

If the log summation is working at the NLL level, the ratio plotted should be close to 1 and should get closer to 1 as
Q^{2}
increases. We note two features of the results. First, for any fixed value of
Q^{2}
,
\tau g(\tau)
fails to match the parton shower result for sufficiently small
\tau
. The value of
\tau
at which this failure sets in decreases as
Q^{2}
grows. For larger values of
\tau
, but still with
\tau<0.1
,
R(\tau,Q^{2})
is approximately constant:

R(\tau,Q^{2})\approx R_{0}(Q^{2})\;.

(270)

These values (
R_{0}=1.190,1.112,1.070
) are shown as dashed lines in Fig.
9
. Second, we note that
R(\tau,Q^{2})
is fairly close to 1 and gets closer to 1 as
Q^{2}
increases. In fact, to within about 10%,

R_{0}(Q^{2})-1\approx 23\,\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}(Q^{2})\;.

(271)

This is consistent with the expectation that
R_{0}(Q^{2})\to 0
as
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2})\to 0
. We tentatively conclude from these results that the
k_{\mathrm{T}}
-ordered
Deductor
shower is correctly summing thrust logarithms at the NLL level, even though the difference between the shower result and the NLL analytical result is larger for
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering than for
\Lambda
ordering.

XXVII
Effect of the momentum mapping for
\Lambda
ordering

Recall from Sec.
XVIII
that in a splitting
p_{l}\to\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
, we always have
p_{l}\neq\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
. In order to conserve momentum, we need to map the momenta
p_{i}
into new momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{{m\!+\!1}}\hat{p}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\;.

(272)

In the
Deductor
algorithm, this is accomplished by using a Lorentz transformation
NSI

\hat{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\Lambda^{\mu}_{\nu}\,p_{i}^{\nu}\;,\hskip 28.45274pti\notin\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}\;.

(273)

The Lorentz transformation is defined to be a boost in the plane of
p_{l}
and
Q
. We have found in Sec.
XVIII
that the boost angle
\omega
is small, of order
y
, and that the effect of this small Lorentz transformation on the thrust is small compared to the order
y
effect produced by the splitting itself.

For any parton shower, one will need a momentum mapping that preserves the total momentum. The
global
mapping produced by a Lorentz transformation is not the only possibility. A more widely used
local
choice is provided by the Catani-Seymour dipole splitting formalism
CataniSeymour
or the local mapping in
Pythia

SjostrandSkands
. For the Catani-Seymour choice, we start with the parton
l
that splits and its dipole partner
k
, with momenta
p_{l}
and
p_{k}
. After the splitting, we have a new parton
m+1
and new momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
,
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}
and
\hat{p}_{k}
. The definition is

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}={}&(1-z)\,p_{l}+zy\,p_{k}+k_{\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{l}={}&zp_{l}+(1-z)\,y\,p_{k}-k_{\perp}\;,\\
\hat{p}_{k}={}&\left(1-y\right)p_{k}\;,\end{split}

(274)

with
k_{\perp}\cdot p_{j}=k_{\perp}\cdot p_{k}=0
. Here
z
,
y
, and
k_{\perp}
are different from
z
,
y
and
k_{\perp}
defined for
Deductor
kinematics. The momenta of the other partons are unchanged:

\hat{p}_{i}=p_{i}\,\qquad i\notin\{l,k,{m\!+\!1}\}\;.

(275)

With this definition,

p_{l}+p_{k}=\hat{p}_{l}+\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}+\hat{p}_{k}\;.

(276)

Thus the total momentum is conserved. We have
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}+\hat{p}_{l}=p_{l}+y\,p_{k}
so

y=\frac{\hat{p}_{l}\cdot\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}}{p_{l}\cdot p_{k}}\;.

(277)

From
\hat{p}_{m\!+\!1}^{2}=0
we derive

-k_{\perp}^{2}=z(1-z)y\,2p_{l}\cdot p_{k}\;.

(278)

Note that if we start with a two parton state,
m=2
, and let one of the two partons,
l
, split to produce parton
{m\!+\!1}
, then there is precisely one parton
i
with
i\notin\{l,{m\!+\!1}\}
in Eq. (
273
) and this is the same as parton
k
in Eq. (
274
). That is, the global and local mappings are the same for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[1]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
for
m=2
. The operators
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(\mu^{2};\nu)
, with
k
real or virtual splittings, do depend on the choice of momentum mapping for
k\geq 2
.

The local momentum mapping has a feature for thrust that one might regard as peculiar. Suppose that parton
l
is in the right thrust hemisphere,
l\in\mathrm{R}
. Then for a small angle splitting, the daughter partons
l
and
{m\!+\!1}
will also be in the right hemisphere. In the case that
k\in\mathrm{R}
, we split a dipole that is entirely in
R
. Then Eqs. (
275
) and (
276
) imply that both
\tau_{\mathrm{R}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{L}}
in Eq. (
124
) are unchanged by the splitting, so that
\tau=\tau_{\mathrm{R}}+\tau_{\mathrm{L}}
is unchanged. Since, in this class of choices for the dipole that splits, the thrust is not changed, the real-virtual cancelation between
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\mu^{2})
and
\mathcal{S}^{[0,1]}(\mu^{2})
simply removes contributions of these dipoles from the calculation of the thrust distribution.

With
\Lambda
ordering and a local momentum mapping, the argument in Sec.
XXI
that the shower sums logarithms of thrust at the LL level still works, but the argument in Appendix
A
for cancellations at the NLL level fails. Thus we cannot expect a
\Lambda
-ordered parton shower that uses a local momentum mapping following Eqs. (
275
) and (
276
) to properly sum the logarithms of
\nu
at NLL accuracy.

0
2
4

, for the

is approximately quadratic in

, indicating that
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
changes the NLL result.

We can check what happens numerically by calculating
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
, Eq. (
248
), using the
\Lambda
-ordered
Deductor
parton shower algorithm with exact color but with the Catani-Seymour momentum mapping substituted for the global momentum mapping. The result is shown as the solid red curve in Fig.
10
. We note immediately that this result is completely different from the result in Fig.
1
: in the range
\log(\nu)<8
,
|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|
with the global momentum mapping is less than 1 while with the local mapping it reaches values greater than 30. Leaving aside the magnitude of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
, if the parton shower algorithm with a local momentum mapping produced NLL accuracy for summing
\log(\nu)
factors, the graph of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
would be a straight line, but it is not. The dashed blue curve is
d\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle/d\log(\nu)
. This curve is not a constant but rather a straight line. This implies that at large
\log(\nu)
,
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
is has contributions up to
\log^{2}(\nu)
.

We conclude from the combination of the analytical argument and the numerical results that using a local momentum mapping destroys the NLL accuracy of the result from a
\Lambda
-ordered parton shower, although LL accuracy is maintained.

XXVIII
Local momentum mapping with other orderings

As we have seen in Sec.
XXVII
, a parton shower algorithm needs to conserve momentum while accommodating the approximation that a parton that splits to two partons was on shell before the splitting.
Deductor
uses a global recoil strategy that spreads the needed momentum over all of the other partons in the event. With a local momentum mapping in the style of Catani-Seymour, Eq. (
274
), the recoil momentum is taken up by a single parton, possibly a very soft parton. For this reason the global recoil strategy seems less likely to lead to problems than the local recoil strategy.

Nevertheless, a local momentum mapping can certainly work. Indeed, we present an argument in Appendix
B
that
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
in
Deductor
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering is well behaved. In this construction, the local and global momentum mappings were equivalent in the limits considered. Thus
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering and a local momentum mapping should be well behaved.

Figure 14:

Plot of
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
, as in Fig.
