, versus
\log(\nu)
for a shower with
\beta=0.5
ordering and the Catani-Seymour local momentum mapping
CataniSeymour
according to an algorithm based on the
PanLocal
dipole shower of Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
with exact color.

We can investigate this issue by calculating
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
using two shower algorithms with a local momentum mapping following Eq. (
274
). The algorithms we use follow closely the PanLocal shower of Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
, but with color treated exactly. In the first algorithm that we use, the parameter
\beta
that defines the ordering variable in the PanLocal algorithm is set to
\beta=0
. That corresponds to
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering. In the second algorithm, we choose
\beta=0.5
. Roughly, that is half way between
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering and
\Lambda
ordering. Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
claims that these
PanLocal
showers sum the trust distribution at NLL accuracy at leading color.

The results are shown in Figs.
11
and
12
. In each case, in the range
\log(\nu)<8
,
|\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle|
reaches values greater than 10, while for
Deductor
with
\Lambda
ordering this same quantity is less than 1. Nevertheless, in each case, we see that
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\rangle
is, to a good approximation, a linear function of
\log(\nu)
for large
\log(\nu)
. This is consistent with NLL accuracy for summing logarithms of
\nu
.

In Figs.
13
and
14
, we plot
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
for the two
PanLocal
shower algorithms. To be consistent with NLL accuracy,
\langle\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)\rangle
at large
\log(\nu)
should not contain terms
\log^{j}(\nu)
for
j=3
or higher. The numerical results are consistent with this NLL expectation. In fact, in each case the highest power of
\log(\nu)
numerically is
\log^{1}(\nu)
. The coefficient of
\log^{2}(\nu)
vanishes to a good approximation. This tells us that the average value of the scale of the coupling inside the integrations is about
Q^{2}/\nu
.

XXIX
Conclusions

In Secs.
II
through
X
, we presented a general program for gaining direct access to how a parton shower sums large logarithms in either hadron-hadron, electron-hadron, or electron-positron collisions. We provided some summarizing remarks on the general program in Sec.
X
. In Secs.
XI
through
XXVIII
, we applied this program to electron-positron annihilation. We have limited ourselves to just one example, the thrust distribution. We have, however, looked at results for more than one shower algorithm.

The method that we propose works with the appropriate integral transform of the distribution of interest. In this case, we need the Laplace transform
\tilde{g}(\nu)
, Eq. (
126
), of the thrust distribution. We seek to find how
\tilde{g}(\nu)
behaves for large
\nu
.

We rearrange the cross section calculation so as to write
\tilde{g}(\nu)
in the form from Eq. (
231
),

\begin{split}\tilde{g}(\nu)={}&\exp\!\Big(\big({1}\big|\mathcal{I}(\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}}\big)\Big)\;.\end{split}

(279)

Here
|{\{p,f,c,c\}_{2}})
is a color and flavor singlet
q\bar{q}
basis state with
p_{1}+p_{2}=Q
and the operator
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
is an integral,

\mathcal{I}(\nu)=\int_{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\bar{\mu}^{2}}{\bar{\mu}^{2}}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{\mu}^{2};\nu)\;.

(280)

We expand
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
in powers of the shower evolution operator
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
. Then the coefficients
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
, proportional to
k
powers of
\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2})
, can be further expanded as

\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\right]^{n}\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)\;,

(281)

in which the strong coupling is evaluated at a fixed scale
Q^{2}/\nu
. Thus the shower result is quite directly expressed in exponentiated form in terms of an operator
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
with a known perturbative expansion.

For the
Deductor
shower algorithm with either
\Lambda
or
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering,
\mathcal{I}^{[1]}(\nu)
provides the standard NLL summation of
\log(\nu)
factors.
16
16

16

The current
Deductor
code with
\Lambda
ordering, as distinct from the algorithm that it is based on, lacks the term with coefficient
\beta_{1}
needed to evaluate the dependence of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}((1-z)yQ^{2})
on
(1-z)
. This changes the
Deductor
result at the NLL level.
In order for the contributions
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
for
k\geq 2
to not spoil the NLL summation,
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
should not contain more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

For the
Deductor
shower algorithm with its default
\Lambda
ordering, we find analytically that
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
does not contain more than
n-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
.

We have no such result for
Deductor
with
k_{T}
ordering, but we outline an argument in Appendix
B
that real-virtual cancellations in
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
reduce its large
\nu
behavior from
\log^{4}(\nu)
to
\log^{1}(\nu)
.

We evaluate
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
numerically. In order not to spoil NLL summation, its large
\nu
behavior should be no more than
\log^{1}(\nu)
. For the
Deductor
algorithm with
\Lambda
ordering but with a local momentum mapping instead of the global momentum mapping used in
Deductor
, we find
\log^{2}(\nu)
behavior, implying a failure of NLL accuracy (Fig.
10
). In other cases, we find
\log^{1}(\nu)
behavior, consistently with NLL accuracy. These cases include
Deductor
-
\Lambda
(Fig.
1
),
Deductor
-
k_{\mathrm{T}}
(Fig.
6
),
PanLocal
-(
\beta
=0) (Fig.
11
), and
PanLocal
-(
\beta
=0.5) (Fig.
12
).

We also evaluate
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
numerically for the shower algorithms
Deductor
-
\Lambda
(Fig.
2
),
Deductor
-
k_{\mathrm{T}}
(Fig.
7
),
PanLocal
-(
\beta
=0) (Fig.
13
), and
PanLocal
-(
\beta
=0.5) (Fig.
14
). In each case, we find large
\log(\nu)
behavior with no more than 2 powers of
\log(\nu)
, consistently with NLL accuracy.

We emphasize in this paper writing the appropriate integral transform of the distribution of interest, such as the thrust distribution, as an exponential and examining the exponent
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
. However, it is also possible to simply look directly at the distribution of interest as it is generated by a given parton shower. For this, one needs to simulate collisions at large values of
Q^{2}
. We have not pushed this method to nearly as large a value of
Q^{2}
as in Ref.
DasguptaShowerSum
. However, we find that, at least for electron-positron annihilation, this direct method can be useful.

Specifically, we examine directly the thrust distribution
\tau g(\tau)
for
Deductor
with
\Lambda
and
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering, using
Q^{2}=(10\ \mathrm{TeV})^{2}
. With
\Lambda
ordering, this works well (Figs.
3
and
4
). With
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering (Fig.
8
), the agreement with the analytic NLL expectation is not as good. However, when we compare
\tau g(\tau)
to the NLL expectation at a sequence of values of
Q^{2}
, we find what appears to be convergence to the NLL result as
Q^{2}
increases (Fig.
9
).

For both analytical and numerical analyses of
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
, we have used exact QCD color. For direct calculations of the thrust distribution using
Deductor
we have used the LC+ approximation for color. We have seen in Fig.
4
that the LC+ approximation is numerically very accurate for the thrust distribution, although we have noted in Sec.
XXV
that the LC+ approximation may change the coefficients of some
\log(\nu)
factors in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
from what they are with full color. We have also seen in Sec.
XXV
that the use of just the leading color approximation can lead to loss of accuracy if one does not carefully adjust the choice between
C_{\mathrm{F}}
and
C_{\mathrm{A}}/2
, as studied in Ref.
HamiltonShowerSum
.

There are several avenues available for future research that extends the results of this paper.

First, the method of this paper applies to several observables in electron-positron annihilation. We have tried variations on the shower algorithm examined, but have looked at only one observable, the thrust distribution. It is certainly worthwhile to see what patterns emerge if we look at other observables.

Second, the method developed in Secs.
II
through
X
applies to observables in hadron-hadron collisions as well as in electron-positron collisions. It is of interest to see how this method works in practice for some hadron-hadron observables, starting with the
k_{\mathrm{T}}
distribution in the Drell-Yan process.

Third, we construct numerical implementations of
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
and
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
for the particular observable examined and for several shower algorithms. This allows one to test numerically if the large
\nu
behaviors of
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
and
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
are consistent with NLL summation. When we find for a certain shower algorithm that NLL summation fails at the level of
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
or
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
, then NLL summation fails for that shower algorithm and observable. However, if NLL summation is not spoiled by
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
or
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{3}(\nu)
, it could still fail in
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
for some larger values of
k
and
n
. Thus it would be valuable to have numerical implementations of
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}_{n}(\nu)
for some larger values of
k
and
n
. Then one would have more stringent numerical tests of NLL summation for a given shower algorithm and a given observable.

Fourth, it would be helpful to have analytical insight into the behavior of the operators
\mathcal{I}^{[k]}(\nu)
for
k\geq 3
in cases that are similar to the thrust distribution using a
k_{\mathrm{T}}
-ordered shower.

Fifth, it would be worthwhile to examine in detail the effect of using the LC+ approximation for color instead of exact color for maintaining LL or NLL summation of large logarithms.

Sixth, although the LC+ approximation for color is numerically quite accurate in cases like that exhibited in Fig.
4
, we have seen in Eq. (
266
) that it leaves out some contributions that are potentially important. Thus it would be worthwhile to find an improved approximation for color in a parton shower.

We close with the observation that it is expecting a lot to expect that a first order shower algorithm will sum logarithms at the LL or NLL level. If we had a parton shower based on splitting functions at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}

NSAllOrder
, then we could expect to correctly produce contributions to
\mathcal{I}(\nu)
of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{j}(\nu)
with
n\leq N
,
j\leq n+1
. We might not correctly produce contributions of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{j}(\nu)
with
n>N
,
j\leq n+1
because we lack the order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
contributions to the shower splitting functions. However, contributions of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j>n+1
should vanish because these contributions can never be provided by
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}
contributions to the shower splitting functions. Currently, all that we have (in several variations) is a first order shower,
N=1
. Thus we can expect to correctly produce contributions of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}\log^{2}(\nu)
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{1}\log^{1}(\nu)
. We can also expect to obtain exponentiation of logarithms of
\nu
: contributions of order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}\log^{j}(\nu)
with
j>n+1
should vanish. With care, we can hope to have LL or NLL summation of
\log(\nu)
factors, but this relies on incorporating the most important parts of higher order splitting operators into the first order operator
\mathcal{S}
.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the participants in the conference
PSR21-Parton Showers and Resummation
for useful discussions.
This work was supported in part by the United States Department of Energy under grant DE-SC0011640. The work benefited from access to the University of Oregon high performance computer cluster, Talapas.

Appendix A
Structure of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}
at NLL accuracy

We examine
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\mu)
for
x
of order 1 and
k\geq 2
. We prove that this operator has at most
n-1
factors of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
.

Recall from Sec.
XXI
that
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\mu)
for
x
of order 1 and
k\geq 2
has at most
n
factors of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
.

We also note that
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
for
x
of order 1 has one power of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, where the
\log(\nu)
factor arises from an integration
d(1-z)/(1-z)
down to a lower limit proportional to
1/\nu
, as in Eq. (
XIX
). Thus
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
for
x
of order 1 has at most
n
powers of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
.

To proceed, we prove that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\mu)
with
k=2
contains at most
n-1
factors of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{n}(Q^{2}/\nu)
and we prove that if this property holds for
k=2,3,\dots,N
, then it holds for
k=N+1
.

Consider Eq. (
XXI
) for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
for
k\geq 2
. In the first term, at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, there are
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}
and one power from
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}
. In the second term (if
k\geq 3
) at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there are there are
k+1-j
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}
and
j-1
powers from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}
, for a total of just
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
. That is, this contribution is NNLL. In the third term, at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there is one power of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}
and
k-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k]}
, for a total of
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
. That is, this contribution is NNLL. If we expand the NNLL contributions to higher order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, we add just one power of
\log(\nu)
per
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
, so the contributions remain NNLL. This gives us

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-48.36958pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(282)

This leaves us with an NLL contribution if the NLL contribution does not cancel. This result does not include
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}
. For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[2]}
, Eq. (
239
) gives us 2 powers of
\log(\nu)
at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}(Q^{2})
. This is an NLL contribution if the NLL contribution does not cancel.

If we use Eq. (
A
), then we need information on
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}
. We can use Eq. (
XXI
) for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)
for
k\geq 1
. In the first term at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there are
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}
and one power of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}
, giving us a total of
k+1
powers of
\log(\nu)
. This is an NLL contribution. In the second term (for
k\geq 2
) at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there are
k+1-j
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1-j]}
and
j-1
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[j]}
, giving us a total of
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
. This is an NNLL contribution. In the third term at order
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}^{k+1}(Q^{2}/\nu)
there are
k
powers of
\log(\nu)
from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}
. This is an NNLL contribution. Again, if we expand the NNLL contributions to higher order in
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, we add just one power of
\log(\nu)
per
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
, so the contributions remain NNLL. This leaves us with

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{[k+1]}(xQ^{2}/\nu;\nu)\hskip-51.21504pt

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{l}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\ \mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times(1-e^{-\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}})

\displaystyle+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(283)

This derivation does not include
\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}
. For
\mathcal{Y}^{[1]}
we can use Eq. (
237
), which gives us just Eq. (
A
) with
\mathcal{Y}^{[0]}
replaced by 1 and no NNLL additional contribution.

Eq. (
A
) gives us a recursion relation that we can solve to NLL accuracy in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}(xQ^{2}

\displaystyle/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}\sum_{l_{1}\dots l_{k}}\int_{0}^{x}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{x_{k-1}}\!\frac{dx_{k}}{x_{k}}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{k}}^{[1,0]}(x_{k}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{k}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{k}})

\displaystyle\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(x_{2}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{2}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{2}})

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}})

\displaystyle+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(284)

We can substitute this solution for
\mathcal{Y}^{[k]}
into Eq. (
A
) to give us

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(

\displaystyle x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}\sum_{l_{0}\dots l_{k}}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{x_{k-1}}\!\frac{dx_{k}}{x_{k}}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{k}}^{[1,0]}(x_{k}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{n}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{k}})

\displaystyle\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(x_{2}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{2}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{2}})

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(x_{1}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-\xi_{l_{1}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{1}})

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,e^{-\xi_{l_{0}}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{0}}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(285)

The explicit exponential exponential factors restrict the
x_{i}
integrations to
x_{i}
of order 1 (as we have already seen). We now want to find how many factors of
\log(\nu)
are contained in the operators
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
. Since
\log(x/\nu)
is equivalent for this purpose to
\log(1/\nu)
when
x
is of order 1, we can replace all of the
x_{i}
factors in the arguments of
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)
by 1.

In Eq. (
A
), we have factors
\exp(-\hat{\xi}_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}x_{i})
. The parameters
\xi_{l}
, are defined in Eq. (
204
). They are close to 1:
\xi_{l}-1
is proportional to
[1-\cos(\theta(l,\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}))]
. It is a good approximation to take the thrust axis
\vec{n}_{\mathrm{T}}
to be the direction of either the quark or the antiquark in the
q
-
\bar{q}
state at the start of the shower. Then the angle between
\vec{p}_{l}
at a later stage of the shower and
\vec{n}
is determined by the emission angles at the intervening stages. But in order to accumulate the maximal number of
\log(\nu)
factors in these splittings, all of these emission angles must be small. That is, if we expand
\exp(-\xi_{l}x_{i})
in powers of
[1-\cos(\theta)]
, where
\theta
is one of the splitting angles, then a factor
[1-\cos(\theta)]
will eliminate a
\log(\nu)
factor in an integration
d\cos(\theta)/[1-\cos(\theta)]
with limits analogous to the limits in Eq. (
114
). We conclude that for the purpose of our present NLL calculation we can set all of the
\xi_{l}^{\mathrm{op}}
factors in Eqs. (
A
) to 1.

These changes gives us

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(

\displaystyle x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}\sum_{l_{0}\dots l_{k}}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{x_{k-1}}\!\frac{dx_{k}}{x_{k}}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{k}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-x_{k}})

\displaystyle\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{2}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-x_{2}})

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{1}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-x_{1}})

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\,e^{-x_{0}}

\displaystyle\times\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(286)

The first
\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)
factor in Eq. (
A
) is

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}=\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.

The contribution from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
is rather simple and we will consider it later.

We begin by considering the contribution from
{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}
. This operator, acting on the state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
, produces a linear combination of states with
m+1
partons,
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}})
,

\begin{split}\sum_{l_{0}=1}^{m}&\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)e^{-x_{0}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
\approx{}&-\sum_{l_{0}=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{0}=1\\
k_{0}\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{0}(l_{0},k_{0})\big|{\{c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\int\!\frac{d\phi_{0}}{2\pi}\int\!\frac{dz_{0}}{1-z_{0}}\ \frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\big(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z_{0})Q^{2}/(\nu a_{l_{0}})\big)}{2\pi}\\
&\times\Theta\!\left(\frac{a_{l_{0}}}{\nu\,\vartheta(l_{0},k_{0})}<1-z_{0}<1\right)\\
&\times e^{-x_{0}}\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(287)

Here we use the approximate form of
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)
given in Eq. (
109
). We split parton
l_{0}
with dipole partner parton
k_{0}
, creating a new parton
m+1
, which we consider to be a gluon. The color operator is

\mathcal{C}_{0}(l_{0},k_{0})=T_{l_{0}}\otimes T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}+T_{k_{0}}\otimes T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}\;,

(288)

as defined below Eq. (
109
). We have specified a scale argument based on the transverse momentum for the splitting for
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}
. The new momentum
\hat{p}_{{m\!+\!1}}
and the new momentum
\hat{p}_{l}
are given by the splitting variables
y=1/\nu
,
z_{0}
and
\phi_{0}
. The new momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
for
i\neq l_{0},{m\!+\!1}
are slightly different from the starting momenta, as specified by the momentum mapping.

Let us consider what the one of the operators,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{i}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
, in Eq. (
A
) does to this state. We consider the quantity

\big|{A_{i}}\big)=\sum_{l_{i}=1}^{{m\!+\!1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{i}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.

(289)

Again, we use the approximate form of
\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)
given in Eq. (
109
), so that

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}&-\sum_{l=1}^{{m\!+\!1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)\\
&\times\int\!\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\int\!\frac{dz}{1-z}\ \frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\big(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z)Q^{2}/(\nu\hat{a}_{l})\big)}{2\pi}\\
&\times\Theta\!\left(\frac{1}{\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l,k)}<\frac{1-z}{\hat{a}_{l}}<\frac{1}{\hat{a}_{l}}\right)\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(290)

Here the hats in
\hat{\vartheta}(l,k)
and
\hat{a}_{l}
indicate that these quantities are based on the momenta in
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}})
. In Eq. (
290
), we split parton
l
with dipole partner parton
k
, creating a new parton
m+2
, which we consider to be a gluon.
17
17

17

We omit splittings
\mathrm{g}\to q\bar{q}
since these splittings lack a soft singularity. For a
q\to q\mathrm{g}
or
\bar{q}\to\bar{q}\mathrm{g}
splitting from an
m+1
parton state, the daughter gluon is labelled
m+2
.
However, the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
operation, Eqs. (
157
) and (
158
), returns us to the starting momentum and flavor state
|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}})
. With the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
operation, Eq. (
159
), the color operator is

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}(l,k)={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{T_{l}\otimes T_{k}^{\dagger}+T_{k}\otimes T_{l}^{\dagger}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
={}&1\otimes\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{k}+\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{k}\otimes 1\;.\end{split}

(291)

In the first term in the second line, the operator
\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{k}
operates on the bra color state and leaves the number of partons in the color state unchanged. The operator inserts a color matrix
T^{a}
with gluon color index
a
on line
l
and another
T^{a}
on line
k
. The dot in
\bm{T}_{k}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{l}
indicates a sum over
a
. In the second term, the same operator is applied to the bra state.

There is an integration over the splitting variables
\phi
and
z
. It will prove helpful to define a function
L(w,u)
given by performing this integration,

L(w,u)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\!\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\int_{1/w}^{1/u}\!\frac{dx}{x}\ \frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}xQ^{2}/\nu)}{2\pi}\;.

(292)

This function is to be expanded in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
. At lowest order, this integration gives simply
[\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}/(2\pi)]\log(w/u)
. At higher orders in an expansion in powers of
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{2}/\nu)
the result is more complicated. With this notation,

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}&-\sum_{l=1}^{{m\!+\!1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)\,L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l,k),\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(293)

We break up the sums in the form

\displaystyle\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}

\displaystyle-\bigg\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l,k),\hat{a}_{l}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}(l_{0},k)L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}^{2}(l_{0},k),\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)

(294)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},k)L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}^{2}({m\!+\!1},k),\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}^{2}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1}),\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},l_{0})L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}({m\!+\!1},l_{0}),\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.

Now, as long as neither
l
nor
k
equals
m+1
, the angle variable
\hat{\vartheta}(l,k)
is very close to the
corresponding angle variable
\vartheta(l,k)
in the state
|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
before the first splitting. The
angle variable
\hat{\vartheta}({m\!+\!1},k)
for
k\neq l_{0}
is very close to
\vartheta(l_{0},k)
in the state before the first
splitting, since partons
l_{0}
and
m+1
are nearly collinear in the integration region that can lead to a
\log(\nu)

factor in the first splitting. Thus we regard these angles as fixed when calculating
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}})
. On the other hand,
\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
is the angle variable for the first splitting and
is thus an integration variable in this calculation. Integrating over this variable can produce a
\log(\nu)

factor. Thus we treat
\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
as potentially small in Eq. (
A
), but we treat the
other angle variables as being finite. For the purpose of finding
\log(\nu)
factors, we simply replace these finite
angle variables by 1. These substitutions give us

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}&-\bigg\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}(l_{0},k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\\
&+\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1}),\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)\\
&+\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1}),\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\bigg\}\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(295)

In two of the terms in Eq. (
295
), the parameter
\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}
appears. This parameter is large when the momentum fraction
1-z_{0}
of parton
m+1
in the first splitting is small:

\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}\approx\frac{a_{l_{0}}}{1-z_{0}}\;.

(296)

We also note that the angle variable
\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
is proportional to
1/(1-z_{0})
according to Eq. (
113
). We have

\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})\approx\frac{a_{l_{0}}}{\nu(1-z_{0})}\;.

(297)

Combining these equations gives us

\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}\approx\nu\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})\;.

(298)

With this replacement, the function
L
, Eq. (
292
), in the last term in Eq. (
295
) is approximately

L\big(\nu\,\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1}),\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\approx L(\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}},\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}})=0\;.

(299)

In the fourth term in Eq. (
295
), we use this replacement to eliminate
\hat{\vartheta}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
in favor of
\hat{a}_{m\!+\!1}
. With these substitutions, we have

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}&-\bigg\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}(l_{0},k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\\
&+\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})L\big(\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}},\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)\bigg\}\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(300)

Using the definition (
292
) of
L(w,u)
, this function in the last term can be written as

L\big(\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}},\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)=-L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)+L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l_{0}}\big)\;.

(301)

In the sum in the second term in Eq. (
300
) we can add and subtract a contribution from
k=m+1
. After adding this contribution, the sum includes
k=m+1
, so that this sum can be combined with the sums in the first term. Then in the first term we can include
l=l_{0}
in the sum over
l
. In the third term in Eq. (
300
) we can add and subtract a contribution from
k=l_{0}
, so that after adding this contribution the sum includes
k=l_{0}
. With these changes, we have

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}&-\bigg\{\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},k)L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\\
&-2\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\bigg\}\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(302)

In the first term in Eq. (
302
), we can use color conservation to write

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}\mathcal{C}(l,k)={}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{{m\!+\!1}}[\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{k}\otimes 1+1\otimes\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{k}]\\
={}&-[\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{l}\otimes 1+1\otimes\bm{T}_{l}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{l}]\\
={}&-2C_{l}[1\otimes 1]\;,\end{split}

(303)

where
C_{l}=C_{\mathrm{A}}
if parton
l
is a gluon and
C_{l}=C_{\mathrm{F}}
if parton
l
is a quark or antiquark. The same applies to the second term:

\begin{split}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}({m\!+\!1},k)=-2C_{\mathrm{A}}[1\otimes 1]\;,\end{split}

(304)

where we have used
C_{{m\!+\!1}}=C_{\mathrm{A}}
since parton
m+1
must be a gluon in order to give a leading
\log(\nu)
contribution. These substitutions give us

\displaystyle\big|{A_{i}}\big)\approx{}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\sum_{l=1}^{m}2C_{l}[1\otimes 1]\,L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)

(305)

\displaystyle+2\big[C_{\mathrm{A}}[1\otimes 1]+\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})\big]L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.

Consider now the term in Eq. (
305
) that contains a color operator
\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
, defined in Eq. (
291
). We apply this operator after the color operator for the initial splitting,
\mathcal{C}_{0}(l_{0},k_{0})
, defined in Eq. (
288
). This gives us an operator with four terms,

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{i}={}&(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})\,T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}+T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}\,(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})\\
&+(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})\,T_{k_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}+T_{k_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}\,(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})\;.\end{split}

(306)

There can be several factors of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}_{l}^{[1,0]}(xQ^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
in Eq. (
A
) and in some of those factors we can select the
\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
term in Eq. (
305
). Finally, there is a
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\cdots}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
operation. This gives us a sum of color operators of the form

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{A}\,T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}\,(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{B}\\
&+(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{A}\,T_{k_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}\,(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{B}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(307)

Using Eq. (
158
), this becomes

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{A+B}\,T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}\\
&+T_{k_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}\,(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{A+B}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(308)

Now consider the color operator
\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}}\,T_{l_{0}}^{a}
. In diagrams, parton
l_{0}
emits a gluon with label
{m\!+\!1}
, leaving parton
l_{0}
in a new color state. Then a gluon is exchanged between partons
l_{0}
and
{m\!+\!1}
. This gives us a color triangle diagram,

\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}}\,T_{l_{0}}^{a}={\mathrm{i}}f_{abc}T_{l_{0}}^{b}T_{l_{0}}^{c}\;.

(309)

Then we can use

\begin{split}{\mathrm{i}}f_{abc}T_{l_{0}}^{b}T_{l_{0}}^{c}={}&\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{i}}f_{abc}[T_{l_{0}}^{b},T_{l_{0}}^{c}]=\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{i}}f_{abc}{\mathrm{i}}f_{bcd}T_{l_{0}}^{d}\\
={}&-\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\,T_{l_{0}}^{a}\;.\end{split}

(310)

Thus

\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}}\,T_{l_{0}}^{a}=-\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\,T_{l_{0}}^{a}\;.

(311)

This gives us

(\bm{T}_{l_{0}}\!\cdot\!\bm{T}_{{m\!+\!1}})^{A+B}\,T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}=\left[-\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\right]^{A+B}T_{l_{0}}\!\otimes\!T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}\;.

(312)

The second term in Eq. (
308
) gives the same result, so that the net color operator defined in Eq. (
307
) is

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}={}&\left[-\frac{C_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}\right]^{A+B}\!\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{T_{l_{0}}\otimes T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}+T_{k_{0}}\otimes T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\;.\end{split}

(313)

We conclude that when
\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})
in Eq. (
305
) is part of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
in Eq. (
A
), we get the same result for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
by making the replacement

\mathcal{C}(l_{0},{m\!+\!1})\to-C_{\mathrm{A}}[1\otimes 1]\;.

(314)

There is a factor 2 for each
C_{\mathrm{A}}
here because there are two
T_{l_{0}}\otimes T_{k_{0}}^{\dagger}
terms and two
T_{k_{0}}\otimes T_{l_{0}}^{\dagger}
terms in Eq. (
306
).

With this replacement, the terms in Eq. (
305
) proportional to
L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{{m\!+\!1}}\big)
cancel. Thus we get the same result for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
by making the replacement

\big|{A_{i}}\big)\to\big|{A_{i}^{\mathrm{eff}}}\big)\;,

(315)

where

\begin{split}\big|{A_{i}^{\mathrm{eff}}}\big)\approx{}&\sum_{l=1}^{m}2C_{l}[1\otimes 1]\,L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(316)

Note that
\big|{A_{i}^{\mathrm{eff}}}\big)
is a number, which we may call
\lambda_{i}
, times the starting state vector,

\big|{A_{i}^{\mathrm{eff}}}\big)=\lambda_{i}\big|{\{\hat{p},\hat{f},\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{{m\!+\!1}}}\big)\;.

(317)

Return now to Eq. (
A
) for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
applied to the starting state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
. In the last factor, we have dealt with the operator
\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)
, which creates a new parton with label
m+1
. Now we turn to the remaining operator,
-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}
. This operator, acting on the state
\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
, produces a linear combination of states with
m
partons,
\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)
. Here the momentum and flavors are the same as in the initial state, but the colors change. More precisely,

\begin{split}\sum_{l_{0}=1}^{m}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}e^{-x_{0}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
\approx{}&-\sum_{l_{0}=1}^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{0}=1\\
k_{0}\neq l_{0}\end{subarray}}^{m}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{C}(l_{0},k_{0})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&\times\int\!\frac{d\phi_{0}}{2\pi}\int\!\frac{dz_{0}}{1-z_{0}}\ \frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\big(\lambda_{\mathrm{R}}(1-z_{0})Q^{2}/(\nu a_{l_{0}})\big)}{2\pi}\\
&\times\Theta\!\left(\frac{a_{l_{0}}}{\nu\,\vartheta(l_{0},k_{0})}<1-z_{0}<1\right)\\
&\times e^{-x_{0}}\;.\end{split}

(318)

Let us consider what the one of the operators,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{i}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}
, in Eq. (
A
) does to this state. We consider the quantity

\big|{B_{i}}\big)=\sum_{l_{i}=1}^{{m\!+\!1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{i}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.

(319)

With an analysis similar to but simpler than our previous analysis, we obtain

\begin{split}\big|{B_{i}}\big)\approx{}&\sum_{l=1}^{m}2C_{l}[1\otimes 1]\,L\big(\nu,\hat{a}_{l}\big)\\
&\times(1-e^{-x_{i}})\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(320)

This gives us

\big|{B_{i}}\big)=\lambda_{i}\big|{\{p,f,\hat{c},\hat{c}^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\;,

(321)

where the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
is exactly the
\lambda_{i}
in Eq. (
317
).

We can substitute Eqs. (
321
) and (
317
) into Eq. (
A
) to obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(&x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
={}&(-1)^{n}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\!\frac{dx_{1}}{x_{1}}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\frac{dx_{2}}{x_{2}}\cdots\int_{0}^{x_{k-1}}\!\frac{dx_{k}}{x_{k}}\\
&\times\lambda_{k}\cdots\lambda_{2}\,\lambda_{1}\\
&\times\sum_{l_{0}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
&\times e^{-x_{0}}\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)\\
&+\mathrm{NNLL}\;.\end{split}

(322)

However

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big]_{1-\mathbb{P}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\hskip-42.67912pt&\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
={}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}_{l_{0}}^{[1,0]}(Q^{2}/\nu)}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\\
={}&0\;.\end{split}

(323)

Thus the NLL contributions to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)
vanish:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{[k+1]}(x_{0}Q^{2}/\nu;\nu)\big|{\{p,f,c,c^{\prime}\}_{m}}\big)=\mathrm{NNLL}\;.

(324)

Appendix B
Cancellation with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering

In this appendix, we explore the cancellation of large
\log(\nu)
factors in
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering. We can write
({1}|\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}(\nu)\big|{\{\tilde{p},\tilde{f},\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{2}}\big)
in the form

\begin{split}\big({1}\big|\mathcal{I}^{[2]}&(\nu)\big|{\{\tilde{p},\tilde{f},\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{2}}\big)\\
={}&\int_{0}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{d\tilde{k}_{T}}{\tilde{k}_{T}}\int\!d\tilde{\eta}\int\!\frac{d\tilde{\phi}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{Q^{2}}\!\frac{dk_{T}}{k_{T}}\int\!d\eta\int\!\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\ \\
&\times\Theta(k_{\mathrm{T}}<\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}})(1-e^{\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)})e^{-\nu\tau}\\
&\times\big({1}\big|\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(k_{\mathrm{T}},\eta,\phi)\\
&\times\big\{\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\phi})-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big[}{\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\phi})}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\big]_{\mathbb{P}}}\big\}\\
&\times\big|{\{\tilde{p},\tilde{f},\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}\}_{2}}\big)\;.\end{split}

(325)

We begin with a
q\bar{q}
state with parton momenta
\tilde{p}_{1}
and
\tilde{p}_{2}
aligned along the
+
and
-

z
axis, respectively. Then one of these two partons splits, producing parton 3. We suppose that it is parton
1
that splits. After the splitting, we have partons with momenta
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and
p_{3}
. The value of
1-T
in this state is
\tau
and we suppose that
\tau\ll 1
. Then there is a second splitting, producing partons with momenta
\hat{p}_{1}
,
\hat{p}_{2}
,
\hat{p}_{3}
, and
\hat{p}_{4}
with a thrust variable
\hat{\tau}\ll 1
. We consider either the splitting of parton 3 with parton 2 as the dipole partner or the splitting of parton 2 with parton 3 as dipole partner. Other splitting possibilities are not as important and we omit consideration of them here. We limit our consideration to the leading color approximation.

We begin with the first splitting, which we describe with splitting variables
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
,
\tilde{\eta}
,
\tilde{\phi}
that relate
p_{3}
to
\tilde{p}_{1}
and
\tilde{p}_{2}
:

p_{3}=e^{\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\,\tilde{p}_{1}+e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\,\tilde{p}_{2}+\tilde{k}_{\perp}\;.

(326)

Here
|Q|=[Q^{2}]^{1/2}=[2\tilde{p}_{1}\cdot\tilde{p}_{2}]^{1/2}
and
\tilde{k}_{\perp}
is a vector that is orthogonal to
\tilde{p}_{1}
and
\tilde{p}_{2}
:

\tilde{k}_{\perp}\cdot\tilde{p}_{1}=\tilde{k}_{\perp}\cdot\tilde{p}_{2}=0\;.

(327)

We have defined the scalar
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
by

\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}=\left[-\tilde{k}_{\perp}^{2}\right]^{1/2}\;.

(328)

This definition gives
p_{3}^{2}=0
. The variable
\tilde{\eta}
is the rapidity of
p_{3}
. We need one more splitting variable, the azimuthal angle
\tilde{\phi}
of
\tilde{k}_{\perp}
.

For emission from parton 1, the splitting function is small for
\tilde{\eta}<0
. There is a maximum value of
\tilde{\eta}
for fixed
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
, set by the condition for a maximally collinear emission

e^{\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}=1\;.

(329)

When
\tilde{\eta}
is close to this upper bound, the splitting function tends to zero. Thus we integrate over the splitting variables with measure
d\tilde{\eta}\,d\log(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|)
over the range
0\lesssim\tilde{\eta}\lesssim-\log(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|)
. In this range, as long as
\tilde{\eta}
is not near either endpoint, the splitting function is approximately constant. For small
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
, this is a large range. The integration gives us a large logarithm, which comes from integrating over the interior of the range, omitting the regions near the endpoints:

0\ll\tilde{\eta}\ll-\log(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|)\;.

(330)

We will assume that
\tilde{\eta}
lies in this range in the analysis that follows.

For an emission from parton 1, we define the momentum of parton 1 after the emission to be

p_{1}=\left[1-e^{\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right]\tilde{p}_{1}+\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}/|Q|^{2}}{1-e^{\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|}\,\tilde{p}_{2}-\tilde{k}_{\perp}\;.

(331)

With this definition,
p_{1}^{2}=0
and
p_{1}-\tilde{p}_{1}+p_{3}
lies entirely in the direction of
\tilde{p}_{2}
:

p_{1}-\tilde{p}_{1}+p_{3}=\frac{e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|}{1-e^{\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|}\,\tilde{p}_{2}\;.

(332)

Finally, we need to define the momentum
p_{2}
of parton 2 after the splitting so that momentum is conserved:
p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}=\tilde{p}_{1}+\tilde{p}_{2}
.
Using Eq. (
332
) we obtain
p_{2}
by applying a small boost in the
z
direction to
\tilde{p}_{2}
:

p_{2}=\left[1-\frac{e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|}{1-e^{\tilde{\eta}}\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|}\right]\tilde{p}_{2}\;.

(333)

This is the exact relation. In the integration range (
330
), this relation becomes

\tilde{p}_{2}-p_{2}\approx e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\,\tilde{p}_{2}\;.

(334)

We use Eqs. (
123
) and (
124
) to calculate the thrust for the state after the first splitting:

\begin{split}\tau={}&\frac{1}{Q^{-}}\left(p_{1}^{-}+p_{3}^{-}+p_{2}^{+}\right)\\
={}&\frac{1}{Q^{-}}\left(\tilde{p}_{2}^{-}-p_{2}^{-}+\tilde{p}_{1}^{-}+p_{2}^{+}\right)\;.\end{split}

(335)

We can use
\tilde{p}_{1}^{-}=p_{2}^{+}=0
. Then we can use
\tilde{p}_{2}^{-}=Q^{-}
and Eq. (
334
) for
\tilde{p}_{2}^{-}-p_{2}^{-}
. This gives
\tau\approx e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\,{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}/{|Q|}
or

\nu\tau\approx\nu e^{-\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;.

(336)

This relation is significant because this emission is accompanied by a measurement function
\exp(-\nu\tau)
. The measurement function is approximately 1 for
\nu\tau\ll 1
but approximately zero for
1\ll\nu\tau
. Thus we effectively integrate over the range

\nu\tau<1\;.

(337)

In the analysis that follows, we will need a relation between
2p_{3}\cdot Q
and the values of
\tilde{\eta}
and
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
for the splitting. We can use Eq. (
326
) with
\eta\gg 0
together with
2\tilde{p}_{1}\cdot Q=Q^{2}
to give

\frac{2p_{3}\cdot Q}{Q^{2}}=e^{\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;.

(338)

Figure 15:

Integration regions for second splitting.

We now turn to the second splitting. We will describe the splitting using variables and a momentum mapping that are slightly different from what is used in
Deductor
with
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering. In fact, we will use a local momentum mapping. However, in the kinematic limit of interest, the description used here reduces to the description used in
Deductor
. The splitting kinematics are illustrated in Fig.
15
. We describe the second splitting with splitting variables
k_{\mathrm{T}}
,
\eta
,
\phi
that relate
\hat{p}_{4}
to
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
:

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{4}={}&A_{32}\,e^{\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\,p_{3}+A_{23}\,e^{-\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\,p_{2}+k_{\perp}\;,\end{split}

(339)

where

\begin{split}A_{32}={}&\left[\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{2}\cdot p_{3}}\,\frac{p_{2}\cdot Q}{p_{3}\cdot Q}\right]^{1/2}\;,\\
A_{23}={}&\left[\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{2}\cdot p_{3}}\,\frac{p_{3}\cdot Q}{p_{2}\cdot Q}\right]^{1/2}\;.\end{split}

(340)

Here
k_{\perp}
is a vector that is orthogonal to
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
:

k_{\perp}\cdot p_{3}=k_{\perp}\cdot p_{2}=0\;.

(341)

As for the first splitting, we have defined the scalar
k_{\mathrm{T}}=\left[-k_{\perp}^{2}\right]^{1/2}
. This definition gives
\hat{p}_{4}^{2}=0
. The variable
\eta
describes the rapidity of
\hat{p}_{4}
with respect to the emitting dipole, with a constant
\log(p_{2}\cdot Q/p_{3}\cdot Q)/2
added
DasguptaShowerSum
. We need one more splitting variable, the azimuthal angle
\phi
of
k_{\perp}
in the dipole c.m. frame.

There is a limit to how large
\eta
can be:
\eta_{\mathrm{min}}<\eta<\eta_{\mathrm{max}}
. The limits are fixed by the requirements that the components of
\hat{p}_{4}
along
p_{3}
and
p_{2}
cannot be larger than 1:

\begin{split}\eta_{\mathrm{max}}={}&-\log\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right)-\log\left(A_{32}\right)\;,\\
\eta_{\mathrm{min}}={}&\log\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right)+\log\left(A_{23}\right)\;.\end{split}

(342)

The lines
\eta=\eta_{\mathrm{max}}
and
\eta=\eta_{\mathrm{min}}
are indicated in Fig.
15
as the lines labelled collinear. This is a large integration range. We will assume in what follows that
\eta
is not near to the endpoints of the integration range:

\eta_{\mathrm{min}}\ll\eta\ll\eta_{\mathrm{max}}\;.

(343)

For emission from parton 3, we let the momentum of parton 3 after the emission be

\begin{split}\hat{p}_{3}\approx{}&\left[1-A_{32}\,e^{\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right]p_{3}\\
&+\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{3}\cdot p_{2}}\left[1-A_{32}\,e^{\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right]^{-1}p_{2}-k_{\perp}\;.\end{split}

(344)

With this definition,
\hat{p}_{3}^{2}=0
and
\hat{p}_{3}-p_{3}+\hat{p}_{4}
lies entirely in the direction of
p_{2}
. Then we can maintain momentum conservation,
\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2}+\hat{p}_{3}+\hat{p}_{4}=p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}
by setting
\hat{p}_{1}=p_{1}
and obtaining
\hat{p}_{2}
by performing a small boost on
p_{2}
:

\hat{p}_{2}=e^{-\omega}p_{2}\;.

(345)

With a few algebraic steps, we find

\begin{split}e^{-\omega}={}&1-A_{23}\,e^{-\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\left[1-A_{32}\,e^{\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\right]^{-1}\;.\end{split}

(346)

These definitions have been exact for the kinematic variables and momentum mapping chosen. We can now make some approximations. Given our kinematic conditions (
330
) for the first emission, the momentum
p_{3}
has large rapidity. That is, it makes a small angle with the
z
axis. The transverse momentum vector defined in Eq. (
339
) is orthogonal to
p_{3}
and
p_{2}
whereas the transverse momentum vector in
Deductor
is orthogonal to
p_{3}
and
Q
. However, since
p_{3}
makes a small angle with the
z
axis, this is almost the same thing. In
Deductor
, momentum is conserved by applying a boost in the plane of
p_{3}
and
Q
. Since
p_{3}
makes a small angle with the
z
axis, this boost is almost exactly along the
z
axis. The boost is applied to both
p_{2}
and
p_{1}
, but this difference has only a tiny effect on the resulting thrust. Thus in the limit considered, the
Deductor
kinematics and the kinematics used here are equivalent.

We now examine the change in thrust produced by the emission of parton 4 from parton 3. We assume that
p_{4}
is in the right thrust hemisphere. This is always the case when
\eta\gg 0
. There is a region near
\eta\approx 0
in which this assumption fails. With the kinematics that we are using, the thrust axis is along
-\vec{p}_{2}
. That is, it is the
z
axis. Then we have

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}-\tau={}&\frac{1}{Q^{-}}\left[\hat{p}_{4}^{-}+\hat{p}_{3}^{-}-p_{3}^{-}+\hat{p}_{2}^{+}-p_{2}^{+}\right]\\
={}&\frac{1}{Q^{-}}\left[p_{2}^{-}-\hat{p}_{2}^{-}+\hat{p}_{2}^{+}-p_{2}^{+}\right]\;.\end{split}

(347)

We have
p_{2}^{+}=0
,
\hat{p}_{2}=e^{-\omega}p_{2}
from Eq. (
345
), and
p_{2}^{-}/Q^{-}=2p_{2}\cdot Q/Q^{2}
. This gives us

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}-\tau={}&\frac{2p_{2}\cdot Q}{Q^{2}}[1-e^{-\omega}]\;.\end{split}

(348)

Now the condition
\eta\ll\eta_{\mathrm{max}}
that we assume implies that
A_{32}\,e^{\eta}k_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|\ll 1
. Thus in Eq. (
346
), we can replace the factor
1-A_{32}\,e^{\eta}k_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|
in
e^{-\omega}
by just 1. Then

\begin{split}\hat{\tau}-\tau={}&\frac{2p_{2}\cdot Q}{Q^{2}}\,A_{23}\,e^{-\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;.\end{split}

(349)

Since
p_{3}
makes a small angle with the
z
axis, we obtain the approximations

\begin{split}2p_{2}\cdot p_{3}\approx{}&\frac{2p_{2}\cdot Q\,2p_{3}\cdot Q}{Q^{2}}\;,\\
A_{32}\approx{}&\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{3}\cdot Q}\;,\\
A_{23}\approx{}&\frac{Q^{2}}{2p_{2}\cdot Q}\;.\end{split}

(350)

With these approximations, we have

\begin{split}\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)\approx{}&\nu e^{-\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;.\end{split}

(351)

With the same approximations, we obtain for the change in thrust produced by an emission from parton 2 with the dipole partner being parton 3,

\begin{split}\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)\approx{}&\nu e^{\eta}\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;.\end{split}

(352)

Again, this is for
|\eta|\gg 0
. For the soft emission region near
\eta=0
, there is the possibility that
p_{4}
is in the opposite thrust hemisphere from the parton that emitted it, so that the thrust calculation changes.

These relations are significant because the second emission is accompanied by a measurement function
1-\exp(-\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau))
. The measurement function is approximately 1 for
1\ll\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)
but approximately zero for
\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)\ll 1
. Thus we effectively integrate over the range

\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)>1\;.

(353)

The boundary of this integration region is indicated in Fig.
15
as straight lines with the labels
\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)=1
.

There is one more restriction on the integration range for the second splitting. We are analyzing a
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordered shower, so

k_{\mathrm{T}}<\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}\;.

(354)

The line
k_{\mathrm{T}}=\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
is indicated in Fig.
15
.

To analyze Eq. (
354
), we will need to know the value
k_{\mathrm{T},\star}
of
k_{\mathrm{T}}
at the point labelled with a star in Fig.
15
. We first note that the line for
\eta>0
labelled collinear in Fig.
15
is given by
\eta=\eta_{\mathrm{max}}
in Eq. (
342
),
e^{\eta}k_{\mathrm{T}}/|Q|=1/A_{32}
. We can use Eqs. (
350
) and (
338
) for
A_{32}
, giving

e^{\eta}\,\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\approx e^{\tilde{\eta}}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{collinear}\;.

(355)

Then using Eq. (
336
) to eliminate
\tilde{\eta}
and Eq. (
351
) to eliminate
\eta
we have

\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\approx\frac{\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}{\nu\tau}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{collinear}\;.

(356)

The point labelled with a star in Fig.
15
is the intersection of the collinear line and the line
\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)=1
. Thus,

\frac{k_{\mathrm{T},\star}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\approx\frac{1}{\nu\tau}\,\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\;.

(357)

Since in the dominant integration region
\nu\tau<1
, we conclude that
k_{\mathrm{T},\star}>\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
. Thus the line
k_{\mathrm{T}}=\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
lies below the point
(\eta_{\star},k_{\mathrm{T},\star})
in Fig.
15
. This implies that the effective integration region for the second splitting is the region shaded in yellow in Fig.
15
. Inside this region, the integrand is approximately 1.

Now consider the case in which the first splitting is virtual. The corresponding contribution comes from the term
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[}{\mathcal{S}^{[1,0]}(\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\phi})\,e^{-\nu\tau}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}]_{\mathbb{P}}}
in the last line of Eq. (
325
). We integrate over the splitting variables for the first splitting, including the measurement function
e^{-\nu\tau}
, but we start the second splitting from the
q\bar{q}
state with just partons with momenta
\tilde{p}_{1}
and
\tilde{p}_{2}
, but with the
k_{\mathrm{T}}
ordering requirement
k_{\mathrm{T}}<\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
. Now the limits on
\eta
in Fig.
15
, indicated by the lines labelled collinear, are expanded to the dotted lines in the figure. However, the effective integration region for the second splitting is the region shaded in yellow in Fig.
15
. When we subtract the virtual contribution from the real contribution, we get zero within the approximations that we have used.

In Eq. (
357
), we have equality,
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}=k_{\mathrm{T},\star}
, when the value of
\tau
for the first splitting is given by
\nu\tau=1
. The value of
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
in the first splitting can be less than
k_{\mathrm{T},\star}
, but if
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
is too small then the integration region in Fig.
15
disappears. From Eq. (
351
) at
\eta=0
,
\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)=1
and
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}=k_{\mathrm{T}}
, we see that this limits
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
to

\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{|Q|}>\frac{1}{\nu}\;.

(358)

Our analysis above has assumed that the first emission is at large rapidity,
\tilde{\eta}\gg 0
. What happens when
\tilde{\eta}\approx 0
? The approximations that we have used are not adequate in this situation, so it might seem that there is nothing that we can say. However, we can examine what happens when
\tilde{\eta}
is large enough that the approximations are still valid, but
\tilde{\eta}
becomes smaller and smaller. Start with Eq. (
355
) for the collinear line in Fig.
15
and use Eq. (
336
) to eliminate
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
and Eq. (
351
) to eliminate
k_{\mathrm{T}}
, giving

e^{2\eta}\approx\frac{\nu\tau}{\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}\,e^{2\tilde{\eta}}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{collinear}\;.

(359)

The point labelled with a star in Fig.
15
is the intersection of the collinear line and the line
\nu(\hat{\tau}-\tau)=1
. Thus,

e^{2\eta_{\star}}\approx\nu\tau\,e^{2\tilde{\eta}}\;.

(360)

In the effective integration range for the first splitting, we have
\nu\tau<1
. Thus

\eta_{\star}<\tilde{\eta}\;.

(361)

This tells us that when the rapidity of the first splitting becomes small,
\tilde{\eta}\to 0
, we have
\eta_{\star}\to 0
. In this limit, the real-virtual cancellation in this region deteriorates, but this deterioration does not matter because the allowed integration region for the second splitting in Fig.
15
shrinks to zero.

The cancellation will fail on a certain surface in the integration region. On this surface, the splitting variables for the second emission are given by

(k_{\mathrm{T}},\eta)\approx(k_{\mathrm{T},\star},\eta_{\star})\;.

(362)

In this region, the second emission is collinear rather than both soft and collinear, so that the emission probability does not match the constant that appears in the region in which the second emission is both soft and collinear. However in the virtual subtraction the second emission is both soft and collinear so that the emission probability is this constant. Thus the emission probabilities do not match between the real emission and the subtraction.

The surface of non-matching probabilities is specified as follows. If
k_{\mathrm{T}}=k_{\mathrm{T},\star}
, then the line
k_{\mathrm{T}}=\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}
in Fig.
15
must pass through
(k_{\mathrm{T},\star},\eta_{\star})
, so that
\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}=k_{\mathrm{T},\star}
. Then Eq. (
357
) implies that the value of
\tau
for the first emission is given by
\nu\tau=1
. Then Eq. (
336
) gives

\tilde{\eta}\approx\log(\nu)+\log\!\left(\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{Q}\right)\;.

(363)

The transverse momentum for the first emission varies in the range

-\log(\nu)\ll\log\!\left(\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{Q}\right)\ll-\frac{1}{2}\log(\nu)\;.

(364)

Here the lower limit is from Eq. (
358
) and the upper limit is from Eqs. (
330
), (
336
), and (
337
). For the second emission,
(k_{\mathrm{T}},\eta)\approx(k_{\mathrm{T},\star},\eta_{\star})
:

\begin{split}\eta\approx{}&\tilde{\eta}\;,\\
\log\!\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{T}}}{Q}\right)\approx{}&\log\!\left(\frac{\tilde{k}_{\mathrm{T}}}{Q}\right)\;.\end{split}

(365)

Thus the integration region inside which cancellation fails is one dimensional, so we are left with a contribution to
\mathcal{I}^{[2]}_{2}
proportional to
\log^{1}(\nu)
.
